\overline{D}^{(n)}\coloneqq D_{1}^{(n)}-\sqrt{|J_{n}|}\cdot\gamma
and note that

\overline{D}^{(n)}=|J_{n}|^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{j\in J_{n}}W_{j},

where
W_{j}
is given by
W_{j,t}\coloneqq\int_{0}^{t}G_{j,s}(\bm{\lambda}_{j,s}-\lambda_{j,s})\mathrm{d}s-\gamma_{t}
for each
j\in J_{n}
. By assumption, the variables
\{W_{j}\colon j\in J_{n}\}
are i.i.d. with the same distribution as the process
W
given by
W_{t}\coloneqq\int_{0}^{t}G_{s}(\bm{\lambda}_{s}-\lambda_{s})\mathrm{d}s-\gamma_{t}
.
For each
\theta\in\Theta
, let
\Gamma^{\theta}
be a Gaussian process with mean zero and covariance function
(s,t)\mapsto\cov(W_{s}^{\theta},W_{t}^{\theta})
, which is well-defined by computations shown below.

We will show that
\overline{D}^{(n),\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}\Gamma^{\theta}
in
C[0,1]
by applying Lemma
3.B.12
, which is an example of Prokhorov’s method of ”tightness + identification of limit”.
We first prove that for any given
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
0\leq t_{1}<t_{2}<\cdots<t_{k}\leq 1
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}^{(n)}\coloneqq(\overline{D}_{t_{1}}^{(n)},\overline{D}_{t_{2}}^{(n)},\ldots,\overline{D}_{t_{k}}^{(n)})\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}(\Gamma_{t_{1}}^{\theta},\Gamma_{t_{2}}^{\theta},\ldots,\Gamma_{t_{k}}^{\theta}).

To this end we will apply the uniform CLT of
Lundborg et al. (2022b, Proposition 19)

to the sequence of random vectors
\mathbf{D}^{(n)}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, i.e., the sequence of normalized sums of i.i.d. copies of
\mathbf{W}\coloneqq(W_{t_{1}},\ldots,W_{t_{k}})
.
The process
(W_{t})
is mean zero and hence
\mathbf{W}
is also mean zero. For any
t\in[0,1]
we observe that

\displaystyle\var(W_{t})=\var(W_{t}+\gamma_{t})\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{t}|G_{s}|\cdot|\bm{\lambda}_{s}-\lambda_{s}|\mathrm{d}s\right)^{2}\right]\leq 2C^{2}(C^{\prime})^{2}.

Therefore the trace of
\var(\mathbf{W})
is uniformly bounded, which is implies the trace condition in Proposition 19 of
Lundborg et al. (2022b)
.
From Hölder’s inequality and Minkowski’s inequality, we note that for any
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}

\|\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{b}\|_{2}^{3}\leq k^{3/2}\|\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{b}\|_{3}^{3}\leq k^{3/2}(\|\mathbf{a}\|_{3}+\|\mathbf{b}\|_{3})^{3}\leq 8k^{3/2}(\|\mathbf{a}\|_{3}^{3}+\|\mathbf{b}\|_{3}^{3}).

Combining the above with Assumption
3.4.1
and Lemma
3.A.2
yields that

\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{W}\|_{2}^{3}]\leq C_{k}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{1}|G_{s}|\cdot|\bm{\lambda}_{s}-\lambda_{s}|\mathrm{d}s\right)^{3}\right]+C_{k}\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|\gamma_{t}|^{3}\leq 16C_{k}C^{3}(C^{\prime})^{3},

where
C_{k}=8k^{5/2}
. Hence Proposition 19 of
Lundborg et al. (2022b)
lets us conclude that
\mathbf{D}^{(n)}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}\mathcal{N}(0,\var(\mathbf{W}))
. By definition of
\Gamma^{\theta}
, this is equivalent to

\mathbf{D}^{(n)}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}(\Gamma_{t_{1}}^{\theta},\Gamma_{t_{2}}^{\theta},\ldots,\Gamma_{t_{k}}^{\theta})
.

We now argue that
(\overline{D}^{(n)})
and
(\Gamma^{\theta})
are stochastically equicontinuous uniformly over
\Theta
. From the definition of
\Gamma^{\theta}
and by Assumption
3.4.1
, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(\Gamma_{t}^{\theta}-\Gamma_{s}^{\theta})^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[(W_{t}-W_{s})^{2}]\leq(2CC^{\prime}(t-s))^{2}.

(3.A.4)

Hence
\frac{1}{t-s}(\Gamma_{t}^{\theta}-\Gamma_{s}^{\theta})
is Gaussian with a variance bounded over
\Theta
and
0\leq s<t\leq 1
. In particular, it is sub-Gaussian with a uniform variance factor over
\Theta
and
0\leq s<t\leq 1
.
Since
W
is uniformly bounded over
\Theta
, an application of Hoeffding’s Lemma yields that
A_{j}^{t,s}\coloneqq\frac{1}{t-s}(W_{j,t}-W_{j,s})
is also sub-Gaussian with a variance factor
\nu
that is uniform over
\Theta
,
0\leq s<t\leq 1
, and
j\in J_{n}
. Letting
A_{\bullet}^{s,t}=\frac{1}{t-s}(\overline{D}_{t}^{(n)}-\overline{D}_{s}^{(n)})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}e^{xA_{\bullet}^{s,t}}=\prod_{j\in J_{n}}\mathbb{E}[e^{x|J_{n}|^{-1/2}A_{j}^{s,t}}]\leq\prod_{j\in J_{n}}e^{\frac{x^{2}\nu}{2|J_{n}|}}=e^{x^{2}\nu/2}.

Hence
A_{\bullet}^{s,t}
is also sub-Gaussian with a variance factor uniformly over
\Theta
and
0\leq s<t\leq 1
.

From the uniform chaining lemma, Corollary
3.B.15
, we now conclude that both
(\Gamma^{\theta})
and
(\overline{D}^{(n)})
are stochastically equicontinuous uniformly over
\Theta
. By Proposition
3.B.16
, this means that the collection
(\overline{D}^{(n),\theta})
is sequentially tight
and that
(\Gamma^{\theta})
, which is constant in
n
, is uniformly tight.

Now we have shown convergence of the finite-dimensional marginals and appropriate tightness conditions, so Lemma
3.B.12
lets us conclude that
\overline{D}^{(n)}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}\Gamma^{\theta}
weakly in
C[0,1]
.
∎

Before moving on to the term
D_{2}^{(n)}
, we first note that Lemma
3.A.8
implies that stochastic boundedness, as we will use this result in the proof of Theorem
3.4.5
.

Lemma 3.A.9
.

Suppose that Assumptions
3.4.1
and
3.4.2
hold.
Then
\overline{D}^{(n)}\coloneqq D_{1}^{(n)}-\sqrt{|J_{n}|}\cdot\gamma
is stochastically bounded uniformly over
\Theta
, i.e.,
for every
\varepsilon>0
there exists
K>0
such that

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}\left(\|\overline{D}^{(n),\theta}\|_{\infty}>K\right)<\varepsilon.

Proof.

We have established in the proof of Lemma
3.A.8
, under the same conditions, that
\overline{D}^{(n),\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}\Gamma^{\theta}
weakly in
C[0,1]
.
By the uniform continuous mapping theorem formulated in Proposition
3.B.5
, it follows that
\|\overline{D}^{(n),\theta}\|_{\infty}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}\|\Gamma^{\theta}\|_{\infty}
.
From
Bengs and Holzmann (2019)
Theorem 4.1 we then obtain that

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}(\|\overline{D}^{(n),\theta}\|_{\infty}>K)\leq\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}(\|\Gamma^{\theta}\|_{\infty}>K)\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}\|\Gamma^{\theta}\|_{\infty}}{K}.

Hence it suffices to argue that
\mathbb{E}\|\Gamma^{\theta}\|_{\infty}
is uniformly bounded over
\Theta
.
To this end, we note that Equation (
3.A.4
)
shows that square means of the increments of
\Gamma^{\theta}

are smaller that those of a standard Brownian motion scaled by
2CC^{\prime}
. Then the Sudakov–Fernique comparison inequality
(
Adler and Taylor, 2007
, Theorem 2.2.3)
allows us to leverage this relationship to the expected uniform norms, i.e.,
\mathbb{E}\|\Gamma^{\theta}\|_{\infty}\leq 2CC^{\prime}\mathbb{E}(\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|B_{t}|)
. It can be verified that
\mathbb{E}(\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|B_{t}|)
is finite, and in fact, equal to
\sqrt{\pi/2}
as shown in
saz (2019)
.
∎

Lemma 3.A.10
.

Suppose that Assumptions
3.4.1
and
3.4.2

hold, and that
G_{t}=X_{t}-\Pi_{t}
is the additive residual process.
Then
D_{2}^{(n)}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$P/\Theta$}}0
in
D[0,1]
as
n\to\infty
.

Proof.

Note first that the terms in
D_{2}^{(n)}
are i.i.d. conditionally on
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{1}^{n,c}
, with the same distribution as the process
\xi
given by

\displaystyle\xi_{t}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|J_{n}|}}\int_{0}^{t}(\widehat{G}_{s}^{(n)}-G_{s})(\bm{\lambda}_{s}-\lambda_{s})\mathrm{d}s.

Since
\bm{\lambda}_{t}
is independent of
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{1}^{n,c}
, we have from the innovation theorem that

\mathbb{E}(\bm{\lambda}_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-}\vee\tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{1}^{n,c})=\mathbb{E}(\bm{\lambda}_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-})=\lambda_{t}.

For the additive residual process we also note that

G_{t}-\widehat{G}_{t}^{(n)}=\widehat{\Pi}_{t}^{(n)}-\Pi_{t}
is
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-predictable conditionally on
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{1}^{n,c}
. It now follows that

\displaystyle\sqrt{|J_{n}|}\cdot\mathbb{E}[\xi_{t}\mid\tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{1}^{n,c}]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}[(\widehat{G}_{s}^{(n)}-G_{s})(\bm{\lambda}_{s}-\lambda_{s})\mid\tilde{\mathcal{G}}^{n,c}]\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}[(\widehat{G}_{s}^{(n)}-G_{s})(\mathbb{E}[\bm{\lambda}_{s}\mid\mathcal{F}_{s-}\vee\tilde{\mathcal{G}}^{n,c}]-\lambda_{s})\mid\tilde{\mathcal{G}}^{n,c}]\mathrm{d}s=0.

We can therefore conclude that
D_{2}^{(n)}
is mean zero conditionally on
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}^{n,c}
.
Using that the terms of
D_{2}^{(n)}
are i.i.d. conditionally on
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}^{n,c}
once more, we now obtain that

\displaystyle\var(D_{2,t}^{(n)}\mid\tilde{\mathcal{G}}^{n,c})=|J_{n}|\cdot\var(\xi_{t}\mid\tilde{\mathcal{G}}^{n,c})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{t}(\widehat{G}_{s}^{(n)}-G_{s})(\bm{\lambda}_{s}-\lambda_{s})\mathrm{d}s\right)^{2}\Big|\tilde{\mathcal{G}}^{n,c}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 4C^{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{1}(\widehat{G}_{s}^{(n)}-G_{s})^{2}\mathrm{d}s\Big|\tilde{\mathcal{G}}^{n,c}\right).

Taking expectation of the above we have

\var(D_{2,t}^{(n)})=\mathbb{E}(\var(D_{2,t}^{(n)}\mid\tilde{\mathcal{G}}^{n,c}))\leq 4C^{2}g(n)^{2}
.
By Chebyshev’s inequality we get for all
\epsilon>0

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(|D_{2,t}^{(n)}|>\epsilon\right)\leq\frac{4C^{2}g(n)^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}},

and by Assumption
3.4.2
we conclude that
D_{2,t}^{(n)}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$P/\Theta$}}0
for each
t\in[0,1]
.

We now apply the same chaining argument used in the proofs of Lemma
3.A.7
and Lemma
3.A.8
. From Assumption
3.4.1
, we have for
0\leq s<t\leq 1
that
|\xi_{t}-\xi_{s}|\leq 4\sqrt{|J_{n}|}CC^{\prime}(t-s)
. Hence the conditional Hoeffding’s lemma (Lemma
3.A.5
) yields that

A_{j}^{s,t}=\frac{1}{t-s}\int_{s}^{t}(\widehat{G}_{j,s}^{(n)}-G_{j,s})(\bm{\lambda}_{j,s}-\lambda_{j,s})\mathrm{d}s

is sub-Gaussian conditionally on
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{1}^{n,c}
with a variance factor
\nu
that is uniform over
\Theta
and
s<t
(cf. the proof of Lemma
3.A.6
). Letting
A_{\bullet}^{s,t}=\frac{1}{t-s}(D_{2,t}^{(n)}-D_{2,s}^{(n)})
, we have for any
x\in\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(e^{xA_{\bullet}^{s,t}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[e^{xA_{\bullet}^{s,t}}\mid\tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{1}^{n,c}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigg(\prod_{j\in J_{n}}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{x|J_{n}|^{-1/2}A_{j}^{s,t}}\mid\tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{1}^{n,c}\right]\bigg)\leq\prod_{j\in J_{n}}e^{\frac{x^{2}\nu}{2|J_{n}|}}=e^{x^{2}\nu/2},

so
A_{\bullet}^{s,t}
is also sub-Gaussian uniformly over
s<t
and

\Theta
. In terms of
D_{2}^{(n)}
, this means that we can apply the uniform chaining lemma, Corollary
3.B.15
, and conclude that it is stochastically equicontinuous uniformly over
\Theta
.

Since
D_{2,t}^{(n)}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$P/\Theta$}}0
for each
t\in[0,1]
and
(D_{2}^{(n)})
is stochastically equicontinuous uniformly over
\Theta
, Lemma
3.B.13
now lets us conclude that
\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|D_{2,t}^{(n)}|\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$P/\Theta$}}0
and we are done.
∎

3.A.7
Proof of Theorem
3.4.5

Before proving Theorem
3.4.5
, we first prove that the collection of Gaussian martingales from Proposition
3.4.2
is tight in
C[0,1]
(see Definition
7
).

Lemma 3.A.11
.

Let
(U^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
be the collection of Gaussian martingales from Proposition
3.4.2
, i.e.,
U^{\theta}
is a mean zero continuous Gaussian martingale with variance function
\mathcal{V}^{\theta}
.
Under Assumption
3.4.1
,
(U^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
is uniformly tight in
C[0,1]
.

Proof.

We will use Theorem 7.3 in
Billingsley (2013)
, which characterizes tightness of measures in
C[0,1]
. The first condition of the theorem is trivially satisfied for
(U^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
since
\mathbb{P}(U_{0}^{\theta}=0)=1
for all
\theta\in\Theta
.

By Proposition
3.C.1
,
U^{\theta}
has a distributional representation as a time-transformed Brownian motion such that
(U_{t}^{\theta})_{t\in[0,1]}\overset{\mathcal{D}}{=}(B_{\mathcal{V}^{\theta}(t)})_{t\in[0,1]}
, where
B
is a Brownian motion.
Recall that Brownian motion is
\alpha
-Hölder continuous for
\alpha\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
, which means
that

K(\alpha)=\sup_{s\neq t}\frac{|B_{t}-B_{s}|}{|t-s|^{\alpha}}<\infty.

Note also that the collection of variance functions is uniformly Lipschitz by Lemma
3.A.2
with uniform Lipschitz constant
C_{0}
, say. It follows that for every
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{|t-s|<\delta}|U_{t}^{\theta}-U_{s}^{\theta}|>\epsilon\Big)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{|t-s|<\delta}|B_{\mathcal{V}^{\theta}(t)}-B_{\mathcal{V}^{\theta}(s)}|>\epsilon\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}\Big(K(\alpha)\sup_{|t-s|<\delta}|\mathcal{V}^{\theta}(t)-\mathcal{V}^{\theta}(s)|^{\alpha}>\epsilon\Big)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}\mathbb{P}\Big(K(\alpha)C_{0}^{\alpha}\delta^{\alpha}>\epsilon\Big)=0.

This establishes the second condition of Theorem 7.3 in
Billingsley (2013)
, and we thus conclude that
(U^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
is uniformly tight in
C[0,1]
.
∎

We now return to the proof of Theorem
3.4.5
.

For part i), we first note that under
H_{0}
we can take
\bm{\lambda}_{t}=\lambda_{t}
,
which implies that both
D_{1}^{(n)}
and
D_{2}^{(n)}
equal the zero-process.

Combining Propositions
3.4.2
and
3.4.3
with the uniform version of Slutsky’s theorem formulated in Lemma
3.B.4
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\sqrt{|J_{n}|}\widehat{\gamma}^{(n)}=\underbrace{U^{(n)}}_{\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta_{0}$}}U^{\theta}}+\underbrace{R_{1}^{(n)}+R_{2}^{(n)}+R_{3}^{(n)}}_{\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$P/\Theta$}}0}+\underbrace{D_{1}^{(n)}+D_{2}^{(n)}}_{=0\,\text{under }H_{0}}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$P/\Theta_{0}$}}U^{\theta},

in
D[0,1]
as
n\to\infty
, where
U^{\theta}
is the Gaussian martingale from Proposition
3.4.2
.

For part ii) we can, in addition to Propositions
3.4.2
and
3.4.3
, apply Proposition
3.4.4
and Lemma
3.A.9
. Using the triangle inequality on the decomposition (
3.4.17
) yields that

\displaystyle\sqrt{|J_{n}|}\cdot\|\widehat{\gamma}^{(n)}-\gamma\|_{\infty}\leq

\displaystyle\|U^{(n)}\|_{\infty}+\|D_{1}^{(n)}-\sqrt{|J_{n}|}\gamma\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle+\|R_{1}^{(n)}\|_{\infty}+\|R_{2}^{(n)}\|_{\infty}+\|R_{3}^{(n)}\|_{\infty}+\|D_{2}^{(n)}\|_{\infty}.

All the terms in the second line converge in probability to zero uniformly over
\Theta
. Combined with the convergences established in Proposition
3.4.2
and Lemma
3.A.8
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}\left(\sqrt{|J_{n}|}\cdot\|\widehat{\gamma}^{(n),\theta}-\gamma^{\theta}\|_{\infty}>K\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}\left(\|U^{\theta}\|_{\infty}>K/6\right)+\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}\left(\|\Gamma^{\theta}\|_{\infty}>K/6\right),

(3.A.5)

where
\Gamma^{\theta}
is the limiting Gaussian process from (the proof of) Lemma
3.A.8
.
The last term in (
3.A.5
) can be made arbitrarily small for
K
sufficiently large by Lemma
3.A.9
.
Lemma
3.A.11
states that the family
(U^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
is tight in
C[0,1]
, and hence the family
(\|U^{\theta}\|_{\infty})_{\theta\in\Theta}
is tight in
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. This implies that the first term in (
3.A.5
) can also be made arbitrarily small for
K
sufficiently large.
This establishes (
3.4.26
) and we are done.
∎

3.A.8
Proof of Proposition
3.4.6

Consider the decomposition of the variance function estimator given by

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{n}(t)

\displaystyle=A^{(n)}_{t}+B^{(n)}_{t}+2C^{(n)}_{t}

where

\displaystyle A^{(n)}_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|J_{n}|}\sum_{j\in J_{n}}\int_{0}^{t}G_{j,s}^{2}\mathrm{d}N_{j,s},

\displaystyle B^{(n)}_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|J_{n}|}\sum_{j\in J_{n}}\int_{0}^{t}\left(G_{j,s}-\widehat{G}_{j,s}^{(n)}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}N_{j,s},

\displaystyle C^{(n)}_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|J_{n}|}\sum_{j\in J_{n}}\int_{0}^{t}G_{j,s}\left(G_{j,s}-\widehat{G}_{j,s}^{(n)}\right)\mathrm{d}N_{j,s}.

We first consider the asymptotic limit of
A^{(n)}
, which is the empirical mean of
|J_{n}|
i.i.d. samples of the process
\int_{0}^{t}G_{s}^{2}\mathrm{d}N_{s}
.
Under Assumption
3.4.1
, we can apply the first part of Lemma
3.A.1
which states
\bm{M}_{t}^{2}-\bm{\Lambda}_{t}
is a martingale. We use this fact to note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(N_{1}^{2})=\mathbb{E}((\bm{M}_{1}+\bm{\Lambda}_{1})^{2})\leq 2\left(\mathbb{E}(\bm{M}_{1}^{2})+\mathbb{E}(\bm{\Lambda}_{1}^{2})\right)=4\,\mathbb{E}\left(\Big(\int_{0}^{1}\bm{\lambda}_{s}\mathrm{d}s\Big)^{2}\right)\leq 4C^{2}.

Now, another use of Assumption
3.4.1
shows that
\int_{0}^{t}G_{s}^{2}\mathrm{d}N_{s}
has a second moment bounded by
4(CC^{\prime})^{2}
.
Thus we can apply the uniform law of large numbers
(
Shah and Peters, 2020
, Lemma 19)
to conclude for each
t\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle A_{t}^{(n)}=\frac{1}{|J_{n}|}\sum_{j\in J_{n}}\int_{0}^{t}G_{j,s}^{2}\mathrm{d}N_{j,s}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$P/\Theta$}}\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{t}G_{s}^{2}\mathrm{d}N_{s}\right)=\mathcal{V}(t).

Note also that
A^{(n)}
and
\mathcal{V}
are non-decreasing and that the collection
(\mathcal{V}^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
is uniformly equicontinuous by Lemma
3.A.2
. These are exactly the conditions for Lemma
3.B.11
, so we can automatically conclude that
\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|A_{t}^{(n)}-\mathcal{V}(t)|\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$P/\Theta$}}0
.

Next we show that the remainder terms

B^{(n)}
and
C^{(n)}
converge uniformly
to zero in expectation. Similarly to the proof of Lemma
3.A.4
, we have under Assumptions
3.4.1
and
3.4.2
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\sup_{0\leq t\leq 1}B_{t}^{(n)}\right)=\mathbb{E}(B_{1}^{(n)})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{1}{|J_{n}|}\sum_{j\in J_{n}}\int_{0}^{1}\left(G_{j,s}-\widehat{G}_{j,s}^{(n)}\right)^{2}\bm{\lambda}_{j,s}\mathrm{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{1}{|J_{n}|}\sum_{j\in J_{n}}\int_{0}^{1}\left(G_{j,s}-\widehat{G}_{j,s}^{(n)}\right)^{2}\bm{\lambda}_{j,s}\mathrm{d}s\mid\tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{1}^{c}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{1}\left(G_{s}-\widehat{G}_{s}^{(n)}\right)^{2}\bm{\lambda}_{s}\mathrm{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot g(n)^{2}\longrightarrow 0

as
n\to\infty
uniformly over
\Theta
.
Lastly, we see that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left|\sup_{0\leq t\leq 1}C_{t}^{(n)}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left(\sup_{0\leq t\leq 1}|C_{t}^{(n)}|\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{1}{|J_{n}|}\sum_{j\in J_{n}}\sup_{0\leq t\leq 1}\int_{0}^{t}|G_{j,s}||G_{j,s}-\widehat{G}_{j,s}^{(n)}|\bm{\lambda}_{j,s}\mathrm{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{1}{|J_{n}|}\sum_{j\in J_{n}}\int_{0}^{1}|G_{j,s}||G_{j,s}-\widehat{G}_{j,s}^{(n)}|\bm{\lambda}_{j,s}\mathrm{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{1}{|J_{n}|}\sum_{j\in J_{n}}\int_{0}^{1}|G_{j,s}||G_{j,s}-\widehat{G}_{j,s}^{(n)}|\bm{\lambda}_{j,s}\mathrm{d}s\mid\tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{1}^{c}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{1}|G_{s}||G_{s}-\widehat{G}_{s}^{(n)}|\bm{\lambda}_{s}\mathrm{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\leq CC^{\prime}\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{1}|G_{s}-\widehat{G}_{s}^{(n)}|\mathrm{d}s\right)

\displaystyle\leq CC^{\prime}\cdot g(n)\longrightarrow 0

as
n\to\infty
uniformly over
\Theta
by Assumption
3.4.2
.
Combining the convergences established for
A^{(n)}
,
B^{(n)}
, and
C^{(n)}
, we get by a generalized Slutsky (Lemma
3.B.9
) that

\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{n}(t)-\mathcal{V}(t)|\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$P/\Theta$}}0.

∎

3.A.9
Proof of Corollary
3.4.7

Under Assumptions
3.4.1
and
3.4.2
we know by Theorem
3.4.5
and Proposition
3.4.6
that

\displaystyle\sqrt{|J_{n}|}\widehat{\gamma}^{(n),\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta_{0}$}}U^{\theta}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{n}^{\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$P/\Theta_{0}$}}\mathcal{V}^{\theta}

(3.A.6)

in
D[0,1]
as
n\to\infty
. If we were to show pointwise convergence of the test statistic, this would now be a straightforward consequence of the continuous mapping theorem.
However, to show uniform convergence, we will need an additional tightness argument.

Let
(\theta_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\Theta_{0}
be an arbitrary sequence. Proposition
3.B.2
then states that it suffices to show that there exists a subsequence
(\theta_{k(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq(\theta_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, with
k\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
strictly increasing, such that

\lim_{n\to\infty}d_{BL}\big(\widehat{D}_{k(n)}^{\theta_{k(n)}},\mathcal{J}(U^{\theta_{k(n)}},\mathcal{V}^{\theta_{k(n)}})\big)=0.

(3.A.7)

Here
d_{BL}
denotes the bounded Lipschitz metric defined in Section
3
. By Lemma
3.A.11
, the collection
(U^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
is tight in
C[0,1]
under Assumption
3.4.1
. Therefore, Prokhorov’s theorem
(
Kallenberg, 2021
, Theorem 23.2)
asserts that there exists a subsequence
(\theta_{a(n)})\subset(\theta_{n})
, and a
C[0,1]
-valued random variable
\tilde{U}
such that
U^{\theta_{a(n)}}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}}\tilde{U}
in
C[0,1]
.

Likewise, Lemma
3.A.2
states that the collection
(V^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
is uniformly bounded and uniformly equicontinuous under Assumption
3.4.1
. Thus the Arzelà-Ascoli theorem yields that there exists a further subsequence
(\theta_{b(n)})\subset(\theta_{a(n)})
and a function
\tilde{\mathcal{V}}\in C[0,1]
such that
\|\mathcal{V}^{\theta_{b(n)}}-\tilde{\mathcal{V}}\|_{\infty}\to 0
.

Combining the convergences of
U^{\theta_{b(n)}}
and
\mathcal{V}^{\theta_{b(n)}}
with those in Equation (
3.A.6
), it follows from the triangle inequality of the metric
d_{BL}
that also

\displaystyle\sqrt{|J_{b(n)}|}\widehat{\gamma}^{(b(n)),{\theta_{b(n)}}}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}}\tilde{U}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{b(n)}^{\theta_{b(n)}}\xrightarrow{P}\tilde{\mathcal{V}},

in
D[0,1]
as
n\to\infty
. Now we may use that convergence in Skorokhod topology is equivalent to convergence in uniform topology whenever the limit variable continuous, see e.g.
Kallenberg (2021, Theorem 23.9)
. Hence the convergences above also hold in
(D[0,1],\|\cdot\|_{\infty})
.

Since
\mathcal{V}
is deterministic, this implies the joint convergences

\displaystyle(U^{\theta_{b(n)}},\mathcal{V}^{\theta_{b(n)}})\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}}(\tilde{U},\tilde{\mathcal{V}})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Big(\sqrt{|J_{b(n)}|}\widehat{\gamma}^{(b(n)),{\theta_{b(n)}}},\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{b(n)}^{\theta_{b(n)}}\Big)\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}}(\tilde{U},\tilde{\mathcal{V}})

in the product space
D[0,1]\times D[0,1]
endowed with the uniform topology.
Since
(\tilde{U},\tilde{\mathcal{V}})\in C[0,1]\times\overline{\{\mathcal{V}^{\theta}\colon\theta\in\Theta_{0}\}}
takes values in the continuity set of
\mathcal{J}
by assumption, the classical continuous mapping theorem lets us conclude that

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(U^{\theta_{b(n)}},\mathcal{V}^{\theta_{b(n)}})\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{J}(\tilde{U},\tilde{\mathcal{V}})\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{D}_{b(n)}^{\theta_{b(n)}}=\mathcal{J}\Big(\sqrt{|J_{b(n)}|}\widehat{\gamma}^{(b(n)),{\theta_{b(n)}}},\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{b(n)}^{\theta_{b(n)}}\Big)\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{J}(\tilde{U},\tilde{\mathcal{V}})

as
n\to\infty
. Now another application of the triangle inequality with
\mathcal{J}(\tilde{U},\tilde{\mathcal{V}})
as intermediate value shows that (
3.A.7
) holds with
k(n)=b(n)
, so we are done.
∎

3.A.10
Proof of Theorem
3.5.1

We will apply Corollary
3.4.7
with the functional
\mathcal{J}
given by

\mathcal{J}(f_{1},f_{2})=\mathbbl{1}(f_{2}\neq 0)\frac{\|f_{1}\|_{\infty}}{\sqrt{|f_{2}(1)|}},\qquad f_{1},f_{2}\in D[0,1].

Under Assumption
3.5.1
, it suffices to check continuity of
\mathcal{J}
on the set
\Upsilon
given by

\Upsilon\coloneqq C[0,1]\times\{f\in C[0,1]\mid\delta_{1}\leq|f(1)|\}\supset C[0,1]\times\overline{\{\mathcal{V}^{\theta}\colon\theta\in\Theta_{0}\}}.

To see that
\mathcal{J}
is continuous on
\Upsilon
in the uniform topology, we note that it can be written as a composition of the continuous maps

\displaystyle\Upsilon\longrightarrow[0,\infty)\times[\delta_{1},\infty),

\displaystyle\qquad(f_{1},f_{2})\mapsto(\|f_{1}\|_{\infty},|f_{2}(1)|),

\displaystyle[0,\infty)\times[\delta_{1},\infty)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle\qquad(x_{1},x_{2})\mapsto\frac{x_{1}}{\sqrt{x_{2}}}.

Thus it follows from Corollary
3.4.7
that

\displaystyle\widehat{T}_{n}=\frac{\sqrt{|J_{n}|}\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|\widehat{\gamma}_{t}^{(n)}|}{\sqrt{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{n}(1)}}=\mathcal{J}\left(\sqrt{|J_{n}|}\widehat{\gamma}^{(n)},\;\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{n}\right)\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta_{0}$}}\mathcal{J}(U,\mathcal{V})=\frac{\|U\|_{\infty}}{\mathcal{V}(1)}.

With
(B_{u})
a Brownian motion it follows by Proposition
3.C.1

that

\displaystyle\dfrac{\|U\|_{\infty}}{\sqrt{\mathcal{V}(1)}}\overset{\mathcal{D}}{=}\dfrac{\sup_{0\leq t\leq 1}|B_{\mathcal{V}(t)}|}{\sqrt{\mathcal{V}(1)}}=\dfrac{\sup_{0\leq u\leq\mathcal{V}(1)}|B_{u}|}{\sqrt{\mathcal{V}(1)}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}\sup_{0\leq t\leq 1}|B_{t}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}S,

(3.A.8)

where we have used that
\mathcal{V}
is continuous and that Brownian motion is scale invariant. This establishes the first part of the theorem.

For the second part, we first note that the distribution of
S
is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure, which follows from Equation (
3.5.31
).
Then we can use Theorem 4.1 of
Bengs and Holzmann (2019)
to conclude that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}|\mathbb{P}(\widehat{T}_{n}\leq z_{1-\alpha})-(1-\alpha)|=0.

It follows from the triangle inequality that

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}(\Psi_{n}^{\alpha}=1)=\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}(\widehat{T}_{n}>z_{1-\alpha})\leq\alpha.

∎

3.A.11
Proof of Theorem
3.5.2

Let
0<\alpha<\beta<1
be given.
The second part of Theorem
3.4.5
permits us to choose
K>0
sufficiently large such that

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}\left((\sqrt{|J_{n}|}\|\widehat{\gamma}^{(n),\theta}-\gamma^{\theta}\|_{\infty})>K\right)<1-\beta.

(3.A.9)

We then choose
c>K+z_{1-\alpha}\sqrt{1+C(C^{\prime})^{2}}
such that for all
\theta\in\mathcal{A}_{c,n}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\sqrt{|J_{n}|}\|\gamma^{\theta}\|_{\infty}-z_{1-\alpha}\sqrt{1+\mathcal{V}^{\theta}(1)}\geq c-z_{1-\alpha}\sqrt{1+C(C^{\prime})^{2}}>K,

where we have used Lemma
3.A.2
in the first inequality. The (reverse) triangle inequality now yields that for any
\theta\in\mathcal{A}_{c,n}

\displaystyle(\Psi_{n}^{\theta}=0)=(\widehat{T}_{n}^{\theta}\leq z_{1-\alpha})

\displaystyle=\left(\|\widehat{\gamma}^{(n),\theta}\|_{\infty}\leq\sqrt{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{n}^{\theta}(1)}\frac{z_{1-\alpha}}{\sqrt{|J_{n}|}}\right)

\displaystyle\subseteq\left(\|\gamma^{\theta}\|_{\infty}-\left\|\widehat{\gamma}^{(n),\theta}-\gamma^{\theta}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\sqrt{\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{n}^{\theta}(1)}\frac{z_{1-\alpha}}{\sqrt{|J_{n}|}}\right)

\displaystyle\subseteq E_{1}^{(n),\theta}\cup E_{2}^{(n),\theta},

where

\displaystyle E_{1}^{(n),\theta}

\displaystyle=\left(\sqrt{|J_{n}|}\left\|\widehat{\gamma}^{(n),\theta}-\gamma^{\theta}\right\|_{\infty}>K\right),

\displaystyle E_{2}^{(n),\theta}

\displaystyle=\left(\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{n}^{\theta}(1)>1+\mathcal{V}^{\theta}(1)\right)\subseteq\left(|\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{n}^{\theta}(1)-\mathcal{V}^{\theta}(1)|>1\right).

From Proposition
3.4.6
we know that
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}(E_{2}^{(n),\theta})=0
, so from the choice of
K
we conclude that

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}(\Psi_{n}=0)\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}(E_{1}^{(n),\theta})<1-\beta.

The desired statement follows from substituting
\mathbb{P}(\Psi_{n}=0)=1-\mathbb{P}(\Psi_{n}=1)
into the above equation and simplifying. ∎

3.A.12
Proof of Theorem
3.5.3

Assume that
H_{0}
holds and note that Assumptions
3.4.1
and
3.4.2
are satisfied for every sample split
J_{n}^{k}\cup(J_{n}^{k})^{c}
,
k=1,\ldots,K
.

We consider the decomposition in Equation (
3.4.17
) for each sample split
J_{n}^{k}\cup(J_{n}^{k})^{c}
, and denote the corresponding processes by

U^{k,(n)}
,
R_{1}^{k,(n)}
,
R_{2}^{k,(n)}
,
R_{3}^{k,(n)}
,
D_{1}^{k,(n)}
, and
D_{2}^{k,(n)}
.
For each fold
k\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
, we can then apply the results in Section
3.4
for a single data split:

•

By Proposition
3.4.2
, we have that
U^{k,(n)}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}U
in
D[0,1]
, where
U
is a mean zero continuous Gaussian martingale with variance function
\mathcal{V}
.

•

By Proposition
3.4.3
,

R_{\ell}^{k,(n)}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$P/\Theta$}}0
in
(D[0,1],\|\cdot\|_{\infty})
as
n\to\infty
.

•

Under
H_{0}
, the processes
D_{1}^{k,(n)}
and
D_{2}^{k,(n)}
are equal to the zero process almost surely.

Recall that the folds are assumed to have uniform asymptotic density, which is equivalent to
\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\sqrt{K|J_{n}^{k}|}}\to 1
as
n\to\infty
.
Thus we may also conclude that for each fixed
k
and
\ell
,

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\sqrt{K|J_{n}^{k}|}}U^{k,(n)}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}U\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\sqrt{n}}{K\sqrt{|J_{n}^{k}|}}R_{\ell}^{k,(n)}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$P/\Theta$}}0,

where the convergences hold in the Skorokhod and uniform topology, respectively. Now the key observation is that

\displaystyle U^{1,(n)}\,\perp\!\!\!\perp\,\cdots\,\perp\!\!\!\perp\,U^{K,(n)}.

To see this, note that
U^{k,(n)}
is constructed from
(G_{j},M_{j})_{j\in J^{k}}
only, and by the i.i.d. assumption of the data, the collections
(G_{j},M_{j})_{j\in J^{1}},\ldots,(G_{j},M_{j})_{j\in J_{n}^{K}}
are jointly independent.
We can therefore apply Lemma
3.B.10
iteratively to the sequences

\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\sqrt{K|J_{n}^{1}|}}U^{1,(n)},\ldots,\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\sqrt{K|J_{n}^{K}|}}U^{K,(n)}

to conclude that their sum is uniformly convergent to the sum of
K
independent copies of
U
. Using the convolution property of the Gaussian distribution, it therefore follows that

\displaystyle\check{U}^{K,(n)}\coloneqq\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\sqrt{K|J_{n}^{k}|}}U^{k,(n)}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}U

in
D[0,1]
as
n\to\infty
. By the uniform Slutsky theorem formulated in Lemma
3.B.9
, we can therefore conclude that

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}\check{\gamma}^{K,(n)}=\check{U}^{K,(n)}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\sqrt{n}}{K\sqrt{|J_{n}^{k}|}}\left(R_{1}^{k,(n)}+R_{2}^{k,(n)}+R_{3}^{k,(n)}+D_{1}^{k,(n)}+D_{2}^{k,(n)}\right)\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta_{0}$}}U

in
D[0,1]
as
n\to\infty
. Note that we use
\theta\in\Theta_{0}
to ensure that
D_{1}^{k,(n)}+D_{2}^{k,(n)}
is equal to the zero process almost surely.
Since the limit
(U^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta_{0}}
is tight in
C[0,1]
by Lemma
3.A.11
,
Proposition
3.B.7
lets us conclude that
\sqrt{n}\|\check{\gamma}^{K,(n)}\|_{\infty}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta_{0}$}}\|U\|_{\infty}
.

Consider now the cross-fitted variance estimator at its endpoint

\check{\mathcal{V}}_{K,n}(1)=\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{|J_{n}^{k}|}\sum_{j\in J_{n}^{k}}\int_{0}^{1}\left(\widehat{G}_{j,s}^{k,(n)}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}N_{j,s}.

From Proposition
3.4.6
, we see that
\check{\mathcal{V}}_{K,n}(1)
is an average of
K
variables converging uniformly in probability to
\mathcal{V}(1)
in the uniform topology. Hence
\check{\mathcal{V}}_{K,n}(1)
also converges uniformly in probability to
\mathcal{V}(1)
in the uniform topology. We can then apply Theorem 6.3 of
Bengs and Holzmann (2019)
, which is a uniform version of Slutsky’s theorem, to conclude that

\displaystyle\check{T}_{n}^{K}=\frac{\sqrt{n}\|\check{\gamma}^{K,(n)}\|_{\infty}}{\check{\mathcal{V}}_{K,n}(1)}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta_{0}$}}\frac{\|U\|_{\infty}}{\mathcal{V}(1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}S,

as
n\to\infty
, where last equality in distribution was established in (
3.A.8
).

Following the second part of the proof of Theorem
3.5.1
, we conclude in the X-LCT has uniform asymptotic level. ∎

3.B. Uniform stochastic convergence

In this section, we discuss weak convergence of random variables with values in a metric space uniformly over a parameter set
\Theta
. The uniformity over the parameter set can be used, for example, to establish uniform asymptotic level as well as power under local alternatives.

The content of this section extends the works of
Bengs and Holzmann (2019)
and
Kasy (2019)
, and we especially build upon Appendix B of
Lundborg et al. (2022b)
, in which uniform stochastic convergence is considered in
separable
Banach spaces and Hilbert spaces.
The space space
(D[0,1],\|\cdot\|_{\infty})
of càdlàg functions endowed with the uniform norm is a Banach space, but it is unfortunately not separable. Therefore we extend the notion of uniform stochastic convergence to random variables in metric spaces, with the condition that the limit is supported on a separable set. This allows to consider uniform weak convergence in two important special cases:
i) convergence in
(D[0,1],\|\cdot\|_{\infty})
towards variables in
(C[0,1],\|\cdot\|_{\infty})
, and ii) convergence in
D[0,1]
endowed with the Skorokhod metric.

The Skorokhod space
D[0,1]
is, if not specified otherwise, equipped with the complete Skorokhod metric
d^{\circ}
, which makes it a Polish space, i.e., a complete and separable metric space. See for example Section 12 in
Billingsley (2013)
for a discussion of the Skorokhod space and in particular Equation (12.16) for a definition of
d^{\circ}
.

3.B.1
Uniform stochastic convergence in metric spaces

Throughout this section we consider a background probability space
(\Omega,\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
and let
(\mathbb{D},d_{\mathbb{D}})
denote a generic metric space.
We define
BL_{1}(\mathbb{D})
as the set of real-valued functions on
\mathbb{D}
with Lipschitz norm bounded by
1
, that is, functions
f\colon\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{R}
with

\|f\|_{\infty}\leq 1
and
|f(x)-f(y)|\leq d_{\mathbb{D}}(x,y)
for every
x,y\in\mathbb{D}
.
Let
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{D})
denote the set of Borel probability measures on
\mathbb{D}
. We then define the
bounded Lipschitz metric
on
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{D})
by

\displaystyle d_{BL}(\mu,\nu)\coloneqq\sup_{f\in BL_{1}(\mathbb{D})}\Big|\int f\mathrm{d}\mu-\int f\mathrm{d}\nu\Big|,\qquad\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{D}).

For any pair
(X,Y)
of
\mathbb{D}
-valued random variables we use the shorthand notation

\displaystyle d_{BL}(X,Y)=d_{BL}(X(\mathbb{P}),Y(\mathbb{P}))=\sup_{f\in BL_{1}(\mathbb{D})}\lvert\mathbb{E}(f(X)-f(Y))\rvert.

If the underlying metric space is ambiguous for
d_{BL}
, we will specify that it is the bounded Lipschitz metric on
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{D})
by writing
d_{BL(\mathbb{D})}
.
Our interest in the bounded Lipschitz metric is due to its characterization of weak convergence.

Proposition 3.B.1
.

Let
X,X_{1},X_{2},\ldots
be a sequence of
\mathbb{D}
-valued random variables. Assume that there exists a separable subset
\mathbb{D}_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{D}

such that
\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathbb{D}_{0})=1
. Then the following are equivalent:

•

The sequence
(X_{n})_{n\geq 1}
converges in distribution to
X
, i.e., for all
f\in C_{b}(\mathbb{D})
it holds that

\mathbb{E}[f(X_{n})]\to\mathbb{E}[f(X)]
as
n\to\infty
.

•

It holds that
d_{BL}(X_{n},X)\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.

Proof.

See Theorem 1.12.2, Addendum 1.12.3, and the following discussion in
van der Vaart and Wellner (1996)
.
∎

To discuss uniform stochastic convergence, we will for the remaining part of this section let
\Theta
be fixed set, which is used as a (possible) parameter set for every random variable. We say that a collection
(X^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
of
\mathbb{D}
-valued random variables is
separable
if there exists a separable subset
\mathbb{D}_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{D}
such that
\mathbb{P}(X^{\theta}\in\mathbb{D}_{0})=1
for all
\theta\in\Theta
. If
\mathbb{D}
is a separable metric space, then any collection of
\mathbb{D}
-valued random variables is automatically separable.

Now Lemma
3.B.1
justifies the following generalization of weak convergence uniformly over
\Theta
:

Definition 6
.

Let
(X_{n}^{\theta})_{n\in\mathbb{N},\theta\in\Theta}
and
(X^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
be collections of
\mathbb{D}
-valued random variables and assume that
(X^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
is separable. We say that:

(i)

X_{n}^{\theta}
converges uniformly in distribution over
\Theta
to
X^{\theta}
in
\mathbb{D}
, and write
X_{n}^{\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}X^{\theta}
, if

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}d_{BL(\mathbb{D})}(X_{n}^{\theta},X^{\theta})=0.

(ii)

X_{n}^{\theta}
converges uniformly in probability over

\Theta
to
X^{\theta}
in
\mathbb{D}
, and write
X_{n}^{\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$P/\Theta$}}X^{\theta}
, if

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}(d_{\mathbb{D}}(X_{n}^{\theta},X^{\theta})>\epsilon)=0

for every
\epsilon>0
.

If for some
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{D})
, it holds that
X_{n}^{\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}X^{\theta}
with
X^{\theta}(\mathbb{P})=\mu
for all
\theta\in\Theta
, we also write
X_{n}^{\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}\mu
. Similarly, we may replace the limit random variable
X^{\theta}
by a point
x\in\mathbb{D}
by interpreting
x
as the constant map
(\theta,\omega)\mapsto x
for
\theta\in\Theta
and
\omega\in\Omega
.

Note that if the parameter set
\Theta=\{\theta_{0}\}
is a singleton, then each type of uniform convergence reduces to the corresponding classical definition of convergence in distribution or probability.
If
\mathbb{D}
is a separable Banach space, we note that Definition
6
coincides with Definition 3 in
Lundborg et al. (2022b)
.

Proposition 3.B.2
.

Let
(X_{n}^{\theta})_{n\in\mathbb{N},\theta\in\Theta}
and
(X^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
be collections of
\mathbb{D}
-valued random variables and assume
(X^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
is separable. Then the following are equivalent:

a)

X_{n}^{\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}X^{\theta}
as
n\to\infty
.

b)

For any sequence
(\theta_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\Theta
it holds that
d_{BL}(X_{n}^{\theta_{n}},X^{\theta_{n}})\to 0
as
n\to 0
.

c)

For any sequence
(\theta_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\Theta
there exists a subsequence
(\theta_{k(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, with
k\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
strictly increasing,
such that

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}d_{BL}(X_{k(n)}^{\theta_{k(n)}},X^{\theta_{k(n)}})=0.

Moreover,
X_{n}^{\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$P/\Theta$}}X^{\theta}
if and only if for any sequence
(\theta_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\Theta
and any
\epsilon>0
it holds that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(d_{\mathbb{D}}(X_{n}^{\theta_{n}},X^{\theta_{n}})>\epsilon)=0.

Proof.

This is essentially Lemma 1 in
Kasy (2019)
for
\mathbb{D}
-valued random variables, except that we have added the equivalent condition c). The proof for the characterization of uniform convergence in probability is identical to the one given by
Kasy (2019)
, so we focus on the equivalence between a), b), and c).
To this end, we to prove that
a)\implies b)\implies c)\implies a)
.

The fact that a) implies b) follows directly from applying the bound

d_{BL}(X_{n}^{\theta_{n}},X^{\theta_{n}})\leq\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}d_{BL}(X_{n}^{\theta},X^{\theta})

and taking the limit as
n\to\infty
. We also see that b) implies c) since any sequence is a subsequence of itself.

We show that c) implies a) by contraposition.
Assume the negation of a), that is, there exists an
\epsilon>0
and a sequence
(\theta_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\Theta
such that

\displaystyle d_{BL}(X_{n}^{\theta_{n}},X^{\theta_{n}})>\epsilon

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, for all subsequences
(\theta_{k(n)})
of
(\theta_{n})
, it holds that
d_{BL}(X_{k(n)}^{\theta_{k(n)}},X^{\theta_{k(n)}})
does not converge to zero. This implies the negation of c).
∎

Proposition
3.B.2
will allow us to extend many results for classical stochastic convergence to uniform stochastic convergence.

Corollary 3.B.3
.

Let
(X_{n}^{\theta})_{n\in\mathbb{N},\theta\in\Theta}
be a collection of
\mathbb{D}
-valued random variables and let
x\in\mathbb{D}
. Then
X_{n}^{\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}x
if and only if
X_{n}^{\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$P/\Theta$}}x
.

Proof.

For any sequence
(\theta_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\Theta
, recall that
X_{n}^{\theta_{n}}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}}x
if and only if
X_{n}^{\theta_{n}}\xrightarrow{P}x
, see e.g. Lemma 5.1 in
Kallenberg (2021)
. Hence the statement follows directly from Proposition
3.B.2
(combined with
Proposition
3.B.1
).
∎

Our goal is to prove uniform versions of Slutsky’s theorem for
D[0,1]
, Rebolledo’s central limit theorem, and the chaining lemma for stochastic processes. To prove Slutsky’s lemma for
D[0,1]
, we first prove a general result for metric spaces.

Lemma 3.B.4
.

Let
(X^{\theta},X_{n}^{\theta},Y_{n}^{\theta})_{n\in\mathbb{N},\theta\in\Theta}
be a collection of
\mathbb{D}
-valued random variables and assume that
(X^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
is separable.
If
X_{n}^{\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}X^{\theta}
and
d_{\mathbb{D}}(X_{n}^{\theta},Y_{n}^{\theta})\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$P/\Theta$}}0
,
then it also holds that
Y_{n}^{\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}X^{\theta}
.

Proof.

By the triangle inequality of the bounded Lipschitz metric, we observe that

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}d_{BL}(Y_{n}^{\theta},X^{\theta})\leq\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}d_{BL}(Y_{n}^{\theta},X_{n}^{\theta})+\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}d_{BL}(X_{n}^{\theta},X^{\theta}).

The last term converges to zero by the assumption of
X_{n}^{\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}X^{\theta}
.
For the other term, let
\epsilon>0
and use the partition

(d_{\mathbb{D}}(X_{n}^{\theta},Y_{n}^{\theta})\leq\epsilon)\cup(d_{\mathbb{D}}(X_{n}^{\theta},Y_{n}^{\theta})>\epsilon)

to obtain that

\displaystyle d_{BL}(X_{n}^{\theta},Y_{n}^{\theta})

\displaystyle=\sup_{f\in BL_{1}(\mathbb{D})}|\mathbb{E}[f(X_{n}^{\theta})-f(Y_{n}^{\theta})]|

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon+\sup_{f\in BL_{1}(\mathbb{D})}\mathbb{E}[|f(X_{n}^{\theta})-f(Y_{n}^{\theta})|\mathbbl{1}(d_{\mathbb{D}}(X_{n}^{\theta},Y_{n}^{\theta})>\epsilon)]

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon+\mathbb{P}(d_{\mathbb{D}}(X_{n}^{\theta},Y_{n}^{\theta})>\epsilon).

Taking the supremum over
\Theta
and the limit superior for
n\to\infty
finishes the proof.
∎

The following formulation of the continuous mapping theorem is analogous to Theorem 1 in
Kasy (2019)
. The proof is almost identical, but we repeat it here for completeness.

Proposition 3.B.5
.

Let
(\mathbb{D}_{1},d_{1})
and
(\mathbb{D}_{2},d_{2})
be metric spaces, and let
\Phi\colon\mathbb{D}_{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}_{2}
be a Lipschitz continuous map.
Let
(X_{n}^{\theta})_{n\in\mathbb{N},\theta\in\Theta}
and
(X^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
be collections of
\mathbb{D}_{1}
-valued random variables, and assume
(X^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
is separable.

If
X_{n}^{\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}X^{\theta}
in
\mathbb{D}_{1}
, then
\Phi(X_{n}^{\theta})\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}\Phi(X^{\theta})
in
\mathbb{D}_{2}
.

Proof.

Note first that if
X^{\theta}
is in a separable subset
\mathbb{D}_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{D}_{1}
, then the variables
\Phi(X_{\theta})
for
\theta\in\Theta

are all in the separable subset
\Phi(\mathbb{D}_{0})\subseteq\mathbb{D}_{2}
.
Hence it is well-defined to consider uniform convergence in distribution towards
(\Phi(X^{\theta}))_{\theta\in\Theta}
. Let
f\in BL_{1}(\mathbb{D}_{2})
and let
K
be the Lipschitz constant of
\Phi
. Consider the map

g\colon\mathbb{D}_{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R},\qquad g(x)=\min(1,K^{-1})f(\Phi(x)).

Then
\|g\|_{\infty}\leq\|f\|_{\infty}\leq 1
and for all
x,y\in\mathbb{D}_{1}
,

\displaystyle|g(x)-g(y)|

\displaystyle\leq\min(1,K^{-1})d_{2}(\Phi(x),\Phi(y))

\displaystyle\leq\min(1,K^{-1})Kd_{1}(x,y)\leq d_{1}(x,y)

Hence
g\in BL_{1}(\mathbb{D}_{1})
. It follows that

\displaystyle d_{BL_{1}(\mathbb{D}_{2})}(\Phi(X_{n}^{\theta}),\Phi(X^{\theta}))

\displaystyle=\sup_{f\in BL_{1}(\mathbb{D}_{2})}|\mathbb{E}[f(\Phi(X_{n}^{\theta}))-f(\Phi(X^{\theta}))]|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\min(1,K^{-1})}\sup_{g\in BL_{1}(\mathbb{D}_{1})}|\mathbb{E}[g(X_{n}^{\theta})-g(X^{\theta})]|

\displaystyle\leq\max(1,K)\cdot d_{BL_{1}(\mathbb{D}_{1})}(X_{n}^{\theta},X^{\theta})

Taking the supremum over
\Theta
and the limit superior as
n\to\infty
finish the proof.
∎

We will also need the following two notions of tightness.

Definition 7
.

Let
(\mu^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
be a family of probability measures on
\mathbb{D}
, and let
(X^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
and
(X_{n}^{\theta})_{n\in\mathbb{N},\theta\in\Theta}
be collections of
\mathbb{D}
-valued random variables.

i)

We say that
(\mu^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
is
tight
if for any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a compact set
K\subseteq\mathbb{D}
such that

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mu^{\theta}(K^{c})<\varepsilon
.
We say that
(X^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
is
uniformly tight
if the collection of distributions
(X^{\theta}(\mathbb{P}))_{\theta\in\Theta}
is tight.

ii)

The sequence
((X_{n}^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of collections is said to be
sequentially tight
if for any sequence
(\theta_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\Theta
, the sequence of distributions
(X_{n}^{\theta_{n}}(\mathbb{P}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight.

Definition
7
i) is a classical concept, whereas sequential tightness was introduced by
Lundborg et al. (2022b)
and relaxes uniform tightness for sequences of variables parametrized over an infinite set.

The importance of tightness is mainly due to Prokhorov’s theorem
(
Kallenberg, 2021
, Theorem 23.2)
, which states that if
\mathbb{D}
is a Polish space
4
4

4

The ‘only if’ part does not require separability nor completeness.
, then
(\mu^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
is tight if and only if all sequences in
(\mu^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
have a weakly convergent subsequence.

The continuous mapping theorem in Proposition
3.B.5
is more restrictive than the classical theorem as it requires Lipschitz continuity. However, we also have an alternative version of uniform continuous mapping when the limit variable is tight.

Proposition 3.B.6
.

Let
(\mathbb{D}_{1},d_{1})
and
(\mathbb{D}_{2},d_{2})
be Polish spaces, and let
(X_{n}^{\theta})_{n\in\mathbb{N},\theta\in\Theta}
and
(X^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
be collections of
\mathbb{D}_{1}
-valued random variables. Assume
(X^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
is uniformly tight, and let
\Phi\colon\mathbb{D}_{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{D}_{2}
be a map that is continuous on the support of
(X^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
.

If
X_{n}^{\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}X^{\theta}
in
\mathbb{D}_{1}
, then
\Phi(X_{n}^{\theta})\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}\Phi(X^{\theta})
in
\mathbb{D}_{2}
.

Proof.

Same as the proof of Proposition 10 in
Lundborg et al. (2022b)
, but with norms of differences replaced by metric distances.
∎

3.B.2
Uniform stochastic convergence in Skorokhod space

In this section we consider the special case where
(\mathbb{D},d{D})
is the Skorokhod space
(D[0,1],d^{\circ})
. We can also equip
D[0,1]
with the uniform norm,
\|x\|_{\infty}=\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|x_{t}|
, and it known that weak convergence based on either
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
or
d^{\circ}
are equivalent when the limit is continuous. We now extend this result to stochastic convergence uniformly over
\Theta
.

Proposition 3.B.7

(Skorokhod equivalence)
.

Let
(X_{n}^{\theta})_{n\in\mathbb{N},\theta\in\Theta}
be a collection of
D[0,1]
-valued random variables and let
(X^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
be a uniformly tight collection of
C[0,1]
-valued random variables. Then
X_{n}^{\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}X^{\theta}
in
(D[0,1],d^{\circ})
if and only if
X_{n}^{\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}X^{\theta}
in
(D[0,1],\|\cdot\|_{\infty})
. In the affirmative,
\|X_{n}^{\theta}\|_{\infty}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}\|X\|_{\infty}
.

Proof.

To avoid ambiguity in the topology on
D[0,1]
, we will throughout this proof use
\mathbb{D}^{\circ}
to denote the metric space
(D[0,1],d^{\circ})
and we use
\mathbb{D}_{\infty}
to denote the Banach space
(D[0,1],\|\cdot\|_{\infty})
. Note also that
C[0,1]
is separable within
\mathbb{D}_{\infty}
, so
(X^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
is separable, and hence the convergence
X_{n}^{\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}X^{\theta}
is well-defined in the non-separable space
\mathbb{D}_{\infty}
.

The ‘if’ part is clear since
d^{\circ}(x,y)\leq\|x-y\|_{\infty}
for all
x,y\in D[0,1]
.

For the ‘only if’ part, assume that
X_{n}^{\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}X^{\theta}
in
\mathbb{D}^{\circ}
and let
(\theta_{n})\subseteq\Theta
be an arbitrary sequence. Since
(X^{\theta_{n}}(\mathbb{P}))
is tight, Prokhorov’s Theorem asserts that there exists a subsequence

(\theta_{k(n)})
and a probability distribution
\mu
on
C[0,1]
such that
X^{\theta_{k(n)}}(\mathbb{P})\xrightarrow{wk}\mu
in
\mathbb{D}^{\circ}
. By the triangle inequality

\displaystyle d_{BL(\mathbb{D}^{\circ})}(X_{k(n)}^{\theta_{k(n)}},\mu)\leq d_{BL(\mathbb{D}^{\circ})}(X_{k(n)}^{\theta_{k(n)}},X^{\theta_{k(n)}})+d_{BL(\mathbb{D}^{\circ})}(X^{\theta_{k(n)}},\mu)\to 0,\quad n\to 0.

This shows that also
X_{k(n)}^{\theta_{k(n)}}(\mathbb{P})\xrightarrow{wk}\mu
in
\mathbb{D}^{\circ}
. Now we can use that weak convergence in the Skorokhod topology and the uniform topology are equivalent when the limit is continuous
(
Kallenberg, 2021
, Theorem 23.9 (iii))
.
We therefore conclude that the convergences
X^{\theta_{k(n)}}(\mathbb{P})\xrightarrow{wk}\mu
and
X_{k(n)}^{\theta_{k(n)}}(\mathbb{P})\xrightarrow{wk}\mu
also hold in
\mathbb{D}_{\infty}
.
But then another use of the triangle inequality shows that

d_{BL(\mathbb{D}_{\infty})}(X_{k(n)}^{\theta_{k(n)}},X^{\theta_{k(n)}})\leq d_{BL(\mathbb{D}_{\infty})}(X_{k(n)}^{\theta_{k(n)}},\mu)+d_{BL(\mathbb{D}_{\infty})}(\mu,X^{\theta_{k(n)}})\to 0.

Since
(\theta_{k(n)})
is a subsequence of the arbitrarily chosen sequence
(\theta_{n})
, we conclude that
X_{n}^{\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}X^{\theta}
in
\mathbb{D}_{\infty}
by Proposition
3.B.2
.

Finally, as the uniform norm is Lipschitz continuous as a map from
\mathbb{D}_{\infty}
to
\mathbb{R}
, the continuous mapping theorem formulated in Proposition
3.B.5

yields that

\displaystyle X_{n}^{\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}X^{\theta}\>\text{ in }\>\mathbb{D}_{\infty}\qquad\implies\qquad\|X_{n}^{\theta}\|_{\infty}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}\|X\|_{\infty}.

This establishes the last part of the lemma.
∎

Using
\|\mu\|_{\infty}
to denote the pushforward measure for any
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(D([0,1]))
we restate the result above for a fixed limit distribution.

Corollary 3.B.8
.

Let
(X_{n}^{\theta})_{n\in\mathbb{N},\theta\in\Theta}
be a collection of
D[0,1]
-valued random variables and let
\mu
be a probability measure on
C[0,1]
.
Then
X_{n}^{\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}\mu
in
(D[0,1],d^{\circ})
if and only if
X_{n}^{\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}\mu
in
(D[0,1],\|\cdot\|_{\infty})
. In the affirmative,
\|X_{n}^{\theta}\|_{\infty}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}\|\mu\|_{\infty}
.

Proof.

Since
\mu
is a probability measure on the Polish space
C[0,1]
, it is, in particular, tight
(
Billingsley, 2013
, Theorem 1.3)
. Hence the statement is a special case of Proposition
3.B.7
.
∎

Now we are ready to prove a uniform version of Slutsky’s theorem in the Skorokhod space.

Lemma 3.B.9

(Uniform Slutsky in Skorokhod space)
.

Let
(X^{\theta},X_{n}^{\theta},Y_{n}^{\theta})_{n\in\mathbb{N},\theta\in\Theta}
be a collection of
D[0,1]
-valued random variables such that
Y_{n}^{\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$P/\Theta$}}0
and
X_{n}^{\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}X^{\theta}
in
D[0,1]
.
Then it holds that
X_{n}^{\theta}+Y_{n}^{\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}X^{\theta}
.

Proof.

Since
Y_{n}^{\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$P/\Theta$}}0
, Corollary
3.B.8
implies that
\|Y_{n}^{\theta}\|_{\infty}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}0
, and Corollary
3.B.3
implies that
\|Y_{n}^{\theta}\|_{\infty}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$P/\Theta$}}0
.
Using the trivial estimate
d^{\circ}(x+y,x)\leq\|(x+y)-x\|_{\infty}=\|y\|_{\infty}
for
x,y\in D[0,1]
, it follows that

d^{\circ}(X_{n}^{\theta}+Y_{n}^{\theta},X_{n}^{\theta})\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$P/\Theta$}}0
.
Combining the latter with
X_{n}^{\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}X^{\theta}
, the desired conclusion now follows from Lemma
3.B.4
.
∎

We also have a related result for sums of independent sequences.

Lemma 3.B.10
.

Let
(X_{n}^{\theta},Y_{n}^{\theta})_{n\in\mathbb{N},\theta\in\Theta}
be a collection of
D[0,1]
-valued random variables and let
(X^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
and
(Y^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
be uniformly tight collections of
C[0,1]
-valued random variables.
Assume that
X_{n}^{\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}X^{\theta}
and
Y_{n}^{\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}Y^{\theta}
in
D[0,1]
, and that for each
\theta\in\Theta
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, it holds that

X_{n}^{\theta}\,\perp\!\!\!\perp\,Y_{n}^{\theta}
.
Let
Z^{\theta}
have distribution

X^{\theta}(\mathbb{P})*Y^{\theta}(\mathbb{P})
, that is, the same distribution as the sum of two independent copies of each of
X^{\theta}
and
Y^{\theta}
.

Then it also holds that

X_{n}^{\theta}+Y_{n}^{\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}Z^{\theta}

in
(D[0,1],\|\cdot\|_{\infty})
.

Proof.

We may assume without loss of generality that
X^{\theta}\,\perp\!\!\!\perp\,Y^{\theta}
and that
Z^{\theta}=X^{\theta}+Y^{\theta}
. Let
(\theta_{n})\subseteq\Theta
be an arbitrary sequence. By tightness of
(X^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
and
(Y^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
, we can apply Prokhorov’s theorem twice to obtain probability measures
\mu
and
\nu
on
C[0,1]
, and a subsequence
(\theta_{k(n)})
, such that

X^{\theta_{k(n)}}(\mathbb{P})\xrightarrow{wk}\mu

and

Y^{\theta_{k(n)}}(\mathbb{P})\xrightarrow{wk}\nu
.
Hence the product measures converge,

X^{\theta_{k(n)}}(\mathbb{P})\otimes Y^{\theta_{k(n)}}(\mathbb{P})\xrightarrow{wk}\mu\otimes\nu,

in
C[0,1]
as
n\to\infty
, see, for example, Theorem 2.8 (ii) in
Billingsley (2013)
.

Since
X_{n}^{\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}X^{\theta}
in
D[0,1]
by assumption and
(X^{\theta})
is uniformly tight in
C[0,1]
, Proposition
3.B.7
implies that the convergence also holds in
(D[0,1],\|\cdot\|_{\infty})
.
The triangle inequality now yields

\displaystyle d_{BL}(X_{k(n)}^{\theta_{k(n)}},\mu)\leq d_{BL}(X_{k(n)}^{\theta_{k(n)}},X^{\theta_{k(n)}})+d_{BL}(X^{\theta_{k(n)}},\mu)\to 0,

so also
X_{k(n)}^{\theta_{k(n)}}(\mathbb{P})\xrightarrow{wk}\mu
in
(D[0,1],\|\cdot\|_{\infty})
. An analogous computation shows that
Y_{k(n)}^{\theta_{k(n)}}(\mathbb{P})\xrightarrow{wk}\nu
, and hence also

\displaystyle X_{k(n)}^{\theta_{k(n)}}(\mathbb{P})\otimes Y_{k(n)}^{\theta_{k(n)}}(\mathbb{P})\xrightarrow{wk}\mu\otimes\nu

in the product space
D[0,1]\times D[0,1]
endowed with the uniform product topology. From the independence statements

X^{\theta}\,\perp\!\!\!\perp\,Y^{\theta}
and

X_{n}^{\theta}\,\perp\!\!\!\perp\,Y_{n}^{\theta}
, we have thus shown that

(X^{\theta_{k(n)}},Y^{\theta_{k(n)}})\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}}\mu\otimes\nu\quad\text{and}\quad(X_{k(n)}^{\theta_{k(n)}},Y_{k(n)}^{\theta_{k(n)}})\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}}\mu\otimes\nu

in the uniform product topology. Since addition
+\colon D[0,1]\times D[0,1]\to D[0,1]
is continuous with respect to this topology, we conclude by the classical continuous mapping theorem that

\displaystyle Z^{\theta_{k(n)}}=X^{\theta_{k(n)}}+Y^{\theta_{k(n)}}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}}\mu*\nu\quad\text{and}\quad X_{k(n)}^{\theta_{k(n)}}+Y_{k(n)}^{\theta_{k(n)}}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}}\mu*\nu.

It now follows that

\displaystyle d_{BL}

\displaystyle(X_{k(n)}^{\theta_{k(n)}}+Y_{k(n)}^{\theta_{k(n)}},Z^{\theta_{k(n)}})

\displaystyle\leq d_{BL}(X_{k(n)}^{\theta_{k(n)}}+Y_{k(n)}^{\theta_{k(n)}},\mu*\nu)+d_{BL}(\mu*\nu,Z^{\theta_{k(n)}})\to 0.

Since
(\theta_{k(n)})
is a subsequence of the arbitrarily chosen sequence
(\theta_{n})
, we conclude that
X_{n}^{\theta}+Y_{n}^{\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}Z^{\theta}
in
(D[0,1],\|\cdot\|_{\infty})
by Proposition
3.B.2
.
∎

We also need the following lemma, which is a generalization of the classical result: pointwise convergence of a sequence of monotone functions towards a continuous limit is in fact uniform over compact intervals.

Lemma 3.B.11
.

Let
(X_{n}^{\theta})_{n\in\mathbb{N},\theta\in\Theta}
be a collection of
D[0,1]
-valued random variables with non-decreasing sample paths.
Let
(f^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}\subset C[0,1]
be a uniformly equicontinuous collection of non-decreasing functions. If
X_{n}^{\theta}(t)\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$P/\Theta$}}f^{\theta}(t)
for each
t\in[0,1]
, then it also holds that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|X_{n}^{\theta}(t)-f^{\theta}(t)|\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$P/\Theta$}}0.

Proof.

Let
\epsilon>0
. By uniform equicontinuity we can find

0=t_{1}<\cdots<t_{k}=1
such that
f^{\theta}(t_{i})-f^{\theta}(t_{i-1})<\epsilon/2
for all
\theta
and
i
. Using that
X_{n}^{\theta}
and
f^{\theta}
are non-decreasing, we observe that for
t_{i-1}\leq t\leq t_{i}
:

\displaystyle X_{n}^{\theta}(t)-f^{\theta}(t)

\displaystyle\leq X_{n}^{\theta}(t_{i})-f^{\theta}(t_{i})+\epsilon/2,

\displaystyle X_{n}^{\theta}(t)-f^{\theta}(t)

\displaystyle\geq X_{n}^{\theta}(t_{i-1})-f^{\theta}(t_{i-1})-\epsilon/2.

Combining the inequalities over the entire grid we have

\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|X_{n}^{\theta}(t)-f^{\theta}(t)|\leq\max_{i=0,\ldots,k}|X_{n}^{\theta}(t_{i})-f^{\theta}(t_{i})|+\epsilon/2.

By assumption,
X_{n}^{\theta}(t)\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$P/\Theta$}}f^{\theta}(t)
for each
t
, and in particular

\max_{i=0,\ldots,k}|X_{n}^{\theta}(t_{i})-f^{\theta}(t_{i})|\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$P/\Theta$}}0

as
n\to\infty
. We therefore conclude that

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|X_{n}^{\theta}(t)-f^{\theta}(t)|>\epsilon\Big)\leq\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{i=0,\ldots,k}|X_{n}^{\theta}(t_{i})-f^{\theta}(t_{i})|>\epsilon/2\Big)\longrightarrow 0

as
n\to\infty
.
∎

The last auxiliary result of this section is an example of Prokhorov’s method of “tightness + identification of limit”.

Lemma 3.B.12
.

Let
(\mathbb{D},d{D})
be either
(C[0,1],\|\cdot\|_{\infty})
or
(D[0,1],d^{\circ})
, and let
(X^{\theta},X_{n}^{\theta})_{n\in\mathbb{N},\theta\in\Theta}
be a collection of
\mathbb{D}
-valued random variables with
(X^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
separable. Suppose that

•

The finite dimensional marginals converge uniformly: for any
0\leq t_{1}<\cdots<t_{k}\leq 1

\displaystyle\pi_{t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}}(X_{n}^{\theta})\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}\pi_{t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}}(X^{\theta}),\qquad n\to\infty,

where
\pi_{t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}}\colon\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
is the projection given by
\pi_{t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}}(x)=(x(t_{1}),\ldots,x(t_{k}))
.

•

(X_{n}^{\theta})_{n\in\mathbb{N},\theta\in\Theta}
is sequentially tight.

•

(X^{\theta})_{n\in\mathbb{N},\theta\in\Theta}
is uniformly tight.

Then
X_{n}^{\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}X^{\theta}
as
n\to\infty
.

Proof.

The statement is analogous to Proposition 18 in
Lundborg et al. (2022b)
, the difference being that the functionals
\langle\cdot,h\rangle
in

Lundborg et al. (2022b)
have been replaced by the functionals
\pi_{t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}}
.

The proof of
Lundborg et al. (2022b)
also works in our case, given that the finite dimensional marginals form a separating class for the both the Borel algebra on
C[0,1]
and the Borel algebra on
D[0,1]
. This is established in
Billingsley (2013)
, Example 1.3 and Theorem 12.5 (iii).
∎

3.B.3
Chaining in time uniformly over a parameter

We extend the basic chaining arguments to hold uniformly over
\Theta
. Our arguments closely follow those of
Pollard (1984, Chapter VII.2.)
and
Newey (1991)
. The results are formulated for processes indexed over a general metric space
T
, but we will only apply the results in the case
T=[0,1]
. We have the following extension of stochastic equicontinuity to the uniform setting.

Definition 8
.

A collection of sequences

\big(Z^{(n),\theta}\big)_{n\in\mathbb{N},\theta\in\Theta}=\Big(Z_{t}^{(n),\theta}\Big)_{t\in T,n\in\mathbb{N},\theta\in\Theta}

of stochastic processes indexed over a metric space
(T,d)
is called
stochastically equicontinuous uniformly over
\Theta
if for all
\epsilon,\eta>0
there exists
\delta>0
such that

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{s,t\in T\colon d(s,t)\leq\delta}\big|Z_{s}^{(n),\theta}-Z_{t}^{(n),\theta}\big|>\epsilon\Big)<\eta.

In Section 2.8.2 of
van der Vaart and Wellner (1996)
, the same definition is given in the context of empirical processes.
Recall that we write, e.g.,
Z^{(n)}
as a shorthand for
Z^{(n),\theta}
and let the dependency on
\theta
be implicit for notational ease. We also write
\sup_{d(s,t)\leq\delta}
as a shorthand for
\sup_{s,t\in T\colon d(s,t)\leq\delta}
.
Definition
8
is a direct extension of pointwise stochastic equicontinuity.
Accordingly, Theorem 2.1 from
Newey (1991)
generalizes as follows:

Lemma 3.B.13
.

Let
(Z_{t}^{(n)})_{t\in T,n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of stochastic processes indexed by a compact metric space
T
. Assume that
(Z_{t}^{(n)})
is stochastically equicontinuous uniformly over
\Theta
and that for each
t\in T
it holds that
Z_{t}^{(n)}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$P/\Theta$}}0
. Then
\sup_{t\in T}|Z_{t}^{(n)}|\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$P/\Theta$}}0
as
n\to\infty
.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon,\eta>0
be given, and let
\delta>0
be the corresponding distance obtained from the uniform stochastic equicontinuity of
(Z^{(n)})
. By compactness of
T
there exists a finite set
T^{*}\subseteq T
such that
T=\bigcup_{t\in T^{*}}B(t,\delta)
. By the triangle inequality we get that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in T}|Z_{t}^{(n)}|=\sup_{t\in T^{*}}\sup_{s\in B(t,\delta)}|Z_{s}^{(n)}|\leq\sup_{t\in T^{*}}|Z_{t}^{(n)}|+\sup_{t\in T^{*}}\sup_{s\in B(t,\delta)}|Z_{s}^{(n)}-Z_{t}^{(n)}|.

Since
T^{*}
is finite, it follows that
\sup_{t\in T^{*}}|Z_{t}^{(n)}|\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$P/\Theta$}}0
, which combined with the inequality implies that

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}\big(\sup_{t\in T}|Z_{t}^{(n)}|>2\varepsilon\big)

\displaystyle\leq 0+\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{t\in T^{*}}\sup_{s\in B(t,\delta)}|Z_{t}^{(n)}-Z_{t}^{(n)}|>\varepsilon\Big)\leq\eta.

As
\varepsilon,\eta>0
were chosen arbitrarily, we conclude that
\sup_{t\in T}|Z_{t}^{(n)}|\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$P/\Theta$}}0
.
∎

To establish uniform stochastic equicontinuity we extend the chaining lemma to a uniform setting. To formulate the theorem we first need some classical definitions related to chaining.

Definition 9
.

Let
T
be a compact metric space. A subset
T^{*}\subseteq T
is called a
\delta
-net if
\bigcup_{t\in T^{*}}B(t,\delta)=T
.
The covering number

N(\delta)=N(\delta,T)\coloneqq\min\{\,|T^{*}|\colon T^{*}\subseteq T,T^{*}\text{ is a }\delta\text{-net}\,\}

is the smallest possible cardinality of a
\delta
-net, which is finite by compactness. The associated covering integral is

\displaystyle J(\delta)=\int_{0}^{\delta}\left(2\log(N(\epsilon)/\epsilon)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathrm{d}\epsilon,\qquad 0\leq\delta\leq 1.

Lemma 3.B.14
.

Let
(T,d)
be a metric space with finite covering integral
J(\cdot)
and let
(Z_{t}^{\theta})_{t\in T,\theta\in\Theta}
be a collection of stochastic processes indexed by
T
with continuous sample paths. Assume there is a uniform constant
\varsigma>0
such that, for all
s,t\in T
and
\eta>0
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}\left(|Z_{s}^{\theta}-Z_{t}^{\theta}|>\eta\cdot d(s,t)\right)\leq 2e^{-\frac{\eta^{2}}{2\varsigma^{2}}}.

Then, for all
0<\epsilon<1
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{d(s,t)\leq\epsilon}|Z_{s}^{\theta}-Z_{t}^{\theta}|>26\varsigma J(\epsilon)\Big)\leq 2\epsilon.

Proof.

The lemma is a direct consequence of classical chaining lemma
(
Pollard, 1984
, page 144)
. For each
\theta\in\Theta
, the conditions of the chaining lemma are met for
(Z_{t}^{\theta})_{t\in T}
with sub-exponential factor
\varsigma
. This implies, in particular, that for any
\theta\in\Theta
and
0<\epsilon<1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{d(s,t)\leq\epsilon}|Z_{s}^{\theta}-Z_{t}^{\theta}|>26\varsigma J(\epsilon)\Big)\leq 2\epsilon,

which is equivalent to the conclusion of the lemma.
∎

This immediately implies the following corollary.

Corollary 3.B.15
.

Let
(T,d)
be a metric space with finite covering integral
J(\cdot)
and let
(Z^{(n),\theta})
be a sequence of stochastic processes on
T
with continuous sample paths. Assume there exists a constant
\varsigma>0
such that, for all
s,t\in T
and
\eta>0
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}\left(|Z_{s}^{(n),\theta}-Z_{t}^{(n),\theta}|>\eta\cdot d(s,t)\right)\leq 2e^{-\frac{\eta^{2}}{2\varsigma^{2}}}.

Then
(Z^{(n)})
is stochastically equicontinuous uniformly over
\Theta
.

For stochastic processes with continuous sample paths, stochastic equicontinuity turns out to be equivalent to sequential tightness (Definition
7
ii)).

Proposition 3.B.16
.

Let
(Z^{(n),\theta})_{n\in\mathbb{N},\theta\in\Theta}
be a collection of
C[0,1]
-valued random variables such that
\mathbb{P}(Z_{0}^{(n),\theta}=0)=1
all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\theta\in\Theta
.
The following are equivalent:

1.

(Z^{(n),\theta})
is stochastically equicontinuous uniformly over
\Theta
.

2.

(Z^{(n),\theta})
is sequentially tight.

Proof.

The equivalence is a straightforward application of Theorem 7.3 in
Billingsley (2013)
.
Condition
(i)
of the aforementioned theorem is satisfied for any sequence of measures from the collection
(Z^{(n),\theta}(\mathbb{P}))_{n\in\mathbb{N},\theta\in\Theta}
, since
Z_{0}^{(n),\theta}=0
almost surely for all
n
and
\theta
.
For any sequence
(\theta_{n})\subseteq\Theta
, stochastic equicontinuity uniformly over
\Theta
implies condition
(ii)
of Theorem 7.3 in
Billingsley (2013)
for the measures
((Z^{(n),\theta_{n}})(\mathbb{P}))
.
We therefore conclude that stochastic equicontinuity uniformly over
\Theta
implies sequential tightness.

On the contrary, assume that
(Z^{(n),\theta})
is sequentially tight and let
\epsilon,\eta>0
be given. For each
n
, choose
\theta_{n}
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{|s-t|\leq\delta}\big|Z_{s}^{(n),\theta}-Z_{t}^{(n),\theta}\big|\geq\epsilon\Big)\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{|s-t|\leq\delta}\big|Z_{s}^{(n),\theta_{n}}-Z_{t}^{(n),\theta_{n}}\big|\geq\epsilon\Big)+\frac{1}{n}.

Since
((Z^{(n),\theta_{n}})(\mathbb{P}))
is tight by assumption, condition
(ii)
of Theorem 7.3 asserts that there exists
\delta,N>0
such that

\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{|s-t|\leq\delta}\big|Z_{s}^{(n),\theta_{n}}-Z_{t}^{(n),\theta_{n}}\big|\geq\epsilon\Big)<\eta

for
n\geq N
. Combining both inequalities and taking the limit superior finish the proof.
∎

3.C. The Functional Martingale CLT

In this section we state Rebolledo’s martingale CLT
(
Rebolledo, 1980
)
based on its formulation in
Andersen et al. (1993)
, and then we extend the result to a uniform version without fixed variance functions.
The one-dimensional case suffices for our purpose, so for simplicity, every local martingale in the following is a real-valued stochastic process. For a local square integrable martingale
(M_{t})
, we let
\langle M\rangle(t)
denote its quadratic characteristic.
The theorem requires a condition on the jumps of the local martingales, for which we will need the following definition.

Definition 10
.

Let
M_{t}
be a local square integrable
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-martingale. For any
\varepsilon>0
, we define
\langle M_{\varepsilon}\rangle(t)
to be the quadratic characteristic of the pure jump-process given by

t\mapsto\sum_{0\leq s\leq t}M_{s}\mathbbl{1}(|\Delta M_{s}|>\varepsilon).

We also need a representation of Gaussian martingales, which ensures their continuity.

Proposition 3.C.1
.

Let
(B_{t})_{t\in[0,\infty)}
be a Brownian motion on
[0,\infty)
with continuous sample paths. For every non-decreasing
f\in C[0,1]
, the process
(B_{f(t)})_{t\in[0,1]}
is a continuous mean zero Gaussian martingale on
[0,1]
with variance function
f
.

Consequently, if
U=(U_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
is a mean zero Gaussian martingale with a continuous variance function
V
, then
U
has the distributional representation

(U_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}(B_{V(t)})_{t\in[0,1]}.

(3.C.1)

Proof.

Let
f\in C[0,1]
be non-decreasing. From the properties of Brownian motion, it follows directly that the time-transformed process
(B_{f(t)})_{t\in[0,1]}
is a mean zero Gaussian process with variance function
f
.
Since
f
is continuous, each sample path
t\mapsto B_{f(t)}
is a composition of continuous functions and thus continuous itself.
Since
f
is non-decreasing, the time-transformation also preserves the martingale property. This establishes the first part.

For the second part, recall that the covariance function of a martingale is determined by its variance function.
Hence the first part implies that the right-hand side in (
3.C.1
) is a Gaussian process with the same mean and covariance structure as the left-hand side. Since the distribution of a Gaussian processes is uniquely determined by its mean and covariance structure, the equality in distribution follows.
∎

Proposition
3.C.1
is a simple, distributional variant of the
Dubins-Schwarz theorem, see
Revuz and Yor (2013)
, Chapter V, Theorems 1.6 and 1.7.
The Dubins-Schwarz theorem implies that, in fact,
U_{t}=B_{V(t)}
for
t\in[0,1]
,
where
B
is a Brownian motion on
[0,V(1)]
. For the purpose of this work we only need
the simpler, distributional equality (
3.C.1
).

We can now formulate Rebolledo’s CLT for local martingales.
To this end, note that Proposition
3.C.1
ensures the existence of the continuous
Gaussian limit martingale
U
when the variance function
V
is continuous.

Theorem 3.C.2

(Rebolledo’s CLT)
.

Let
(U^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence a local square integrable martingales in
D[0,1]
, possibly defined on different sample spaces and with different filtrations for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
U
be a continuous Gaussian martingale with continuous variance function
V\colon[0,1]\to[0,\infty)
, and assume that
U^{(n)}_{0}=U_{0}=0
.
Suppose that for every
t\in[0,1]
and
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\langle U^{(n)}\rangle(t)\xrightarrow{P}V(t)\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\langle U_{\varepsilon}^{(n)}\rangle(t)\xrightarrow{P}0,

as
n\to\infty
.
Then it holds that
U^{(n)}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}}U

in
D[0,1]
as
n\to\infty
.

Proof.

This is a special case of Theorem
\mathrm{II}.5.2
in
Andersen et al. (1993)
.
∎

The general formulation of Rebolledo’s CLT above, which allows for
n
-dependent sample spaces and filtrations, can now be leveraged to obtain a uniform version via the sequential characterization of uniform stochastic convergence.

Theorem 3.C.3

(Uniform Rebolledo CLT)
.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\theta\in\Theta
:

•

Let
\mathcal{F}^{(n),\theta}=(\mathcal{F}_{t}^{(n),\theta})_{t\in[0,1]}
be a filtration satisfying the usual conditions.

•

Let
U_{t}^{(n),\theta}
be a local square integrable
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{(n),\theta}
-martingale in
D[0,1]
with
U_{0}^{(n),\theta}=0
.

•

Let
V^{\theta}\colon[0,1]\to[0,\infty)
be a non-decreasing function with
V^{\theta}(0)=0
.

Assume that
(V^{\theta})_{\theta\in\Theta}
is uniformly equicontinuous and that
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}V^{\theta}(1)<\infty
.
Assume further that for every
\varepsilon>0
and
t\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle\langle U^{(n),\theta}\rangle(t)\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$P/\Theta$}}V^{\theta}(t)\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\langle U_{\varepsilon}^{(n),\theta}\rangle(t)\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$P/\Theta$}}0,

(3.C.2)

as
n\to\infty
. Then it holds that

\displaystyle U^{(n),\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}U^{\theta},\qquad n\to\infty,

in
D[0,1]
uniformly over
\Theta
, where for each
\theta\in\Theta
,
U^{\theta}
is a mean zero continuous Gaussian martingale on
[0,1]
with variance function
V^{\theta}
.

Proof.

We will use the characterization of uniform convergence as stated in Proposition
3.B.2
c). To this end, let
(\theta_{n})\subseteq\Theta
be an arbitrary sequence. By assumption
(V_{\theta_{n}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a uniformly equicontinuous and bounded sequence of functions on a compact interval, so the Arzelà–Ascoli theorem states that there exists a subsequence
\theta_{k(n)}
, with
k\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
strictly increasing, and a function
\tilde{V}\in C[0,1]
such that

\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|V^{\theta_{k(n)}}(t)-\tilde{V}(t)|\longrightarrow 0,\qquad n\to\infty.

Since each function
V^{\theta_{k(n)}}
is non-decreasing, it follows that
\tilde{V}
is non-decreasing. It also holds that

\tilde{V}(0)=\lim_{n\to\infty}V^{\theta_{k(n)}}(0)=0
, and therefore
\tilde{V}
is
the variance function of a continuous Gaussian martingale
\tilde{U}
with
\tilde{U}_{0}=0
.

By assumption of the convergences in (
3.C.2
), we may conclude that

\displaystyle|\langle U^{(k(n)),\theta_{k(n)}}\rangle(t)-\tilde{V}(t)|\leq\underbrace{|\langle U^{(k(n)),\theta_{k(n)}}\rangle(t)-V^{\theta_{k(n)}}(t)|}_{\xrightarrow{P}0}+\underbrace{|V^{\theta_{k(n)}}(t)-\tilde{V}(t)|}_{\to 0}\xrightarrow{P}0

and that

\langle U_{\epsilon}^{(k(n)),\theta_{k(n)}}\rangle(t)\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. Thus we have established the conditions of the classical Rebolledo CLT – Theorem
3.C.2
– for the sequence
U^{(k(n)),\theta_{k(n)}}
and the Gaussian martingale
\tilde{U}
with variance function
\tilde{V}
. We therefore conclude that

\displaystyle U^{(k(n)),\theta_{k(n)}}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}}\tilde{U}

in
D[0,1]
as
n\to\infty
.

We now establish that the sequence
(U^{\theta_{k(n)}})
also converges in distribution to
\tilde{U}
in
C[0,1]
, and in particular also in
D[0,1]
. To this end, we use the characterization of convergence in distribution in
C[0,1]
from Theorem 7.5 in
Billingsley (2013)
, which states that we need to show that

1.

For all
0\leq t_{1}<\cdots<t_{m}\leq 1
, it holds that

(U_{t_{1}}^{\theta_{k(n)}},\ldots,U_{t_{m}}^{\theta_{k(n)}})\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}}(\tilde{U}_{t_{1}},\ldots,\tilde{U}_{t_{m}}),\qquad n\to\infty.

2.

For all
\epsilon>0

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{|t-s|<\delta}|U_{t}^{\theta_{k(n)}}-U_{s}^{\theta_{k(n)}}|>\epsilon\Big)=0.

The first condition is clear since all the marginals are multivariate Gaussian, and the mean and variance of the sequence converges to the mean and variance of the limit distribution. The second condition follows from the same computation as in the proof of Lemma
3.A.11
. By Theorem 7.5 in
Billingsley (2013)
we therefore conclude that

\displaystyle U^{\theta_{k(n)}}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}}\tilde{U},\qquad\text{for}\>\>n\to\infty,

in
C[0,1]
, and hence also in
D[0,1]
.

We can now apply the triangle inequality for the bounded Lipschitz metric to conclude that

\displaystyle d_{BL}(U^{(k(n)),\theta_{k(n)}},U^{\theta_{k(n)}})\leq d_{BL}(U^{(k(n)),\theta_{k(n)}},\tilde{U})+d_{BL}(\tilde{U},U^{\theta_{k(n)}})\longrightarrow 0.

Since
(\theta_{n})\subseteq\Theta
was an arbitrary sequence, we conclude that
U^{(n),\theta}\xrightarrow{\scalebox{0.6}{$\mathcal{D}/\Theta$}}U^{\theta}
by Proposition
3.B.2
.
∎

The following proposition gives explicit expressions for the quadratic characteristics that
appear in Rebolledo’s CLT in the special case where the local martingales are given as stochastic integrals with respect to a compensated counting processes.

Proposition 3.C.4
.

Let
N_{1},\ldots,N_{n}
be counting processes and assume that for each
j=1,\ldots,n
,

N_{j}
has an absolutely continuous
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{(n)}
-compensator
\Lambda_{j,t}
such that
M_{j,t}=N_{j,t}-\Lambda_{j,t}
is a locally square integrable
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{(n)}
-martingale.
Let
H_{1},\ldots,H_{n}
be locally bounded
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{(n)}
-predictable processes, and define the process
U_{t}^{(n)}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}H_{j,s}\mathrm{d}M_{j,s}
. Then
U_{t}^{(n)}
is a local square integrable
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{(n)}
-martingale, and for any
t,\epsilon>0
it holds that

\displaystyle\langle U^{(n)}\rangle(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}H_{j,s}^{2}\mathrm{d}\Lambda_{j,s},

\displaystyle\langle U_{\epsilon}^{(n)}\rangle(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}H_{j,s}^{2}\mathbbl{1}(|H_{j,s}|\geq\epsilon)\mathrm{d}\Lambda_{j,s}.

Proof.

See the discussion following Theorem
\mathrm{II}.5.2
in
Andersen et al. (1993)
, in particular equations
(2.5.6)
and
(2.5.8)
.
∎

3.D. Estimation of
\lambda
and
G

The asymptotic theory for estimation of the LCM crucially relies on
\widehat{\lambda}^{(n)}
and
\widehat{G}^{(n)}
being consistent,
and more importantly, having a product error decaying at an
n^{-1/2}
-rate.
Therefore, a central question when applying the test, is how to model
\lambda
and
G
.

In principle, we could use parametric models to learn
\widehat{\lambda}^{(n)}
and
\widehat{G}^{(n)}
, and under such models it should be possible to achieve
n^{-1/2}
-rates. For example, if we consider a parametrization
(t,\theta)\mapsto\lambda_{t}(\theta)
which is
\kappa(t)
-Lipschitz in
\theta\in\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}
for each
t
, then

\displaystyle h(n)^{2}=E\left(\int_{0}^{1}(\lambda_{t}(\theta_{0})-\lambda_{t}(\widehat{\theta}^{(n)}))^{2}\mathrm{d}t\right)\leq\|\kappa\|_{L_{2}([0,1])}^{2}E\|\theta_{0}-\widehat{\theta}^{(n)}\|_{\mathbb{R}^{p}}^{2}.

Thus the rates from parametric asymptotic theory can be converted to rates for
g
and
h
.

However, it is of greater interest if sufficient rates can be achieved with
nonparametric estimators. Below we give
concrete examples of nonparametric models and discuss which rates are
achievable. For simplicity, we focus on the case where
\mathcal{F}_{t}=\mathcal{F}_{t}^{N,Z}
and where
G_{t}=X_{t}-\Pi_{t}
as in the introductory example.

3.D.1
Nonparametric functional estimation of
\Pi

As seen in Section
3.6.1
, assumptions on the form of
\Pi
turn the
general estimation problem into a concrete problem of estimating a function.

If the system is Markovian, it can be reasonable to assume a

functional concurrent model
. The model asserts that
\Pi_{t}=\mu(t,Z_{t})

for a bivariate function
\mu
, and a survey of methods for estimating
\mu
is
given by
Maity (2017)
. Notably,
Jiang and Wang (2011)

achieve an
n^{-1/3}
-rate of
g(n)
under certain regularity and moment
assumptions, see their Theorem 3.3. That result also holds if
Z
is
replaced by a linear predictor
\beta^{T}\mathbf{Z}
of several covariates.

Consider again the historical linear regression model from Section
3.6.1
,
and assume that the effect of
Z
on
X
is homogeneous over time.
That is,
\rho_{X}(s,t)=\tilde{\rho}_{X}(t-s)
for some function
\tilde{\rho}_{X}
.
This submodel is known as the

functional convolution model
, since
\Pi
can be written as the
convolution of
Z
and
\tilde{\rho}_{X}
. Applying the Fourier transform converts
it into a (complex) linear concurrent model, so by Plancherel’s theorem one can leverage the convergence rates from the concurrent model.
Manrique (2016, Theorem
16)
uses this idea to transfer the
n^{-1/4}
-rate of the

functional ridge regression estimator

(
