\mathbb{E}[Y^{2}\,|\,\mathbf{W},T]\leq C
.

(iii)

There exists
\delta>0
such that
\mathbb{E}\big[|Y|^{2+\delta}\big]<\infty
.

(iv)

It holds that
\mathcal{E}_{1,t}^{(n)}\coloneq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}(\widehat{m}_{\hat{\theta}}(t\,|\,\mathbf{W}_{i})-m_{\hat{\theta}}(t\,|\,\mathbf{W}_{i}))^{2}\xrightarrow{P}0
.

(v)

It holds that
\mathcal{E}_{2,t}^{(n)}\coloneq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}(\widehat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}(t,\mathbf{W}_{i})-g_{\hat{\theta}}(t,\mathbf{W}_{i}))^{2}\xrightarrow{P}0
.

(vi)

It holds that
|\mathcal{I}_{3}|\cdot\mathcal{E}_{1,t}^{(n)}\mathcal{E}_{2,t}^{(n)}\xrightarrow{P}0
.

Classical convergence results of the AIPW are proven under similar conditions, but with
(vi)
replaced by the stronger convergence in (
4.4.11
).
We establish conditional asymptotic results under conditions on the conditional errors
\mathcal{E}_{1,t}^{(n)}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2,t}^{(n)}
.
To the best of our knowledge, the most similar results that we are aware of are those of
Benkeser et al. (2020)
, and our proof techniques are most similar to those of
Chernozhukov et al. (2018)
;
Lundborg and Pfister (2023)
.

We can now state our first asymptotic result for the DOPE.

Theorem 4.4.1
.

Under Assumptions
4.4.1
and
4.4.2
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}_{t}(\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\theta}})^{-1/2}\cdot U_{\hat{\theta}}^{(n)}\xrightarrow{d}\mathrm{N}(0,1),\qquad\text{and}\qquad R_{i}\xrightarrow{P}0,\quad i=1,2,3,

as
n\to\infty
. As a consequence,

\displaystyle\sqrt{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\cdot\mathbb{V}_{t}(\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\theta}})^{-1/2}\left(\widehat{\chi}_{t}^{\mathrm{dope}}-\chi_{t}(\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\theta}})\right)\xrightarrow{d}\mathrm{N}(0,1).

In other words, we can expect the DOPE to have an asymptotic distribution, conditionally on
\hat{\theta}
, approximated by

\widehat{\chi}_{t}^{\mathrm{dope}}(\widehat{g}_{\hat{\theta}},\widehat{m}_{\hat{\theta}})\,|\,\hat{\theta}\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle as.}}{{\sim}}\quad\mathrm{N}\Big(\chi_{t}(\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\theta}}),\,\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\mathbb{V}_{t}(\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\theta}})\Big).

Note that if
|\mathcal{I}_{3}|=\lfloor n/3\rfloor
, say, then the asymptotic variance is
\frac{3}{n}\mathbb{V}_{t}(\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\theta}})
. Our simulation study indicates that the asymptotic approximation may be valid in some cases without the use of sample splitting, in which case
|\mathcal{I}_{3}|=n
and the asymptotic variance is
\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{V}_{t}(\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\theta}})
. A direct implementation of the sample splitting procedure thus comes with an efficiency cost. In the supplementary Section
4
we discuss how the cross-fitting procedure makes use of sample splitting without an efficiency cost.

Given nuisance estimates
(\widehat{g},\widehat{m})

we consider the empirical variance estimator given by

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{t}(\widehat{g},\widehat{m})=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}\widehat{u}_{i}^{2}-\Big(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}\widehat{u}_{i}\Big)^{2}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}\Big(\widehat{u}_{i}-\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}\widehat{u}_{j}\Big)^{2},

(4.4.17)

where

\displaystyle\widehat{u}_{i}=\widehat{u}_{i}(\widehat{g},\widehat{m})=\widehat{g}(t,\mathbf{W}_{i})+\frac{\mathbbl{1}(T=t)(Y_{i}-\widehat{g}(t,\mathbf{W}_{i}))}{\widehat{m}(t\,|\,\mathbf{W}_{i})}.

The following theorem states that the variance estimator with nuisance functions
(\widehat{g}_{\hat{\theta}},\widehat{m}_{\hat{\theta}})
is consistent for the asymptotic variance in Theorem
4.4.1
.

Theorem 4.4.2
.

Under Assumptions
4.4.1
and
4.4.2
, it holds that

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{t}(\widehat{g}_{\hat{\theta}},\widehat{m}_{\hat{\theta}})-\mathbb{V}_{t}(\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\theta}})\xrightarrow{P}0

as
n\to\infty
.

4.4.2.2
Asymptotics of representation induced error

We now turn to the discussion of the second term in (
4.4.15
), i.e., the difference between the adjusted mean for the estimated representation
\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\theta}}
and the adjusted mean for the full covariate
\mathbf{W}
. Under sufficient regularity, the delta method
(
van der Vaart, 2000
, Thm. 3.8)
describes the distribution of this error:

Proposition 4.4.3
.

Assume
(T,\mathbf{W},Y)\sim P
satisfies the model (
4.4.12
) with
\theta_{P}\in\Theta\subseteq\real^{p}
. Let
u\colon\Theta\to\real
be the function given by
u(\theta)=\chi_{t}(\varphi(\theta,\mathbf{W});P)
and assume that
u
is differentiable in
\theta_{P}
. Suppose that
\hat{\theta}
is an estimator of
\theta_{P}
with rate
r_{n}
such that

\displaystyle r_{n}\cdot(\hat{\theta}-\theta_{P})\xrightarrow{d}\mathrm{N}(0,\Sigma).

Then

\displaystyle r_{n}\cdot(\chi_{t}(\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\theta}})-\chi_{t})\xrightarrow{d}\mathrm{N}(0,\nabla u(\theta_{P})^{\top}\Sigma\nabla u(\theta_{P}))

as
n\to\infty
.

The delta method requires that the adjusted mean,
\chi_{t}=\chi_{t}(\varphi(\theta,\mathbf{W});P)
, is differentiable with respect to
\theta\in\Theta
. The theorem below showcases that this is the case for the single-index model in Example
4
.

Theorem 4.4.4
.

Let
(T,\mathbf{W},Y)\sim P
be given by the single-index model in Example
4
with
h_{t}(\cdot)\coloneq h(t,\cdot)\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R})
. Assume that
\mathbf{W}
has a distribution with density
p_{\mathbf{W}}
with respect to Lebesgue measure on
d
and that
p_{\mathbf{W}}
is continuous almost everywhere with bounded support. Assume also that the propensity
m(t\mid\mathbf{w})=\mathbb{P}(T=t\,|\,\mathbf{W}=\mathbf{w})
is continuous in
\mathbf{w}
.

Then
u\colon\real^{d}\to\real
, defined by
u(\theta)=\chi_{t}(\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta;P)
, is differentiable at
\theta=\theta_{P}
with

\displaystyle\nabla u(\theta_{P})=\mathbb{E}_{P}\Big[h_{t}^{\prime}(\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta_{P})\Big(1-\frac{\mathbb{P}(T=t\,|\,\mathbf{W})}{\mathbb{P}(T=t\,|\,\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta_{P})}\Big)\mathbf{W}\Big].

The theorem is stated with some restrictive assumptions that simplify the proof, but these are likely not necessary.
It should also be possible to extend this result to more general models of the form (
4.4.12
), but we leave such generalizations for future work. In fact, the proof technique of Theorem
4.4.4
has already found application in
Gnecco et al. (2023)
in a different context.

The convergences implied in Theorem
4.4.1
and Proposition
4.4.3
, with
r_{n}=\sqrt{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}
, suggest that the MSE of the DOPE is of order

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(\widehat{\chi}_{t}^{\mathrm{dope}}-\chi_{t})^{2}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[(\widehat{\chi}_{t}^{\mathrm{dope}}-\chi_{t}(\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\theta}}))^{2}]+\mathbb{E}[(\chi_{t}(\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\theta}})-\chi_{t})^{2}]

\displaystyle\qquad+2\,\mathbb{E}[(\widehat{\chi}_{t}^{\mathrm{dope}}-\chi_{t}(\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\theta}}))(\chi_{t}(\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\theta}})-\chi_{t})]

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\Big(\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{V}_{t}(\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\theta}})]+\nabla u(\theta_{P})^{\top}\Sigma\nabla u(\theta_{P})\Big)+o\left(|\mathcal{I}_{3}|^{-1}\right)

(4.4.18)

The informal approximation ‘
\approx
’ can be turned into an equality by establishing (or simply assuming) uniform integrability, which enables the distributional convergences to be lifted to convergences of moments.

Remark 7
.

The expression in (
4.4.18
) suggests an approximate confidence interval of the form

\widehat{\chi}_{t}^{\mathrm{dope}}\pm\frac{z_{1-\alpha}}{\sqrt{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}}(\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{t}+\widehat{\nabla}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}\widehat{\nabla}),

where
\widehat{\Sigma}
is a consistent estimator of the asymptotic variance of
\sqrt{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}(\hat{\theta}-\theta_{P})
, and where
\widehat{\nabla}
is a consistent estimator of
\nabla_{\theta}\chi_{t}(\varphi(\theta,\mathbf{W}))|_{\theta=\theta_{P}}
. However, the requirement of constructing both
\widehat{\Sigma}
and
\widehat{\nabla}
adds further to the complexity of the methodology, so it might be preferable to rely on bootstrapping techniques in practice. In the simulation study we return to the question of inference and examine the coverage of a naive interval that does not include the term
\widehat{\nabla}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}\widehat{\nabla}
.

4.5
Experiments

In this section, we showcase the performance of the DOPE on simulated and real data.
All code is publicly available on
GitHub
7
7

7

https://github.com/AlexanderChristgau/OutcomeAdaptedAdjustment
.

4.5.1
Simulation study

We present results of a simulation study based on the single-index model from Example
4
. We demonstrate the performance of the DOPE from
Algorithms
1
and
2
, and compare with various alternative estimators.

4.5.1.1
Sampling scheme

We simulated datasets consisting of
n
i.i.d. copies of
(T,\mathbf{W},Y)
sampled according to the following scheme:

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}=(W^{1},\ldots,W^{d})

\displaystyle\sim\mathrm{Unif}(0,1)^{\otimes d}

\displaystyle T\,|\,\mathbf{W}

\displaystyle\sim\mathrm{Bern}(0.01+0.98\cdot\mathbbl{1}(W^{1}>0.5))

\displaystyle Y\,|\,T,\mathbf{W},\beta_{Y}

\displaystyle\sim\mathrm{N}(h(T,\mathbf{W}^{\top}\beta_{Y}),1)

(4.5.19)

where
\beta_{Y}
is sampled once for each dataset with

\displaystyle\beta_{Y}

\displaystyle=(1,\tilde{\beta}_{Y}),\qquad\tilde{\beta}_{Y}\sim\mathrm{N}(0,\mathbf{I}_{d-1}),

and where
n,d
, and
h
are experimental parameters.
The settings considered were
n\in\{300,900,2700\}
,
d\in\{4,12,36\}
, and with
h
being one of

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ll}h_{\text{lin}}(t,z)=t+3z,&\quad h_{\text{square}}(t,z)=z^{1+t},\\
h_{\text{cbrt}}(t,z)=(2+t)z^{1/3},&\quad h_{\text{sin}}(t,z)=(3+t)\sin(\pi z).\end{array}

For each setting,
N=300
datasets were simulated.

Note that while
\mathbb{E}[T]=0.01+0.98\cdot\mathbb{P}(W^{1}>0.5)=0.5
, the propensity score
m(t\mid\mathbf{w})=0.01+0.98\cdot\mathbbl{1}(w^{1}>0.5)
takes the rather extreme values
\{0.01,0.99\}
. Even though it technically satisfies (strict) positivity, these extreme values of the propensity makes the adjustment for
\mathbf{W}
a challenging task. For each dataset, the adjusted mean
\chi_{1}
(conditional on
\beta_{Y}
) was considered as the target parameter, and the ground truth was numerically computed as the sample mean of
10^{7}
observations of
h(1,\mathbf{W}^{\top}\beta_{Y})
.

4.5.1.2
Simulation estimators

This section contains an overview of the estimators used in the simulation. For a complete description see Section
4
in the supplementary material.

Two settings were considered for outcome regression (OR):

•

Linear
: Ordinary Least Squares (OLS).

•

Neural network
: A feedforward neural network with two hidden layers: a linear layer with one neuron, followed by a fully connected ReLU-layer with 100 neurons.
The first layer is a linear bottleneck that enforces the single-index model, and we denote the weights by
\theta\in\real^{d}
. An illustration of the architecture can be found in the supplementary Section
4
.
For a further discussion of leaning single-and multiple-index models with neural networks, see
Parkinson et al. (2023)
and references therein.

For propensity score estimation, logistic regression was used across all settings. A ReLU-network with one hidden layer with 100 neurons was also considered for estimation of the propensity score, but it did not enhance the performance of the resulting ATE estimators in this setting. Random forests and other methods were also initially used for both outcome regression and propensity score estimation. They were subsequently excluded because they did not seem to yield any noteworthy insights beyond what was observed for the methods above.

For each outcome regression, two implementations were explored: a stratified regression where
Y
is regressed onto
\mathbf{W}
separately for each stratum
T=1
and
T=2
, and a joint regression where
Y
is regressed onto
(T,\mathbf{W})
simultaneously. In the case of joint regression, the neural network architecture represents the regression function as a single index model, given by
Y=h(\alpha T+\theta^{\top}\mathbf{W})+\varepsilon_{Y}
. This representation differs from the description of the regression function specified in the sample scheme (
4.5.19
), which allows for a more complex interaction between treatment and the index of the covariates. In this sense, the joint regression is misspecified.

Based on these methods for nuisance estimation, we considered the following estimators of the adjusted mean:

•

The regression estimator

\widehat{\chi}_{1}^{\mathrm{reg}}\coloneq\mathbb{P}_{n}[\widehat{g}(1,\mathbf{W})]
.

•

The AIPW estimator given in Equation (
4.4.10
).

•

The DOPE from Algorithm
1
,
where
\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\theta}}=\mathbf{W}^{\top}\hat{\theta}
and where

\hat{\theta}
contains the weights of the first layer of the neural network designed for single-index regression.
We refer to this estimator as the DOPE-IDX.

•

The DOPE-BCL described in Remark
6
, where the propensity score is based on the final outcome regression.
See also
Benkeser et al. (2020, App. D)
.

We considered two versions of each DOPE estimator: one without sample splitting and another using 4-fold cross-fitting
8
8

8

consistent with Rem. 3.1. in
Chernozhukov et al. (2018)
, which recommends using 4 or 5 folds for cross-fitting.
. For the latter, the final empirical mean is calculated based on the hold-out fold, while the nuisance parameters are fitted using the remaining three folds. For each fold
k=1,\ldots,4
, this means employing Algorithm
1
with
\mathcal{I}_{1}=\mathcal{I}_{2}=[n]\setminus J_{k}
and
\mathcal{I}_{3}=J_{k}
. The two versions showed comparable performance for larger samples. In this section we only present the results for the DOPE without sample splitting, which performed better overall in our simulations. However, a comparison with the cross-fitted version can be found in Section
4
in the supplement.

Numerical results for the IPW estimator

\widehat{\chi}_{1}^{\mathrm{ipw}}\coloneq\mathbb{P}_{n}[\mathbbl{1}(T=t)Y/\widehat{m}(1\,|\,\mathbf{W})]

were also gathered. It performed poorly in our settings, and we have omitted the results for the sake of clarity in the presentation.

4.5.1.3
Empirical performance of estimators

Figure 4.5.3
:
Root mean square errors for various estimators of
\chi_{1}
plotted against sample size. Each data point is an average over 900 datasets, 300 for each choice of
d\in\{4,12,36\}
. The bands around each line correspond to
\pm 1
standard error.
For this plot, the outcome regression (OR) was fitted separately for each stratum
T=1
and
T=2
.

The results for stratified outcome regression are shown in Figure
4.5.3
. Each panel, corresponding to a link function in (
4.5.1.1
), displays the RMSE for various estimators against sample size. Across different values of
d\in\{4,12,36\}
, the results remain consistent, and thus the RMSE is averaged over the
3\times 300
datasets with varying
d
. In the upper left panel, where the link is linear, the regression estimator
\widehat{\chi}_{1}^{\mathrm{reg}}
with OLS performs best as expected. The remaining estimators exhibit similar performance, except for AIPW, which consistently performs poorly across all settings. This can be attributed to extreme values of the propensity score, resulting in a large efficiency bound for AIPW. All estimators seem to maintain an approximate
\sqrt{n}
-consistent RMSE for the linear link as anticipated.

For the nonlinear links, we observe that the OLS-based regression estimators perform poorly and do not exhibit approximate
\sqrt{n}
-consistency.
For the sine link, the RMSEs for all OLS-based estimators are large, and they are not shown for ease of visualization.
For neural network outcome regression, the AIPW still performs the worst across all nonlinear links.
The regression estimator and the DOPE estimators share similar performance when used with the neural network, with DOPE-IDX being more accurate overall. Since the neural network architecture is tailored to the single-index model, it is not surprising that the outcome regression works well, and as a result there is less need for debiasing. On the other hand, the debiasing introduced in the DOPE does not hurt the accuracy, and in fact, improves it in this setting.

Figure 4.5.4
:
Root mean square errors for various estimators of
\chi_{1}
plotted against sample size. Each data point is an average over 900 datasets, 300 for each choice of
d\in\{4,12,36\}
. The bands around each line correspond to
\pm 1
standard error.
For this plot, the outcome regression (OR) was fitted jointly onto
(T,\mathbf{W})
.

The results for joint regression of
Y
on
(T,\mathbf{W})
are shown in Figure
4.5.4
.
The results for the OLS-based estimators provide similar insights as previously discussed, so we focus on the results for the neural network based estimators. The jointly trained neural network is, in a sense, misspecified for the single-index model (except for the linear link), as discussed in Section
4.5.1.2
.
Thus it is not surprising that the regression estimator fails to adjust effectively for larger sample sizes. What is somewhat unexpected, however, is that the precision of DOPE, especially the DOPE-IDX, does not appear to be compromised by the misspecified outcome regression. In fact, the DOPE-IDX even seems to perform better with joint outcome regression.
We suspect that this could be attributed to the joint regression producing more robust predictions for the rare treated subjects with
W^{1}\leq 0.5
, for which
m(1\,|\,\mathbf{W})=0.01
. The predictions are more robust since the joint regression can leverage some of the information from the many untreated subjects with
W^{1}\leq 0.5
at the cost of introducing systematic bias, which the DOPE-IDX deals with in the debiasing step.
While this phenomenon is interesting, a thorough exploration of its exact details, both numerically and theoretically, is a task we believe is better suited for future research.

In summary, the DOPE serves as a middle ground between the regression estimator and the AIPW. It provides an additional safeguard against biased outcome regression, all while avoiding the potential numerical instability entailed by using standard inverse propensity weights.

4.5.1.4
Inference

We now consider approximate confidence intervals obtained from the empirical variance estimator
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}
defined in (
4.4.17
).
Specifically, we consider intervals of the form
\widehat{\chi}_{1}\pm\frac{z_{0.975}}{\sqrt{n}}\widehat{\mathcal{V}}^{-1/2}
, where
z_{0.975}
is the
0.975
quantile of the standard normal distribution.

Figure 4.5.5
:
Coverage of asymptotic confidence intervals of the adjusted mean
\chi_{1}
, aggregated over
d\in\{4,12,36\}
and with the true
\beta
given in (
4.5.19
).

Figure
4.5.5
shows the empirical coverage of
\chi_{1}
for the AIPW, DOPE-BCL and DOPE-IDX, all based on neural network outcome regression fitted jointly. Based on the number of repetitions, we can expect the estimated coverage probabilities to be accurate up to
\pm 0.01
after rounding.

The left column shows the results for no sample splitting, whereas the right column shows the results for 4-fold cross-fitting. For all estimators, cross-fitting seems to improve coverage slightly for large sample sizes. The AIPW is a little anti-conservative, especially for the links
h_{\text{sin}}
and
h_{\text{cbrt}}
, but overall maintains approximate coverage.
The slight miscoverage is to be expected given the challenging nature with the extreme propensity weights.
For the DOPE-BCL and DOPE-IDX, the coverage probabilities are worse, but with the DOPE-IDX having better coverage overall. According to our asymptotic analysis, specifically Theorem
4.4.1
, the DOPE-BCL and DOPE-IDX intervals
will only achieve asymptotic
95\%
coverage of the
random
parameters

\chi_{1}(\widehat{h}(t,\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\theta}}))
and
\chi_{1}(\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\theta}})
, respectively. Thus we do not anticipate these intervals to asymptotically achieve the nominal
95\%
coverage of
\chi_{1}
.
However, we show in Figure
4.B.3
in the supplementary Section
4
that the average widths of the confidence intervals are significantly smaller for the DOPE intervals than the AIPW interval. Hence it is possible that the DOPE intervals can be corrected – as we have also discussed in Remark
7
– to gain advantage over the AIPW interval, but we leave such explorations for future work.

In summary, the lack of full coverage for the naively constructed intervals are consistent with the asymptotic analysis conditionally on a representation
\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\theta}}
.
In view of this, our asymptotic results might be more realistic and pragmatic than the previously studied regime
(
Benkeser et al., 2020
;
Ju et al., 2020
)
, which assumes fast unconditional rates of the nuisance parameters

(\widehat{m}_{\hat{\theta}},\widehat{g}_{\hat{\theta}})
.

4.5.2
Application to NHANES data

We consider the mortality dataset collected by the
National Health and Nutrition Examination Survey I Epidemiologic Followup Study
(
Cox et al., 1997
)
, henceforth referred to as the NHANES dataset.
The dataset was initially collected as in
Lundberg et al. (2020)
9
9

9

See
https://github.com/suinleelab/treeexplainer-study

for their GitHub repository.

.
The dataset contains several baseline covariates, and our outcome variable is the indicator of death at the end of study.
To deal with missing values, we considered both a complete mean imputation as in
Lundberg et al. (2020)
, or a trimmed dataset where covariates with more than
50\%
of their values missing are dropped and the rest are mean imputed.
The latter approach reduces the number of covariates from
79
to
65
.
The final results were similar for the two imputation methods, so we only report the results for the mean imputed dataset here. In the supplementary Section
4
we show the results for the trimmed dataset.

The primary aim of this section is to evaluate the different estimation methodologies on a realistic and real data example. For this purpose we consider a treatment variable based on
pulse pressure
and study its effect on mortality. Pulse pressure is defined as the difference between systolic and diastolic blood pressure (BP).
High pulse pressure is not only used as an indicator of disease but is also reported to increase the risk of cardiovascular diseases
(
Franklin et al., 1999
)
. We investigate the added effect of high pulse pressure when adjusting for other baseline covariates, in particular systolic BP and levels of white blood cells, hemoglobin, hematocrit, and platelets.
We do not adjust for diastolic BP, as it determines the pulse pressure when combined with systolic BP, which would therefore lead to a violation of positivity.

Figure 4.5.6
:
Distribution of estimated propensity scores based on the full covariate set
\mathbf{W}
, the single-index
\mathbf{W}^{\top}\hat{\theta}
, and the outcome predictions
\widehat{g}(1,\mathbf{W})
, respectively.

A pulse pressure of 40 mmHg is considered normal, and as the pressure increases past 50 mmHg, the risk of cardiovascular diseases is reported to increase.
Some studies have used 60 mmHg as the threshold for high pulse pressure
(
Homan et al., 2024
)
, and thus we consider the following treatment variable corresponding to high pulse pressure:

\displaystyle T=\mathbbl{1}\left(\textrm{pulse pressure}>60\textrm{ mmHg}\right).

For the binary outcome regression we consider logistic regression and a variant of the neural network from
Section
4.5.1.2
with an additional ‘sigmoid-activation’ on the output layer.
Based on 5-fold cross-validation, logistic regression yields a log-loss of
0.50
, whereas the neural network yields a log-loss of
0.48
(smaller is better).

Figure
4.5.6
shows the distribution of the estimated propensity scores used in the AIPW, DOPE-IDX, and DOPE-BCL, based on neural network regression. As expected, we see that the full covariate set yields propensity scores that essentially violate positivity, with this effect being less pronounced for DOPE-IDX and even less so for DOPE-BCL, where the scores are comparatively less extreme.

Table 4.5.1
:
Adjusted effect estimates of unhealthy pulse pressure on mortality based on NHANES dataset. Estimators are sorted in increasing order after bootstrap (BS) standard error.

Table
4.5.1
shows the estimated treatment effects

\widehat{\Delta}=\widehat{\chi}_{1}-\widehat{\chi}_{0}
for various estimators together with
95\%
bootstrap confidence intervals. The estimators are sorted according to bootstrap variance based on
B=1000
bootstraps.
Because we do not know the true effect, we cannot directly decide which estimator is best.
The naive contrast provides a substantially larger estimate than all of the adjustment estimators, which indicates that the added effect of high pulse pressure on mortality when adjusting is different from the unadjusted effect.
The IPW estimator,
\widehat{\chi}_{1}^{\mathrm{ipw}}
, is the only estimator to yield a negative estimate of the adjusted effect, and its bootstrap variance is also significantly larger than the other estimators. Thus it is plausible, that the IPW estimator fails to adjust appropriately. This is not surprising given extreme distribution of the propensity weights, as shown in blue in Figure
4.5.6
.

The remaining estimators yield comparable estimates of the adjusted effect, with the logistic regression based estimators having a marginally larger estimates than their neural network counterparts. The (bootstrap) standard errors are comparable for the DOPE and regression estimators, but the AIPW estimators have slightly larger standard errors. As a result, the added effect of high pulse pressure on mortality cannot be considered statistically significant for the AIPW estimators, whereas it can for the other estimators.

In summary, the results indicate that the choice treatment effect estimator impacts the final estimate and confidence interval. While it is uncertain which estimator is superior for this particular application, the DOPE estimators seem to offer a reasonable and stable estimate.

4.6
Discussion

In this paper, we address challenges posed by complex data with unknown underlying structure, an increasingly common scenario in observational studies. Specifically, we have formulated a refined and general formalism for studying efficiency of covariate adjustment. This formalism extends and builds upon the efficiency principles derived from causal graphical models, in particular results provided by
Rotnitzky and Smucler (2020)
. Our theoretical framework has led to the identification of the optimal covariate information for adjustment.
From this theoretical groundwork, we introduced the DOPE for general estimation of the ATE with increased efficiency.

Several key areas within this research merit further investigation:

Extension to other targets and causal effects
: Our focus has predominantly been on the adjusted mean
\chi_{t}=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[Y\,|\,T=t,\mathbf{W}]]
. However, the extension of our methodologies to continuous treatments, instrumental variables, or other causal effects, such as the average treatment effect among the treated, is an area that warrants in-depth exploration. We suspect that many of the fundamental ideas can be modified to prove analogous results for such target parameters.

Beyond neural networks
: While our examples and simulation study has applied the DOPE with neural networks, its compatibility with other regression methods such as kernel regression and gradient boosting opens new avenues for alternative adjustment estimators. Investigating such estimators can provide practical insights and broaden the applicability of our approach.
The Outcome Highly Adapted Lasso
(
Ju et al., 2020
)
is a similar method in this direction that leverages the Highly Adaptive Lasso
(
Benkeser and van der Laan, 2016
)
for robust treatment effect estimation.

Causal representation learning integration
: Another possible direction for future research is the integration of our efficiency analysis into the broader concept of causal representation learning
(
Schölkopf et al., 2021
)
. This line of research, which is not concerned with specific downstream tasks, could potentially benefit from some of the insights of our efficiency analysis and the DOPE framework.

Implications of sample splitting
: Our current asymptotic analysis relies on the implementation of three distinct sample splits. Yet, our simulation results suggest that sample splitting might not be necessary. Using the notation of Algorithm
1
, we hypothesize that the independence assumption
\mathcal{I}_{3}\cap(\mathcal{I}_{1}\cup\mathcal{I}_{2})=\emptyset
could be replaced with Donsker class conditions. However, the theoretical justifications for setting
\mathcal{I}_{1}
identical to
\mathcal{I}_{2}
are not as evident, thus meriting additional exploration into this potential simplification.

Valid and efficient confidence intervals
:
The naive confidence intervals explored in the simulation study, cf.
Section
4.5.1.4
, do not provide an adequate coverage of the unconditional adjusted mean. As indicated in Remark
7
, it would be interesting to explore methods for correcting the intervals, for example by: (i) adding a bias correction, (ii) constructing a consistent estimator of the unconditional variance, or (iii) using bootstrapping. Appealing to bootstrapping techniques, however, might be computationally expensive, in particular when combined with cross-fitting. To implement (i), one approach could be to generalize
Theorem
4.4.4
to establish general differentiability of the adjusted mean with respect to general representations, and then construct an estimator of the gradient. However, one must asses whether this bias correction could negate the efficiency benefits of the DOPE.

Supplement to ‘Efficient adjustment for complex covariates: Gaining efficiency with DOPE’

The supplementary material is organized as follows:
Section
4
contains proofs of the results in the main text and a few auxiliary results;
Section
4
contains details on the experiments in Section
4.5
;
and Section
4
contains a description of the cross-fitting algorithm.

4.A. Auxiliary results and proofs

The proposition below relaxes the criteria for being a valid adjustment set in a causal graphical model. It follows from the results of
Perković et al. (2018)
, which was pointed out to the authors by Leonard Henckel, and is stated here for completeness.

Proposition 4.A.1
.

Let
T,\mathbf{Z}
, and
Y
be pairwise disjoint node sets in a DAG
\mathcal{D}=(\mathbf{V},\mathbf{E})
, and let
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{D})
denote the collection of continuous distributions that are Markovian with respect to
\mathcal{D}
and with
\mathbb{E}|Y|<\infty
. Then
\mathbf{Z}
is an adjustment set relative to
(T,Y)
if and only if for all
P\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{D})
and all
t

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y\,|\,\mathrm{do}(T=t)]=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\frac{\mathbbl{1}(T=t)Y}{\mathbb{P}_{P}(T=t\,|\,\mathrm{pa}_{\mathcal{D}}(T))}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y\,|\,T=t,\mathbf{Z}]]

Proof.

The ‘only if’ direction is straightforward if we use an alternative characterization of
\mathbf{Z}
being an adjustment set: for any density
p
of a distribution
P\in\mathcal{P}
it holds that

p(y\,|\,\mathrm{do}(T=t))=\int p(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{z},t)p(\mathbf{z})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{z},

where the ‘do-operator’ (and notation) is defined as in, e.g.,
Peters et al. (2017)
. Fubini’s theorem then yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y\,|\,\mathrm{do}(T=t)]

\displaystyle=\int_{\real{}^{l}}\mathbf{y}p(\mathbf{y}|do(\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{t}))\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}

\displaystyle=\int_{\real{}^{l}}\mathbf{y}\int_{\real{}^{k}}p(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{z},t)p(\mathbf{z})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{z}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}

\displaystyle=\int_{\real{}^{k}}\int_{\real{}^{l}}\mathbf{y}p(\mathbf{y}|\mathbf{z},t)\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}p(\mathbf{z})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{z}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathbb{E}_{P}[Y\,|\,T=t,\mathbf{Z}]],

which is equivalent to the ‘only if’ part. On the contrary, assume that
\mathbf{Z}
is
not
an adjustment set for
(T,Y)
. Then Theorem 56 and the proof of Theorem 57 in
Perković et al. (2018)
imply the existence of a Gaussian distribution
\tilde{P}\in\mathcal{P}
for which

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{P}}[Y\,|\,\mathrm{do}(T=1)]\neq\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{P}}[\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{P}}[Y\,|\,T=1,\mathbf{Z}]]

This implies the other direction.
∎

The following lemma is a generalization of Lemma 27 in
Rotnitzky and Smucler (2020)
.

Lemma 4.A.2
.

For any
\sigma
-algebras
\mathcal{Z}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{2}\subseteq\sigma(\mathbf{W})

it holds, under Assumption
4.1.1
, that

\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\pi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2};P)^{-1}\,|\,T=t,\mathcal{Z}_{1}\right]=\pi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1};P)^{-1}

for all
P\in\mathcal{P}
.

Proof.

Direct computation yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\pi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2};P)^{-1}\,|\,T=t,\mathcal{Z}_{1}\right]\mathbb{P}_{P}(T=t\,|\,\mathcal{Z}_{1})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\frac{\mathbbl{1}(T=t)}{\pi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2};P)}\,|\,\mathcal{Z}_{1}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P}[\mathbbl{1}(T=t)\,|\,\mathcal{Z}_{2}]}{\pi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2};P)}\,|\,\mathcal{Z}_{1}\right]=1.

∎

4.A.1
Proof of Lemma
4.3.1

Since
P\in\mathcal{P}
is fixed, we suppress it from the notation in the following computations.

From
Y\,\perp\!\!\!\perp\,\mathcal{Z}_{2}\,|\,\mathcal{Z}_{1},T
and
\mathcal{Z}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{2}
, it follows that
\mathbb{E}[Y\,|\,\mathcal{Z}_{1},T]=\mathbb{E}[Y\,|\,\mathcal{Z}_{2},T]
. Hence, for each
t\in\mathbb{T}
,

b_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1})=\mathbb{E}[Y\,|\,\mathcal{Z}_{1},T=t]=\mathbb{E}[Y\,|\,\mathcal{Z}_{2},T=t]=b_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2}).

(4.A.1)

Therefore
\chi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1})=\mathbb{E}[b_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1})]=\mathbb{E}[b_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})]=\chi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})
. If
\mathcal{Z}_{2}
is description of
\mathbf{W}
, this identity shows that
\mathcal{Z}_{2}
is
\mathcal{P}
-valid if and only if
\mathcal{Z}_{1}
is
\mathcal{P}
-valid.

To compare the asymptotic efficiency bounds for
\mathcal{Z}_{2}
with
\mathcal{Z}_{1}
we use the law of total variance. Note first that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\psi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})\,|\,T,Y,\mathcal{Z}_{1}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbbl{1}(T=t)(Y-b_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2}))\frac{1}{\pi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})}+b_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})-\chi_{t}\,|\,T,Y,\mathcal{Z}_{1}\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbbl{1}(T=t)(Y-b_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1}))\,\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{1}{\pi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})}\,|\,T=t,Y,\mathcal{Z}_{1}\Big]+b_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1})-\chi_{t}

\displaystyle=\mathbbl{1}(T=t)(Y-b_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1}))\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\pi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})}\,|\,T=t,\mathcal{Z}_{1}\right]+b_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1})-\chi_{t}

\displaystyle=\mathbbl{1}(T=t)(Y-b_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1}))\frac{1}{\pi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1})}+b_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1})-\chi_{t}=\psi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1}).

Second equality is due to
\mathcal{Z}_{1}
-measurability and (
4.A.1
), whereas the third equality follows from
Y\,\perp\!\!\!\perp\,\mathcal{Z}_{2}\,|\,\mathcal{Z}_{1},T
and the last inequality is due to Lemma
4.A.2
.
On the other hand,

\displaystyle\phantom{=}\mathbb{E}\big[\var(\psi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})\,|\,T,Y,\mathcal{Z}_{1})\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\var\Big(\mathbbl{1}(T=t)(Y-b_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1}))\frac{1}{\pi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})}+b_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1})-\chi_{t}(P)\;\big|\;T,Y,\mathcal{Z}_{1}\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbbl{1}(T=t)(Y-b_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1}))^{2}\var\Big(\frac{1}{\pi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})}\;\big|\;T=t,Y,\mathcal{Z}_{1}\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\pi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1})\var(Y\,|\,T=t,\mathcal{Z}_{1})\var\Big(\frac{1}{\pi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})}\;\big|\;T=t,\mathcal{Z}_{1}\Big)\Big].

Combined we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})

\displaystyle=\var(\psi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2}))=\var(\mathbb{E}[\psi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})\,|\,T,Y,\mathcal{Z}_{1}])+\mathbb{E}[\var[\psi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})\,|\,T,Y,\mathcal{Z}_{1}]]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{V}_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1})+\mathbb{E}\Big[\pi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1})\var(Y\,|\,T=t,\mathcal{Z}_{1})\var\Big(\frac{1}{\pi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})}\Big|\;T=t,Y,\mathcal{Z}_{1}\Big)\Big].

To prove the last part of the lemma, we first note that
\psi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})
and
\psi_{t^{\prime}}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})
are conditionally uncorrelated for
t\neq t^{\prime}
. This follows from (
4.A.1
), from which the conditional covariance reduces to

\displaystyle\cov(\psi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2}),\psi_{t^{\prime}}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})\,|\,T,Y,\mathcal{Z}_{1})=\mathbbl{1}(T=t)\mathbbl{1}(T=t^{\prime})\cov(*,*\,|\,T,Y,\mathcal{Z}_{1})=0.

Thus, letting
\psi(\mathcal{Z}_{2})=(\psi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2}))_{t\in\mathbb{T}}
and using the computation for
\mathbb{V}_{t}
, we obtain that:

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}_{\Delta}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})=\var(\mathbf{c}^{\top}\psi(\mathcal{Z}_{2}))

\displaystyle=\var(\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{c}^{\top}\psi(\mathcal{Z}_{2})\,|\,T,Y,\mathcal{Z}_{1}])+\mathbb{E}[\var[\mathbf{c}^{\top}\psi(\mathcal{Z}_{2})\,|\,T,Y,\mathcal{Z}_{1}]]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{V}_{\Delta}(\mathcal{Z}_{1})+\sum_{t\in\mathbb{T}}c_{t}^{2}\,\mathbb{E}\Big[\pi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1})\var(Y\,|\,T=t,\mathcal{Z}_{1})\var\Big(\frac{1}{\pi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})}\Big|\;T=t,Y,\mathcal{Z}_{1}\Big)\Big].

This concludes the proof. ∎

4.A.2
Proof of Theorem
4.3.2

(i) Assume that
\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\sigma(\mathbf{W})
is a description of
\mathbf{W}
such that
\mathcal{Q}_{P}\subseteq\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{Z}\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu
. Observe that almost surely,

\mathbb{P}_{P}(Y\leq y\,|\,T,\mathbf{W})=\sum_{t\in\mathbb{T}}\mathbbl{1}(T=t)F(y\mid t,\mathbf{W};P)

is
\sigma(T,\mathcal{Q}_{P})
-measurable and hence also

\sigma(T,\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{Z}\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu)
-measurable.
It follows that
\mathbb{P}_{P}(Y\leq y\,|\,T,\mathbf{W})
is also a version of
\mathbb{P}_{P}(Y\leq y\,|\,T,\overline{\mathcal{Z}})
.
As a consequence,
\mathbb{P}_{P}(Y\leq y\,|\,T,\mathbf{W})=\mathbb{P}_{P}(Y\leq y\,|\,T,\mathcal{Z})
almost surely. From Doob’s characterization of conditional independence
(
Kallenberg, 2021
, Theorem 8.9)
, we conclude that
Y\,{\perp\!\!\!\perp}_{P}\,\mathbf{W}\mid T,\mathcal{Z}
, or equivalently that
\mathcal{Z}
is
P
-ODS. The ‘in particular’ follows from setting
\mathcal{Z}=\mathcal{Q}_{P}
.

(ii) Assume that
\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\sigma(\mathbf{W})
is
P
-ODS. Using Doob’s characterization of conditional independence again,

\mathbb{P}_{P}(Y\leq y\,|\,T,\mathbf{W})=\mathbb{P}_{P}(Y\leq y\,|\,T,\mathcal{Z})

almost surely. Under Assumption
4.1.1
, this entails that

F(y\,|\,T=t,\mathbf{W};P)=\mathbb{P}_{P}(Y\leq y\,|\,T=t,\mathcal{Z})
almost surely, and hence
F(y\,|\,T=t,\mathbf{W};P)
must be
\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{Z}\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu
-measurable. As the generators of
\mathcal{Q}_{P}
are
\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{Z}\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu
-measurable, we conclude that
\mathcal{Q}_{P}\subseteq\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{Z}\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu
.

(iii) Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a
P
-ODS description.
From (i) and (ii) it follows that
\mathcal{Q}_{P}\subseteq\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{Z}\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu
, and since
\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\sigma(\mathbf{W})
it
also holds that
Y\,{\perp\!\!\!\perp}_{P}\,\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{Z}\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu\mid T,\mathcal{Q}_{P}
.
Since
\mathbb{V}_{\Delta}(\mathcal{Z};P)=\mathbb{V}_{\Delta}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{Z}\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu;P)
, Lemma
4.3.1
gives the desired conclusion.
∎

4.A.3
Proof of Corollary
4.3.3

Theorem
4.3.2
(i,ii) implies that

\mathcal{Z}
is
\mathcal{P}
-ODS if and only if
\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{Z}\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu^{P}
contains
\mathcal{Q}_{P}
for all
P\in\mathcal{P}
.

If
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{Z}\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu^{P}
for all
P\in\mathcal{P}
then
\mathcal{Q}_{P}\subseteq\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{Z}\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu^{P}

for all
P\in\mathcal{P}
by definition, and
\mathcal{Z}
is
\mathcal{P}
-ODS.
Equation (
4.3.9
) follows from the same argument as Theorem
4.3.2

(iii)
. That is,

\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{Z}\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu^{P}
, and since
\mathcal{Q}
is
P
-ODS and
\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\sigma(\mathbf{W})
we have
Y\,{\perp\!\!\!\perp}_{P}\,\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{Z}\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu^{P}\mid T,\mathcal{Q}
. Lemma
4.3.1
can be applied for each
P\in\mathcal{P}
to obtain the desired conclusion.
∎

4.A.4
Proof of Proposition
4.3.4

Since
\mathcal{R}_{P}\subseteq\mathcal{Q}_{P}
always holds, so it suffices to prove that
\mathcal{Q}_{P}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{P}
.
If
F(y\,|\,t,\mathbf{W};P)
is
\sigma(b_{t}(\mathbf{W};P))
-measurable, then
\mathcal{Q}_{P}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{P}
follows directly from definition.
If
Y
is a binary outcome, then the conditional distribution is determined by the conditional mean, i.e.,
F(y\,|\,t,\mathbf{W};P)
is
\sigma(b_{t}(\mathbf{W};P))
-measurable. If
Y=b_{T}(\mathbf{W})+\varepsilon_{Y}
with
\varepsilon_{Y}\,\perp\!\!\!\perp\,T,\mathbf{W}
,
then
F(y\,|\,t,\mathbf{w};P)=F_{P}(y-b_{t}(\mathbf{w}))
where
F_{P}
is the distribution function for the distribution of
\varepsilon_{Y}
. Again,
F(y\,|\,t,\mathbf{W};P)
becomes
\sigma(b_{t}(\mathbf{W};P))
-measurable.

To prove the last part, let
\mathcal{Z}
be a
P
-OMS description.
Since
b_{t}(\mathbf{W};P)=b_{t}(\mathcal{Z};P)
is
\mathcal{Z}
-measurable for every
t\in\mathbb{T}
, it follows that
\mathcal{R}_{P}\subseteq\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{Z}\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu
.
Hence the argument of Theorem
4.3.2
(iii) establishes
Equation (
4.3.8
) when
\mathcal{Q}_{P}=\mathcal{R}_{P}
.
∎

4.A.5
Proof of Lemma
4.3.5

Since
P\in\mathcal{P}
is fixed throughout this proof, we suppress it from notation. From
\mathcal{Z}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{2}\subseteq\sigma(\mathbf{W})
and
T\,{\perp\!\!\!\perp}_{P}\,\mathcal{Z}_{2}\,|\,\mathcal{Z}_{1}
, it follows that
\pi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1})=\pi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1})
, and hence

\chi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1})=\mathbb{E}[\pi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1})^{-1}\mathbbl{1}(T=t)Y]=\mathbb{E}[\pi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})^{-1}\mathbbl{1}(T=t)Y]=\chi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2}).

This establishes the first part. For the second part, we use that

\pi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1})=\pi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})
and
\chi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1})=\chi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})

to see that

\displaystyle\psi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1})-\psi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})

\displaystyle=b_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1})-b_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})+\frac{\mathbbl{1}(T=t)}{\pi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})}(b_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})-b_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1}))

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{\mathbbl{1}(T=t)}{\pi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})}-1\Big)(b_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})-b_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1}))\eqcolon R_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2}).

Since
\mathbb{E}[R_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2})\,|\,\mathcal{Z}_{2}]=0
we have that
\mathbb{E}[\psi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})R_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2})]=0
, which means that
\psi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})
and
R_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2})
are uncorrelated. Note that from
\pi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1})=\pi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})
it also follows that

\displaystyle b_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1})=\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{Y\mathbbl{1}(T=t)}{\pi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1})}\,|\,\mathcal{Z}_{1}\Big]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{Y\mathbbl{1}(T=t)}{\pi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})}\,|\,\mathcal{Z}_{2}\Big]\,|\,\mathcal{Z}_{1}\Big]=\mathbb{E}[b_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})\,|\,\mathcal{Z}_{1}]

This means that
\mathbb{E}[(b_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})-b_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1}))^{2}\,|\,\mathcal{Z}_{1}]=\var(b_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})\,|\,\mathcal{Z}_{1})
.
These observations let us compute that

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1})

\displaystyle=\var(\psi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1}))

\displaystyle=\var(\psi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2}))+\var\left(R_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2})\right)

\displaystyle=\var(\psi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2}))+\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[R_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2})^{2}\,|\,\mathcal{Z}_{2}]]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{V}_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})+\mathbb{E}\Big[(b_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})-b_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1}))^{2}\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{\mathbbl{1}(T=t)^{2}}{\pi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1})^{2}}-\frac{2\mathbbl{1}(T=t)}{\pi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1})}+1\,|\,\mathcal{Z}_{2}\Big]\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{V}_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})+\mathbb{E}\Big[\var(b_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})\,|\,\mathcal{Z}_{1})\Big(\frac{1}{\pi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1})}-1\Big)\Big].

(4.A.2)

This establishes the formula in the case
\Delta=\chi_{t}
.

For general
\Delta
, note first that
\mathbf{R}
and
(\psi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2}))_{t\in\mathbb{T}}
are uncorrelated since
\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{R}\,|\,\mathcal{Z}_{2}]=0
. Therefore we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}_{\!\Delta}(\mathcal{Z}_{1})

\displaystyle=\var\Big(\sum_{t\in\mathbb{T}}c_{t}\psi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1})\Big)

\displaystyle=\var\Big(\sum_{t\in\mathbb{T}}c_{t}\psi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})\Big)+\var\Big(\sum_{t\in\mathbb{T}}c_{t}R_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2})\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{V}_{\!\Delta}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})+\mathbf{c}^{\top}\var\Big(\mathbf{R}\Big)\mathbf{c}.

It now remains to establish the covariance expressions for the last term, since the expression of
\var\left(R_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2})\right)
was found in (
4.A.2
).
To this end, note first that for any
s,t\in\mathbb{T}
with
s\neq t
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\Big(\frac{\mathbbl{1}(T=s)}{\pi_{s}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})}-1\Big)\Big(\frac{\mathbbl{1}(T=t)}{\pi_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})}-1\Big)\,|\,\mathcal{Z}_{2}\right]=-1.

Thus we finally conclude that

\displaystyle\cov(R_{s},R_{t})

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}\left[(b_{s}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})-b_{s}(\mathcal{Z}_{1}))(b_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})-b_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{1}))\right]

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}\left[\cov(b_{s}(\mathcal{Z}_{2}),b_{t}(\mathcal{Z}_{2})\,|\,\mathcal{Z}_{1})\right],

as desired.
∎

4.A.6
Computations for Example
3

Using symmetry, we see that

\displaystyle\pi_{1}(\mathbf{Z})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[T\mid\mathbf{Z}]=\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{W}\mid\mathbf{Z}]

\displaystyle=(0.5-\mathbf{Z})\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{W}\leq 0.5\,|\,\mathbf{Z})+(0.5+\mathbf{Z})\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{W}>0.5\,|\,\mathbf{Z})\ =\frac{1}{2}.

From
T\,\perp\!\!\!\perp\,\mathbf{Z}
we observe that

\displaystyle b_{t}(\mathbf{W})

\displaystyle=t+\mathbb{E}[g(\mathbf{Z})\,|\,T=t,\mathbf{W}]=t+g(\mathbf{Z}),

\displaystyle b_{t}(\mathbf{Z})

\displaystyle=t+\mathbb{E}[g(\mathbf{Z})\,|\,T=t,\mathbf{Z}]=t+g(\mathbf{Z}),

\displaystyle b_{t}(0)

\displaystyle=t+\mathbb{E}[g(\mathbf{Z})\,|\,T=t]=t+\mathbb{E}[g(\mathbf{Z})].

Plugging these expressions into the asymptotic variance yields:

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}_{t}(0)

\displaystyle=\var\left(b_{t}(0)+\frac{\mathbbl{1}(T=t)}{\pi_{t}(0)}(Y-b_{t}(0))\right)

\displaystyle=\pi_{t}(0)^{-2}\var\left(\mathbbl{1}(T=t)(Y-b_{t}(0))\right)

\displaystyle=4\mathbb{E}[\mathbbl{1}(T=t)(g(\mathbf{Z})-\mathbb{E}[g(\mathbf{Z})]+v(\mathbf{W})\varepsilon_{Y})^{2}]

\displaystyle=4\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{W}(g(\mathbf{Z})-\mathbb{E}[g(\mathbf{Z})])^{2}]+2\mathbb{E}[v(\mathbf{W})\varepsilon_{Y}^{2}]

\displaystyle=2\var(g(\mathbf{Z}))+2\mathbb{E}[v(\mathbf{W})^{2}]\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{Y}^{2}]

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}_{t}(\mathbf{Z})

\displaystyle=\var\left(b_{t}(\mathbf{Z})+\frac{\mathbbl{1}(T=t)}{\pi_{t}(\mathbf{Z})}(Y-b_{t}(\mathbf{Z}))\right)

\displaystyle=\var\left(g(\mathbf{Z})+2\mathbbl{1}(T=t)v(\mathbf{W})\varepsilon_{Y}\right)

\displaystyle=\var\left(g(\mathbf{Z})\right)+2\mathbb{E}[v(\mathbf{W})^{2}]\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{Y}^{2}]

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}_{t}(\mathbf{W})

\displaystyle=\var\left(b_{t}(\mathbf{W})+\frac{\mathbbl{1}(T=t)}{\pi_{t}(\mathbf{W})}(Y-b_{t}(\mathbf{W}))\right)

\displaystyle=\var\left(g(\mathbf{Z})+\frac{\mathbbl{1}(T=t)v(\mathbf{W})\varepsilon_{Y}}{\mathbf{W}}\right)

\displaystyle=\var\left(g(\mathbf{Z})\right)+\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{v(\mathbf{W})^{2}}{\mathbf{W}}\right]\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{Y}^{2}].

∎

Proof of Theorem
4.4.1

We first prove that
R_{i}\xrightarrow{P}0
for
i=1,2,3
.
The third term is taken care of by combining Assumption
4.4.2

(i,iv)
with Cauchy-Schwarz:

\displaystyle|R_{3}|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\sqrt{\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}\big(\widehat{m}_{\hat{\theta}}(t\,|\,\mathbf{W}_{i})^{-1}-m_{\hat{\theta}}(t\,|\,\mathbf{W}_{i})^{-1}\big)^{2}}\sqrt{\mathcal{E}_{2,t}^{(n)}}

\displaystyle\leq\ell_{c}\sqrt{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|\mathcal{E}_{1,t}^{(n)}\mathcal{E}_{2,t}^{(n)}}\xrightarrow{P}0,

where
\ell_{c}>0
is the Lipschitz constant of
x\mapsto x^{-1}

on
[c,1-c]
.

For the first term,
R_{1}
, note that conditionally on
\hat{\theta}
and the observed estimated representations

\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\bullet}}\coloneq(\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\theta},i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}=(\varphi(\hat{\theta},\mathbf{W}_{i}))_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}
, the summands are conditionally i.i.d. due to Assumption
4.4.1
. They are also conditionally mean zero since

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[r_{i}^{1}\,|\,\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\bullet}},\hat{\theta}\,]

\displaystyle=\left(\widehat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}(t,\mathbf{W}_{i})-g_{\hat{\theta}}(t,\mathbf{W}_{i})\right)\Big(1-\frac{\mathbb{E}[\mathbbl{1}(T_{i}=t)\,|\,\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\bullet}},\hat{\theta}\,]}{m_{\hat{\theta}}(t\,|\,\mathbf{W}_{i})}\Big)

\displaystyle=\left(\widehat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}(t,\mathbf{W}_{i})-g_{\hat{\theta}}(t,\mathbf{W}_{i})\right)\Big(1-\frac{\mathbb{E}[\mathbbl{1}(T_{i}=t)\,|\,\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\theta},i},\hat{\theta}]}{m_{\hat{\theta}}(t\,|\,\mathbf{W}_{i})}\Big)=0,

for each
i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}
.
Using Assumption
4.4.2

(i)
, we can bound the conditional variance by

\displaystyle\var\left(r_{i}^{1}\,|\,\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\bullet}},\hat{\theta}\,\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\widehat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}(t,\mathbf{W}_{i})-g_{\hat{\theta}}(t,\mathbf{W}_{i})\right)^{2}}{m_{\hat{\theta}}(t\,|\,\mathbf{W})^{2}}\var\left(\mathbbl{1}(T=t)\,|\,\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\bullet}},\hat{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(\widehat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}(t,\mathbf{W}_{i})-g_{\hat{\theta}}(t,\mathbf{W}_{i})\right)^{2}}{m_{\hat{\theta}}(t\,|\,\mathbf{W})}(1-m_{\hat{\theta}}(t\,|\,\mathbf{W}))

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(1-c)}{c}\left(\widehat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}(t,\mathbf{W}_{i})-g_{\hat{\theta}}(t,\mathbf{W}_{i})\right)^{2}

Now it follows from Assumption
4.4.2

(v)
and the conditional Chebyshev’s inequality that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(|R_{1}|>\epsilon\,|\,\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\bullet}},\hat{\theta}\,\right)

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon^{-2}\var(R_{1}\,|\,\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\bullet}},\hat{\theta})\leq\frac{1-c}{\epsilon^{2}c}\mathcal{E}_{2,t}^{(n)}\xrightarrow{P}0,

from which we conclude that
R_{1}\xrightarrow{P}0
.

The analysis of
R_{2}
is similar. Observe that the summands are conditionally i.i.d. given
\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\bullet}},\hat{\theta}
, and
T_{\bullet}\coloneq(T_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}
. They are also conditionally mean zero since

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[r_{i}^{2}\,|\,\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\bullet}},T_{\bullet},\hat{\theta}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}\,|\,\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\bullet}},T_{\bullet},\hat{\theta}]-g_{\hat{\theta}}(t,\mathbf{W}_{i})\right)\left(\frac{\mathbbl{1}(T_{i}=t)}{\widehat{m}_{\hat{\theta}}(\mathbf{W}_{i})}-\frac{\mathbbl{1}(T_{i}=t)}{m_{\hat{\theta}}(\mathbf{W}_{i})}\right)

\displaystyle=(\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}\,|\,\varphi(\hat{\theta},\mathbf{W}_{i}),T_{i},\hat{\theta}]-g_{\hat{\theta}}(t,\mathbf{W}_{i}))\left(\frac{\mathbbl{1}(T_{i}=t)}{\widehat{m}_{\hat{\theta}}(\mathbf{W}_{i})}-\frac{\mathbbl{1}(T_{i}=t)}{m_{\hat{\theta}}(\mathbf{W}_{i})}\right)=0,

where we use that on the event
(T_{i}=t)
,

\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}\,|\,\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\theta},i},T_{i},\hat{\theta}\,]=\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}\,|\,\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\theta},i},T_{i}=t,\hat{\theta}\,]=g_{\hat{\theta}}(t,\mathbf{W}_{i}).

To bound the conditional variance, note first that Assumption
4.4.1
and Assumption
(ii)
imply

\displaystyle\var\big(Y_{i}\,|\,\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\theta},i},T_{i},\hat{\theta}\,\big)\leq\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}^{2}\,|\,\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\theta},i},T_{i},\hat{\theta}\,\big]=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{i}^{2}\,|\,\mathbf{W}_{i},T_{i},\hat{\theta}\,\big]\,|\,\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\theta},i},T_{i},\hat{\theta}\,\big]\leq C,

which in return implies that

\displaystyle\var\left(r_{i}^{2}\,|\,\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\bullet}},T_{\bullet},\hat{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbbl{1}(T_{i}=t)\left(\frac{1}{\widehat{m}_{\hat{\theta}}(\mathbf{W}_{i})}-\frac{1}{m_{\hat{\theta}}(\mathbf{W}_{i})}\right)^{2}\var\left(Y_{i}\,|\,\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\theta},i},T_{i},\hat{\theta}\,\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\frac{1}{\widehat{m}_{\hat{\theta}}(\mathbf{W}_{i})}-\frac{1}{m_{\hat{\theta}}(\mathbf{W}_{i})}\right)^{2}.

It now follows from the conditional Chebyshev’s inequality that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(|R_{2}|>\epsilon\,|\,\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\bullet}},T_{\bullet},\hat{\theta})

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon^{-2}\var(R_{2}\,|\,\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\bullet}},T_{\bullet},\hat{\theta})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}\left(\frac{1}{\widehat{m}_{\hat{\theta}}(\mathbf{W}_{i})}-\frac{1}{m_{\hat{\theta}}(\mathbf{W}_{i})}\right)^{2}\leq C\ell_{c}^{2}\mathcal{E}_{1,t}^{(n)}.

Assumptions
4.4.2

(iv)
implies convergence to zero in probability, and from this we conclude that also
R_{2}\xrightarrow{P}0
.

We now turn to the discussion of the oracle term
U_{\hat{\theta}}^{(n)}
.
From Assumption
4.4.1
it follows that
(T_{i},\mathbf{W}_{i},Y_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}\,\perp\!\!\!\perp\,\hat{\theta}
, and hence the conditional distribution
U_{\hat{\theta}}^{(n)}\,|\,\hat{\theta}=\theta
is the same as
U_{\theta}^{(n)}
. We show that this distribution is asymptotically Gaussian uniformly over
\theta\in\Theta
. To see this, note that for each
\theta\in\Theta
, the terms of
U_{\theta}^{(n)}
are i.i.d. with mean
\chi_{t}(\mathbf{Z}_{\theta})
and variance
\mathbb{V}_{t}(\mathbf{Z}_{\theta})
.
The conditional Jensen’s inequality and Assumption
4.4.2

(i)
imply that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|u_{i}(\theta)|^{2+\delta}]

\displaystyle\leq C_{2+\delta}(\mathbb{E}[|g_{\theta}(t,\mathbf{W})|^{2+\delta}]+c^{-(1+\delta)}\mathbb{E}[|Y-g_{\theta}(t,\mathbf{W})|^{2+\delta}\,])

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2+\delta}],

where
C^{\prime}>0
is a constant depending on
c
and
\delta
.
Thus we can apply the Lindeberg-Feller CLT, as stated in
Shah and Peters (2020, Lem. 18)
, to conclude that

S_{n}(\theta)\coloneq\sqrt{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\mathbb{V}_{t}(\mathbf{Z}_{\theta})^{-1/2}(U_{\theta}^{(n)}-\chi_{t}(\mathbf{Z}_{\theta}))\xrightarrow{d/\Theta}\mathrm{N}(0,1),

where the convergence holds uniformly over
\theta\in\Theta
. With
\Phi(\cdot)
denoting the CDF of a standard normal, this implies that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in\real}|\mathbb{P}(S_{n}(\hat{\theta})\leq t)-\Phi(t)|

\displaystyle=\sup_{t\in\real}\left\lvert\int(\mathbb{P}(S_{n}(\hat{\theta})\leq t\,|\,\hat{\theta}=\theta)-\Phi(t))P_{\hat{\theta}}(\mathrm{d}\theta)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\sup_{t\in\real}|\mathbb{P}(S_{n}(\theta)\leq t)-\Phi(t)|\longrightarrow 0.

(4.A.3)

This shows that
S_{n}(\hat{\theta})\xrightarrow{d}\mathrm{N}(0,1)
as desired.
The last part of the theorem is a simple consequence of Slutsky’s theorem.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.4.2

For each
i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}
, let
\widehat{u}_{i}=u_{i}(\hat{\theta})+r_{i}^{1}+r_{i}^{2}+r_{i}^{3}

be the decomposition from (
4.4.16
).
For the squared sum, it is immediate from Theorem
4.4.1
that

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}\widehat{u}_{i}\right)^{2}=\left(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}u_{i}(\hat{\theta})\right)^{2}+o_{P}(1).

For the sum of squares, we first expand the squares as

\displaystyle\widehat{u}_{i}^{2}=u_{i}(\hat{\theta})^{2}+2(r_{i}^{1}+r_{i}^{2}+r_{i}^{3})u_{i}(\hat{\theta})+(r_{i}^{1}+r_{i}^{2}+r_{i}^{3})^{2}.

(4.A.4)

We show that the last two terms are convergent to zero in probability. For the cross-term, we note by Cauchy-Schwarz that

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}(r_{i}^{1}+r_{i}^{2}+r_{i}^{3})u_{i}(\hat{\theta})\Big|\leq\left(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}(r_{i}^{1}+r_{i}^{2}+r_{i}^{3})^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}u_{i}(\hat{\theta})^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

We show later that the sum
\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}u_{i}^{2}
is convergent in probability. Thus, to show that the last two terms of (
4.A.4
) converge to zero, it suffices to show that

\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}(r_{i}^{1}+r_{i}^{2}+r_{i}^{3})^{2}\xrightarrow{P}0
.
To this end, we observe that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}(r_{i}^{1}+r_{i}^{2}+r_{i}^{3})^{2}\leq\frac{3}{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}(r_{i}^{1})^{2}+(r_{i}^{2})^{2}+(r_{i}^{3})^{2}

The last term is handled similarly to
R_{3}
in the proof of Theorem
4.4.1
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}(r_{i}^{3})^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}\left(\widehat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}(t,\mathbf{W}_{i})-g_{\hat{\theta}}(t,\mathbf{W}_{i})\right)^{2}\left(\frac{\mathbbl{1}(T_{i}=t)}{\widehat{m}_{\hat{\theta}}(t\,|\,\mathbf{W}_{i})}-\frac{\mathbbl{1}(T_{i}=t)}{m_{\hat{\theta}}(t\,|\,\mathbf{W}_{i})}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\Big(\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}(\widehat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}(t,\mathbf{W}_{i})-g_{\hat{\theta}}(t,\mathbf{W}_{i}))^{2}\Big)\Big(\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}\Big(\frac{\mathbbl{1}(T_{i}=t)}{\widehat{m}_{\hat{\theta}}(t\,|\,\mathbf{W}_{i})}-\frac{\mathbbl{1}(T_{i}=t)}{m_{\hat{\theta}}(t\,|\,\mathbf{W}_{i})}\Big)^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\ell_{c}^{2}n\mathcal{E}_{1}^{(n)}\mathcal{E}_{2,t}^{(n)}\xrightarrow{P}0,

where we have used the naive inequality
\sum a_{i}^{2}b_{i}^{2}\leq(\sum a_{i}^{2})(\sum b_{i}^{2})
rather than Cauchy-Schwarz. From the proof of Theorem
4.4.1
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(r_{i}^{1})^{2}\,|\,\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\bullet}},\hat{\theta}]=\var[r_{i}^{1}\,|\,\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\bullet}},\hat{\theta}]\leq\frac{1-c}{c}\left(\widehat{g}_{\hat{\theta}}(t,\mathbf{W}_{i})-g_{\hat{\theta}}(t,\mathbf{W}_{i})\right)^{2},

and hence the conditional Markov’s inequality yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}(r_{i}^{1})^{2}>\epsilon\,|\,\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\bullet}},\hat{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}(r_{i}^{1})^{2}\,|\,\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\bullet}},\hat{\theta}\right]

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{-1}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[(r_{i}^{1})^{2}\,|\,\mathbf{Z}_{\hat{\bullet}},\hat{\theta}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1-c}{\epsilon c}\mathcal{E}_{2,t}^{(n)}\xrightarrow{P}0.

Thus we also conclude that
\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}(r_{i}^{1})^{2}\xrightarrow{P}0
.
Analogously, the final remainder
\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}(r_{i}^{2})^{2}
can be shown to converge to zero in probability by leveraging the argument for
R_{2}\xrightarrow{P}0
in the proof of Theorem
4.4.1
.

Combining the arguments above we conclude that

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{t}(\widehat{g},\widehat{m})=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}\widehat{u}_{i}^{2}-\left(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}\widehat{u}_{i}\right)^{2}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}u_{i}(\hat{\theta})^{2}-\Big(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}u_{i}(\hat{\theta})\Big)^{2}+o_{P}(1)

As noted in the proof of Theorem
4.4.1
,
for each
\theta\in\Theta
the terms
\{u_{i}(\theta)\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}
are i.i.d
with
\mathbb{E}[|u_{i}(\theta)|^{2+\delta}]\leq C^{\prime}\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{2+\delta}]<\infty
.
Hence, the uniform law of large numbers, as stated in
Shah and Peters (2020, Lem. 19)
, implies that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}u_{i}(\theta)-\mathbb{E}[u_{1}(\theta)])\xrightarrow{P/\Theta}0,\qquad\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}u_{i}^{2}(\theta)-\mathbb{E}[u_{1}(\theta)^{2}]\xrightarrow{P/\Theta}0,

where the convergence in probability holds uniformly over
\theta\in\Theta
.
Since
\var(u_{1}(\theta))=\var(\psi_{t}(\mathbf{Z}_{\theta}))=\mathbb{V}_{t}(\mathbf{Z}_{\theta})
, this lets us conclude that

\displaystyle S_{n}(\theta)\coloneq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}{u}_{i}(\theta)^{2}-\Big(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{I}_{3}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{3}}u_{i}(\theta)\Big)^{2}-\mathbb{V}_{t}(\mathbf{Z}_{\theta})\xrightarrow{P/\Theta}0.

We now use that convergence in distribution is equivalent to convergence in probability for deterministic limit variables, see
Christgau et al. (2023b, Cor. B.4.)
for a general uniform version of the statement. Since Assumption
4.4.1
implies that the conditional distribution
S_{n}(\hat{\theta})\,|\,\hat{\theta}=\theta
is the same as
S_{n}(\theta)
, the computation in (
4.A.3
) with
\Phi(\cdot)=\mathbbl{1}(\cdot\geq 0)
yields that
S_{n}(\hat{\theta})\xrightarrow{d}0
.
This lets us conclude that

\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{t}(\widehat{g},\widehat{m})=S_{n}(\hat{\theta})+o_{P}(1)\xrightarrow{P}0,

which finishes the proof.
∎

4.A.7
Proof of Theorem
4.4.4

We fix
P\in\mathcal{P}
and suppress it from notation throughout the proof.
Given
\theta\in\real^{d}
, the single-index model assumption
(
4.4.13
) implies that

\displaystyle b_{t}(\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta)=\mathbb{E}[Y\,|\,T=t,\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta]=\mathbb{E}[h_{t}(\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta_{P})\,|\,T=t,\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta].

Now since
h_{t}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, we may write

\displaystyle h_{t}(\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta)

\displaystyle=h_{t}(\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta_{P})+R(\theta,\theta_{P}),

\displaystyle R(\theta,\theta_{P})

\displaystyle=h_{t}^{\prime}(\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta_{P})\mathbf{W}^{\top}(\theta-\theta_{P})+r(\mathbf{W}^{\top}(\theta-\theta_{P}))\mathbf{W}^{\top}(\theta-\theta_{P})

for a continuous function
r\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying that
\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}r(\epsilon)=0
. It follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[h_{t}(\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta_{P})\,|\,T=t,\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta]=h_{t}(\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta)-\mathbb{E}[R(\theta,\theta_{P})\,|\,T=t,\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta],

The theoretical bias induced by adjusting for
\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta
instead of
\mathbf{W}
, or equivalently
\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta_{P}
, is therefore

\displaystyle\chi_{t}(\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta;P)-\chi_{t}(\mathbf{W})

\displaystyle=\chi_{t}(\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta;P)-\chi_{t}(\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta_{P})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[h_{t}(\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta)-\mathbb{E}[R(\theta,\theta_{P})\,|\,T=t,\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta]-h_{t}(\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta_{P})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[R(\theta,\theta_{P})-\mathbb{E}[R(\theta,\theta_{P})\,|\,T=t,\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta]]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[R(\theta,\theta_{P})\cdot\left(1-\frac{\mathbbl{1}(T=t)}{\mathbb{P}(T=t\,|\,\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=C(\theta,\theta_{P})^{\top}(\theta-\theta_{P}),

where

\displaystyle C(\theta;\theta_{P})=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(h_{t}^{\prime}(\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta)+r(\mathbf{W}^{\top}(\theta-\theta_{P}))\right)\left(1-\frac{\mathbbl{1}(T=t)}{\mathbb{P}(T=t\,|\,\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta)}\right)\mathbf{W}\right].

To show that
u
is differentiable at
\theta=\theta_{P}
, it therefore suffices to show that the mapping
\theta\mapsto C(\theta;\theta_{P})
is continuous at
\theta_{P}
.

We first show that
\mathbb{P}(T=t\,|\,\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta)
is continuous at
\theta_{P}
almost surely, which follows after applying a coordinate change such that
\theta
becomes a basis vector. To be more precise, we may choose a neighborhood
\mathcal{U}\subset\real^{d}
of
\theta_{P}
and
d-1
continuous functions

q_{2},\ldots,q_{d}\colon\mathcal{U}\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}^{d-1}

such that
Q(\theta)\coloneq(\lVert\theta\rVert^{-1}\theta,q_{2}(\theta),\ldots q_{d}(\theta))
is an orthogonal matrix for every
\theta\in\mathcal{U}
. For example, the first
d
vectors of the Gram-Schmidt process applied to
(\theta,\mathbf{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{e}_{d})
is continuous and yields an orthonormal basis for
\theta\in\real^{d}\setminus\operatorname{span}(\mathbf{e}_{d})
, so this works in case
\theta_{P}\neq\pm\mathbf{e}_{d}
.
Let
Z(\theta)=Q(\theta)^{\top}\mathbf{W}
and note that
Z(\theta)_{1}=\lVert\theta\rVert^{-1}\theta^{\top}\mathbf{W}=\lVert\theta\rVert^{-1}\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta
.
Then by iterated expectations,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(T=t\,|\,\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta)=\mathbb{E}[m(t\,|\,\mathbf{W})\,|\,\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[m(t\,|\,Q(\theta)Z(\theta))\,|\,Z(\theta)_{1}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\int_{\real{}^{d-1}}m(t\,|\,\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(\theta,z))p_{\mathbf{W}}(\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(\theta,z))\mathrm{d}z}{\int_{\real{}^{d-1}}p_{\mathbf{W}}(\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(\theta,z))\mathrm{d}z},\qquad\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(\theta,z)\coloneq Q(\theta)\begin{pmatrix}Z(\theta)_{1}\\
z\end{pmatrix}

Each integrand is bounded and continuous over
\theta\in\mathcal{U}
.
From dominated convergence it follows that
\mathbb{P}(T=t\,|\,\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta)
is continuous at
\theta_{P}
almost surely.
It follows that almost surely,

\displaystyle\left(h_{t}^{\prime}(\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta)+r(\mathbf{W}^{\top}(\theta-\theta_{P}))\right)\left(1-\frac{\mathbbl{1}(T=t)}{\mathbb{P}(T=t\,|\,\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta)}\right)\mathbf{W}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow h_{t}^{\prime}(\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta_{P})\left(1-\frac{\mathbbl{1}(T=t)}{\mathbb{P}(T=t\,|\,\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta_{P})}\right)\mathbf{W},\qquad\text{for }\theta\to\theta_{P}.

By dominated convergence again we conclude that

\displaystyle C(\theta;\theta_{P})\longrightarrow\mathbb{E}\left[h_{t}^{\prime}(\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta_{P})\left(1-\frac{\mathbbl{1}(T=t)}{\mathbb{P}(T=t\,|\,\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta_{P})}\right)\mathbf{W}\right],\qquad\theta\to\theta_{P}.

This shows that
\theta\mapsto C(\theta;\theta_{P})
is continuous at
\theta_{P}
, and hence we conclude that
u
is differentiable at
\theta=\theta_{P}
with gradient

\displaystyle\nabla u(\theta_{P})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[h_{t}^{\prime}(\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta_{P})\left(1-\frac{\mathbbl{1}(T=t)}{\mathbb{P}(T=t\,|\,\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta_{P})}\right)\mathbf{W}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[h_{t}^{\prime}(\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta_{P})\left(1-\frac{\mathbb{P}(T=t\,|\,\mathbf{W})}{\mathbb{P}(T=t\,|\,\mathbf{W}^{\top}\theta_{P})}\right)\mathbf{W}\right].

∎

4.B. Details of simulation study

Our experiments were conducted in Python
(
Van Rossum et al., 1995
)
.
The linear and logistic regression was imported from the
scikit-learn
package
(
Pedregosa et al., 2011
)
, and the neural network for the DOPE-IDX was implemented using
pytorch

(
Paszke et al., 2019
)
.

Figure 4.B.1
:
Neural network architecture for single-index model.

The neural network architecture is illustrated in Figure
4.B.1
.
The network was optimized using MSE loss and the ADAM optimizer with
lr=1e-3
and
n_iter=1200
in the simulation experiment. For the NHANES application the settings were similar, but with BCELoss and
n_iter=3000
.
We discovered that the optimization procedure could get stuck in a local minimum with significantly lower training score. To avoid getting stuck in a such a ‘bad local minimum’, it was possible to refit the network with random initialization several times and pick the model with highest training score. The frequency of bad local minimums is roughly
15\%
, with variation between each setting. Thus, refitting the network, say 5 times, is enough to avoid a bad minimum
>99,99\%
of the time.
To save the computational cost of refitting many times for each simulation, we initialized the first layer with an initial value
\theta_{init}=\beta+\mathrm{Unif}(-0.1,0.1)^{\otimes d}
.
The logistic regression for the propensity score was fitted without
\ell_{2}
-penalty and optimized using the
lbfgs
optimizer. The propensity score was clipped to the interval
(0.01,0.99)
for all estimators of the adjusted mean.

Figure 4.B.2
:
Comparison of DOPE estimators with and without sample splitting.

Figure
4.B.2
shows the cross-fitted DOPE estimators versus their full sample counterparts in the simulation setup of Section
4.5.1
. We observe that cross-fitting generally seems to decrease performance for small sample sizes, but the discrepancy diminishes for larger sample sizes.

4.C. Extension to cross-fitting

Algorithm 2

Cross-fitted DOPE

input
: observations
(T_{i},\mathbf{W}_{i},Y_{i})_{i\in[n]}
, partition
[n]=J_{1}\cup\cdots\cup J_{K}
;

1

options
: integer
1\leq m\leq K-2
and options for Algorithm
1
;

2

for

k=1,\ldots,K

do

3

Set
\mathcal{I}_{3}=J_{k}
,
\mathcal{I}_{1}=\bigcup_{l=k+1}^{k+m}J_{l}
and

\mathcal{I}_{2}=[n]\setminus(\mathcal{I}_{1}\cup\mathcal{I}_{3})
;

4

Compute
\widehat{\chi}_{t,k}^{\mathrm{dope}}
as the output of Algorithm
1
;

5

Compute variance estimate
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{t,k}
given as in (
4.4.17
);

6

return

\widehat{\chi}_{t}^{\mathsf{x}}\coloneq\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\widehat{\chi}_{t,k}^{\mathrm{dope}}

and
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{t}^{\mathsf{x}}\coloneq\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{t,k}

A cross-fitting procedure for the DOPE is described in Algorithm
2
, which computes both a cross-fitted version of the DOPE and its variance estimator. Here the indices of the folds are understood to cycle modulo
K
such that
J_{K+1}=J_{1}
and so forth. This version of cross-fitting with three index sets has also been referred to as ‘double cross-fitting’ by
Zivich and Breskin (2021)
.

We note that the ‘standard arguments’ for establishing convergence of the cross-fitted estimator cannot be applied directly to our case. This is because, for each fold
k\in[K]
, the corresponding oracle terms
U_{\hat{\theta}_{k}}^{(n)}
do not only involve the data indexed by
\mathcal{I}_{3}^{k}
, but also depend on
\hat{\theta}_{k}
which is estimated from data indexed by
\mathcal{I}_{1}^{k}
. Hence the oracle terms are not independent. However, we believe that this dependency should be negligible, and perhaps the convergence can be established under a more refined theoretical analysis.

Chapter 5
Assumption-lean Aalen regression

Alexander Mangulad Christgau and Niels Richard Hansen

5.1
Introduction

Statistical inference for the conditional association between a time-to-event and an exposure is the primary objective in many bio-medical applications. A critical task for such applications is to first define an appropriate measure of association. Formulating a general and interpretable measure is intricate and remains an active research topic.

The hazard ratio is one of the most popular and reported measures of association in clinical research, with its application being a subject of ongoing discussion since the influential work of
Cox (1972)
.
Despite its popularity, the hazard ratio, and the often accompanying Cox model, are subject to several significant drawbacks, as highlighted by
Hernán (2010)
;
Martinussen and Vansteelandt (2013)
;
Stensrud and Hernán (2020)
, among others. These drawbacks
include non-collapsibility of the hazard ratio and that it is unsuitable for quantifying causal effects. The pitfalls of model misspecification is another notable drawback. When used as a conditional association measure within the framework of the
Cox model, its interpretation is problematic without strong model assumptions such as proportionality.

To address the issue of model misspecification,
Vansteelandt et al. (2022)
propose a more general measure of association that reduces to the log (cumulative) hazard ratio under the Cox model. Generalizing a model-based measure of association with a model-free measure has now become known as assumption-lean inference
(
Berk et al., 2019
)
. This approach subsumes model-based and estimand-based methods, and can be advantageous when each method has its own merits, see
Vansteelandt and Dukes (2022)
and the surrounding discussion for more details.

In this work we develop an analogous approach, but based on the hazard difference as an effect measure.
The hazard difference is collapsible for the
additive hazards model
, introduced by Aalen
(
Aalen, 1980
;
Aalen, 1989
)
. As mentioned, this desirable property does not hold for the hazard ratio and the Cox model

(
Martinussen and Vansteelandt, 2013
;
Daniel et al., 2021
)
.
There are other appealing properties of the hazard difference, but the discussion is subtle, see, for example,
Didelez and Stensrud (2022)
;
Martinussen et al. (2020)
.
Regardless, there are several applications where additive hazards are more plausible than proportional ones; we refer to
Breslow and Day (1987)
;
Lin and Ying (1994)
;
Kravdal (1997)
;
Lee S. McDaniel and Chappell (2019)
;
Bischofberger et al. (2023)
.

Assumptions on the non-baseline term

Ratio-based target

Difference-based target

Parametric

Cox model
(
Cox, 1972
)

Additive risk model in
Lin and Ying (1994)

Semi-parametric

Partial Cox model
(
Sasieni, 1992
;
Huang, 1999
;
Zhong et al., 2022
)

Partially additive hazards
(
McKeague and Sasieni, 1994
;
Dukes et al., 2019
;
Hou et al., 2023
)

Assumption-lean

(
Vansteelandt et al., 2022
)

This work

Table 5.1.1
:
Overview of selected works on inference for the conditional association between a time-to-event and an exposure, defined using varying levels of model assumptions. All models include a nonparametric baseline term that does not depend on covariates.

Despite some of their well-behaved theoretical properties, additive hazards are “somewhat overlooked in practice” according to
Martinussen and Scheike (2006, p. 103)
.
A common criticism of additive hazard models is that they can lead to hazard estimates that are negative and are thus not valid hazards.
To circumvent this issue, we define a model-free target parameter based on the
Local Covariance Measure (LCM)
introduced by
Christgau et al. (2023b)
. We show that, within a partially additive hazards model, it coincides with the cumulative effect of the exposure.
However, our target does not rely on such modeling assumptions and we discuss its interpretations in more general settings.
Table
5.1.1
gives an overview of our target parameter in relation to some existing works.
We show how the target may be estimated using flexible machine learning methods, and prove asymptotic normality under suitable rate conditions on the nuisance parameters.

5.1.1
A hazard difference estimand

To motivate our general framework
and results we first present a simplified example.

Example 1
.

Let
T\in[0,1]
denote a survival
time, let
X\in\{0,1\}
denote a binary baseline exposure and let
Z
denote
additional baseline covariates. With
\bm{H}_{t}(X,Z)
denoting the conditional cumulative
hazard given
(X,Z)
, the conditional survival function is

\mathbb{P}(T>t\,|\,X,Z)=e^{-\bm{H}_{t}(X,Z)}
, and

\Theta_{t}(Z)\coloneq\log\frac{\mathbb{P}(T>t\,|\,X=0,Z)}{\mathbb{P}(T>t\,|\,X=1,Z)}=\bm{H}_{t}(1,Z)-\bm{H}_{t}(0,Z)

(5.1.1)

quantifies the, possibly heterogeneous, exposure effect as a function of
Z
.
Suppose that

\bm{H}_{t}(X,Z)=\int_{0}^{t}\bm{h}_{s}(X,Z)\mathrm{d}s

is given in terms of the conditional hazard
\bm{h}_{s}(X,Z)
, then

\Theta_{t}(Z)=\int_{0}^{t}\underbrace{\bm{h}_{s}(1,Z)-\bm{h}_{s}(0,Z)}_{\eqcolon\theta_{s}(Z)}\mathrm{d}s=\int_{0}^{t}\theta_{s}(Z)\mathrm{d}s

is the integrated difference of conditional hazards. We can always express the
conditional hazard as

\bm{h}_{s}(X,Z)=X\theta_{s}(Z)+\bm{h}_{s}(0,Z),

(5.1.2)

and the additive decomposition (
5.1.2
) does not impose any model restrictions unless we restrict one of the functions
\theta_{s}(Z)
or
\bm{h}_{s}(0,Z)
.

We seek an estimand the summarizes the values of
\theta_{s}(Z)
over
s
and
Z
.
To this end, let
w_{s}(Z)
denote a
weight process
with

w_{s}(Z)\geq 0
and
\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}[w_{s}(Z)]\mathrm{d}s=1
,
and consider the
weighted hazard difference estimand

\gamma^{w}_{1}\coloneq\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}[\theta_{s}(Z)w_{s}(Z)]\mathrm{d}s.

The real-valued parameter
\gamma^{w}_{1}
is the endpoint of
the functional estimand

\gamma^{w}_{t}\coloneq\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}[\theta_{s}(Z)w_{s}(Z)]\mathrm{d}s,\quad t\in[0,1].

For
w_{s}(Z)=1
, the estimand simplifies to the expected cumulative hazard difference
\gamma^{1}_{t}=\mathbb{E}(\Theta_{t}(Z))
, but other weights
are also of interest. For the
partially additive hazards model
, where

\theta_{s}
is independent of
Z
, we see that
\gamma_{t}^{w}=\int_{0}^{t}\theta_{s}\mathbb{E}[w_{s}(Z)]\mathrm{d}s
is a cumulative weighted hazard difference.
Efficient and (rate) doubly robust estimation was considered for the partially
additive model in
Dukes et al. (2019)
and
Hou et al. (2023)
using semiparametric estimation theory – in particular when
\theta_{t}=\theta
is independent of
t
and

\gamma_{t}^{w}=\theta t
. In this paper we introduce an assumption-lean
additive hazard estimand and corresponding estimation theory that, as a special case, applies to model (
5.1.2
) without any model restrictions.

To give an explicit formula for our assumption-lean estimand for model (
5.1.2
) within this simplified example,
let
Y_{t}=\mathbbl{1}(T\geq t)
denote the at-risk indicator, and define what we call
the
Aalen covariance
weights

w_{s}^{\textsc{ac}}(Z)\coloneq\frac{Y_{s}\pi_{s}(Z)(1-\pi_{s}(Z))}{\mathbb{E}[Y_{s}\pi_{s}(Z)(1-\pi_{s}(Z))]},

(5.1.3)

where
\pi_{s}(Z)=\mathbb{P}(X=1\,|\,T\geq s,Z)
. The resulting estimand can be expressed as

\gamma_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\left[\theta_{s}(Z)w_{s}^{\textsc{ac}}(Z)\right]\mathrm{d}s=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\theta_{s}(Z)Y_{s}\pi_{s}(Z)(1-\pi_{s}(Z))\right]}{\mathbb{E}[Y_{s}\pi_{s}(Z)(1-\pi_{s}(Z))]}\mathrm{d}s.

(5.1.4)

The weights (
5.1.3
) and the formula (
5.1.4
) may at first appear
unmotivated. In Section 3 in
Vansteelandt and Dukes (2022)

we find weights similar to (
5.1.3
) used to represent assumption-lean GLM regression estimands, and also in Section 2 in
Vansteelandt et al. (2022)
similar
weights appear in the representation of assumption-lean Cox regression estimands.

In Example
4
we show that the Aalen covariance weights and the
estimand (
5.1.4
) appear naturally as an assumption-lean Aalen regression estimand.
The estimand
\gamma_{t}
given by (
5.1.4
) is an example
of the
Aalen covariance measure
(ACM) – the general estimand we introduce in Definition
1
.
This is, in turn, an example of a
Local Covariance Measure
as introduced by
Christgau et al. (2023b)
.
We show in this paper that ACM can be defined and interpreted as an
assumption-lean estimand for any real-valued and time-dependent
X
-process and time-dependent
Z
-process, thus generalizing model (
5.1.2
) considerably.

5.1.2
Organization of paper

We organize the rest of the paper as follows.
In Section
5.2
, we first introduce our general setup and define our target estimand. We then discuss interpretations of the estimand based on theoretical properties and computations in concrete examples.
Conditions on the censoring mechanism are postponed to the end of the section to avoid a technical discussion from the onset.
In Section
5.3
, we discuss an estimation methods for our target estimand, in particular we propose the
x-acm
, which is based on double machine learning estimator.
In Section
5.4
, we prove that the
x-acm
is asymptotically Gaussian with a
\sqrt{n}
-rate under suitable conditions. The most significant conditions are rate-conditions on nuisance estimators, which are similar to those for other (rate) doubly robust estimators.
In Section
5.5
