, conduct a simulation study that demonstrates the effect of the
x-acm
. We conclude with a discussion
in Section
5.6
outlining potential future research directions.

The supplementary material consists of Section
5
, which contains proofs of all our main results and
related auxiliary lemmas, and Section
5
, which includes a discussion of our framework in the context of multiplicative hazards models.

5.2
Target estimand and interpretations

5.2.1
General setup

We consider a survival time
T^{*}>0
censored by a random variable
C\in[0,1]
, such that the observed time is
T\coloneq T^{*}\wedge C
together with the indicator
\Delta\coloneq\mathbbl{1}(T^{*}\leq C)
. From these we define the observed counting process
N_{t}=\mathbbl{1}(T\leq t,\Delta=1)
, and the at-risk indicator
Y_{t}=\mathbbl{1}(T\geq t)
.
In addition to
(T,\Delta)
, we also observe a covariate process
Z=(Z_{t})
and an exposure process
X=(X_{t})
of particular interest. The covariate process can, in principle, take values in any measurable space, whereas the exposure process is assumed real-valued and left-continuous.

We postpone an elaborate discussion of assumptions on the censoring mechanism to Section
5.2.4
. In summary, we introduce Assumption
5.2.1
, which is an instance of
independent right-censoring
in the sense of
Andersen et al. (1993)
, and which will remain an assumption throughout the remainder of the manuscript.

For any stochastic process
W=(W_{t})
, we let
\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muW\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}=(W_{s})_{s<t}
denote the history up to time
t>0
, but not including the timepoint

t
.
10
10

10

This convention differs from other works such as
(
Lok, 2017
;
Lok, 2008
)
, which do include the timepoint
t
in the history. For any left-continuous process, this convention does not affect the information content of the history. In particular, there is no distinction between the conventions when conditioning on
\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}
, see also Remark
1

Note that by left-continuity,
X_{t}=X_{t-}\coloneq\lim_{s\to t^{-}}X_{s}
is determined by the history
\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}
.

The conditional cumulative hazard
\bm{H}_{t}
, given the processes
X
and
Z
, is defined by

\displaystyle\bm{H}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})\coloneq-\log\mathbb{P}(T^{*}>t\,|\,\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}).

The overall objective is to quantify how
\bm{H}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})
depends on the
exposure process
\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}
. Such a dependence can, in general, be complicated and depend on
the value of the covariate process
\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}
. Our approach is
assumption-lean
meaning that
we do not make strong model assumptions on how
\bm{H}_{t}
depends on
\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}
and
\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}
. We will
assume, though, that
\bm{H}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})
is given by a conditional hazard function, denoted by
\bm{h}_{t}
,
that is,
\bm{H}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})=\int_{0}^{t}\bm{h}_{s}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{s},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{s})\mathrm{d}s
.

5.2.2
Aalen Covariance Measure

Before we define our model-free estimand in the most general case, we need to introduce two auxiliary stochastic processes:
the predictable projection of
X
and the
Z
-compensated counting process.

A stochastic process is said to be
càglàd
if its sample paths are left-continuous and have right-limits at all times. We henceforth assume that
X
is càglàd and square-integrable.
Then, the
predictable projection
of
X
onto the histories of
N
and
Z
can be defined as the unique
11
11

11

Technically, it is unique up to
evanescence
.
càglàd process, denoted by
\Pi=(\Pi_{t})
, satisfying that

\Pi_{t}=\mathbb{E}[X_{t}\,|\,\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muN\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}].

(5.2.5)

For more details, see for example Corollary 7.6.8 in
Cohen and Elliott (2015)
.
We will primarily need the process
\Pi_{t}
on the event
(Y_{t}=1)=(T\geq t)
, where it takes the value
\pi_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})\coloneq\mathbb{E}[X_{t}\,|\,T\geq t,\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}]
.
12
12

12

We technically define
\pi_{t}
via the Doob-Dynkin lemma such that
Y_{t}\Pi_{t}=Y_{t}\pi_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})
holds
surely
. Thus we can ensure that
\pi_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})
is a càglàd version of
\mathbb{E}[X_{t}\,|\,T\geq t,\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}]
.

Introducing

h_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{s})=\mathbb{E}[\bm{h}_{s}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{s},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{s})\,|\,T\geq t,\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}],

the
innovation theorem
, see, e.g., Theorem II.T14 in
Brémaud (1981)
,
gives that
h_{t}
is the conditional hazard given
Z
that satisfies the relation

\displaystyle H_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})\coloneq\int_{0}^{t}h_{s}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{s})\mathrm{d}s=-\log\mathbb{P}(T^{*}>t\,|\,\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}).

In terms of the conditional hazard given
Z
we define the
Z
-compensated counting process

M_{t}\coloneq N_{t}-\int_{0}^{t}Y_{s}h_{s}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{s})\mathrm{d}s=N_{t}-H_{t\wedge T}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t\wedge T}).

We can now define our target estimand.

Definition 1

(Aalen Covariance Measure)
.

For each
t\in[0,1]
let

\displaystyle\rho(t)\coloneq\mathbb{E}[Y_{t}(X_{t}-\Pi_{t})^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[Y_{t}(X_{t}-\pi_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}))^{2}],

(5.2.6)

and define, whenever
\rho(t)>0
, the
residual process

G=(G_{t})
by

\displaystyle G_{t}\coloneq\frac{Y_{t}(X_{t}-\Pi_{t})}{\rho(t)}=\frac{Y_{t}(X_{t}-\pi_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}))}{\rho(t)}.

(5.2.7)

If
\rho(t)>0
for all
t\in[0,1]
, the
Aalen Covariance Measure (ACM)
is defined as the functional estimand
\gamma=(\gamma_{t})
given by

\gamma_{t}=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{t}G_{s}\mathrm{d}M_{s}\right]

(5.2.8)

for
t\in[0,1]
.

We discuss the positivity assumption
\rho(t)>0
further in Remark
2
.
To appreciate Definition
1
we compute the ACM for the partially additive hazards model.

Example 2

(Partially additive hazards model)
.

Suppose that

\bm{h}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})=\theta_{t}X_{t}+g_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}),

(5.2.9)

for some
\theta_{t}\in\real
and a function
g_{t}\in L^{1}(P_{\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}})
, where
P_{\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}}
denotes the distribution of
\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}
. The model given by (
5.2.9
) is the
partially additive hazards model
. It specifies the direct effect of
X
in a separate term with time-varying coefficient
\theta_{t}
. For this model
the innovation theorem yields the explicit expression

h_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})=\theta_{t}\pi_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})+g_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}).

(5.2.10)

Introducing the
(X,Z)
-compensated counting process,
\bm{M}_{t}\coloneq N_{t}-\bm{H}_{t\wedge T}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t\wedge T},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t\wedge T})

we get by (
5.2.10
) that

M_{t}=\bm{M}_{t}+\int_{0}^{t}\theta_{s}(X_{s}-\pi_{s}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{s}))\mathrm{d}s.

Since
\bm{M}_{t}
is a mean zero martingale and the residual process is predictable,

\displaystyle\gamma_{t}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{t}G_{s}\mathrm{d}\bm{M}_{s}+\int_{0}^{t}G_{s}\theta_{s}(X_{s}-\pi_{s}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{s}))\mathrm{d}s\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\theta_{s}\frac{Y_{s}(X_{s}-\pi_{s}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{s}))^{2}}{\rho(s)}\mathrm{d}s\right]=\int_{0}^{t}\theta_{s}\mathrm{d}s.

(5.2.11)

Hence, the ACM reduces to the cumulative direct effect
\Theta_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\theta_{s}\mathrm{d}s
for the
partially additive hazards model.

We note that both
Dukes et al. (2019)
;
Hou et al. (2023)
consider models that fall under the partially additive model given by (
5.2.9
), and they propose estimation of the cumulative effect
\Theta_{t}
with doubly robust estimation methods.
The example above shows that our ACM estimand reduces to the cumulative effect for the partially additive model, but we will not make this model assumption. We present additional represetations and interpretations of the ACM in Section
5.2.3

Remark 1
.

The ACM is an example of a
Local Covariance Measure
(LCM), which was introduced by

Christgau et al. (2023b)
in a more general setting of counting processes, where all definitions and results
are formulated in terms of filtrations and intensities. Since this work is focused on survival analysis, the results are formulated using histories and conditional hazards to make them more accessible. To describe the connection between
the notation used in this paper and in
Christgau et al. (2023b)
, introduce the two filtrations

\mathcal{F}_{t}\coloneq\sigma(Z_{s},N_{s};s\leq t)
and
\mathcal{G}_{t}\coloneq\sigma(X_{s},Z_{s},N_{s};s\leq t)
. Then

\mathcal{G}_{t-}=\sigma(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muN\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})
and
\mathcal{F}_{t-}=\sigma(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muN\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})
, and

\lambda_{t}\coloneq Y_{t}h_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})
and
\bm{\lambda}_{t}\coloneq Y_{t}\bm{h}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})
are the

\mathcal{F}_{t}
- and
\mathcal{G}_{t}
-predictable intensities, respectively, for the counting process
N
.
The martingale argument in Example
2
uses that
\bm{M}_{t}
is a
\mathcal{G}_{t}
-martingale, which relies on the implicit Assumption
5.2.1
on the censoring mechanism.
The supplementary Section
5
elaborates on the notation,
which is also used in the proofs.

In
Christgau et al. (2023b)
, the LCM was introduced for any
\mathcal{G}_{t}
-predictable
residual process
G_{t}
satisfying
\mathbb{E}[G_{t}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{t-}]=0
. The residual process given
by (
5.2.7
) is a specific example of such a process. The LCM was introduced
in
Christgau et al. (2023b)
to quantify deviations from the hypothesis that
N
is

locally independent
of
X
given
\mathcal{F}_{t}
. The ACM, being an example of a LCM,
enjoys the same interpretation: under local independence,
\gamma
is equal to the zero function.
The ACM was, however, not considered in
Christgau et al. (2023b)
, and we proceed to show that the ACM enjoys the interpretation as an effect measure generalizing the cumulative effect in a partially additive hazards model.

5.2.3
Interpretations of the ACM as an effect measure

By Proposition 2.6 in
Christgau et al. (2023b)
, the
LCM, and therefore also the ACM, has the following equivalent representation for each
t\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle\gamma_{t}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\cov\big(G_{s},Y_{s}\big(\bm{h}_{s}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{s},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{s})-h_{s}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{s})\big)\big)\mathrm{d}s

(5.2.12)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\left[G_{s}\big(\bm{h}_{s}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{s},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{s})-h_{s}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{s})\big)\right]\mathrm{d}s,

(5.2.13)

where (
5.2.13
) follows from
G_{s}
having mean zero and
Y_{s}\in\{0,1\}
.
Example
2
showed that the ACM equals the cumulative direct effect under the partially additive hazards model. The following proposition asserts that in general – when
(
5.2.9
)
may not hold – the ACM equals the cumulated coefficient in the
L^{2}
-projection of the conditional hazard
\bm{h}_{t}
onto a partially additive model.

Proposition 5.2.1
.

Consider the minimization problem

\displaystyle\minimize_{\vartheta,g}\!:

\displaystyle\quad\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{1}Y_{t}\big(\bm{h}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})-\vartheta_{t}X_{t}-g_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})\big)^{2}\mathrm{d}t\right]

(5.2.14)

over all measurable functions
\vartheta\colon[0,1]\to\real
and collections of functions
(g_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
such that
g_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})
is progressively measurable.
Let
\vartheta^{\star}=(\vartheta_{t}^{\star})
be given by

\vartheta_{t}^{\star}\coloneq\cov\!\big(G_{t},Y_{t}\big(\bm{h}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})-h_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})\big)\big)

for
t\in[0,1]
.

Then there exists
g^{\star}
such that
(\vartheta^{\star},g^{\star})
is a solution to (
5.2.14
), and
\vartheta^{\star}
is unique up to modifications on a null set of
[0,1]
.
In particular, the ACM can be expressed as

\gamma_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\vartheta_{s}\mathrm{d}s,\qquad t\in[0,1],

for any solution
(\vartheta,g)
to (
5.2.14
).

All proofs are deferred to the supplement.

Example 3
.

Continuing Example
2
we see that for the partially additive hazards model
given by (
5.2.9
), the minimizer given by Proposition
5.2.1

is

\displaystyle\vartheta^{\star}_{t}

\displaystyle=\cov\!\big(G_{t},Y_{t}\big(\bm{h}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})-h_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})\big)\big)

\displaystyle=\cov\!\big(G_{t},\theta_{t}Y_{t}\big(X_{s}-\pi_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})\big)\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\theta_{t}\mathbb{E}[Y_{t}(X_{t}-\pi_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}))^{2}]}{\rho(s)}=\theta_{t}.

Thus, as also established directly in Example
2
, for the partially additive hazards model,

\gamma_{t}=\Theta_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\theta_{s}\mathrm{d}s
.

Example 4
.

As a generalization of the introductory Example
1
, suppose that
X_{t}\in\{0,1\}

and that

\bm{h}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})=X_{t}\theta_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})+g_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}).

If
\theta_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})
does not depend on
\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}
, this model is also a partially additive hazards model.
In general, we see that

h_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})=\pi_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})\theta_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})+g_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})

where
\pi_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})=\mathbb{P}(X_{t}=1\,|\,T\geq t,\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})
. Hence,

\displaystyle\rho(t)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{t}(X_{t}-\pi_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}))^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{t}\mathbb{E}\left[(X_{t}-\pi_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}))^{2}\mid T\geq 1,\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{t}\pi_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})(1-\pi_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}))\right].

The minimizer given by Proposition
5.2.1
is

\displaystyle\vartheta^{\star}_{t}

\displaystyle=\cov\!\big(G_{t},Y_{t}\big(\bm{h}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})-h_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})\big)\big)

\displaystyle=\cov\!\big(G_{t},\theta_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})Y_{t}\big(X_{t}-\pi_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})\big)\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\theta_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})\frac{Y_{t}\mathbb{E}\left[\big(X_{t}-\pi_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})\big)^{2}\,|\,T\geq t,Z\right]}{\rho(t)}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\theta_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})\frac{Y_{t}\pi_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})(1-\pi_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}))}{\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{t}\pi_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})(1-\pi_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}))\right]}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\theta_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})w_{t}^{\textsc{ac}}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})\right],

where the Aalen covariance weights are

w_{t}^{\textsc{ac}}(Z)=\frac{Y_{t}\pi_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})(1-\pi_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}))}{\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{t}\pi_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})(1-\pi_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}))\right]}.

(5.2.15)

In the special case with baseline exposure and baseline covariates, as treated
in Example
1
, these weights reduce to the weights in (
5.1.3
),
and the estimand given by (
5.1.4
) is, indeed, the ACM.

To give a more specific example of a model that is

not
a partially additive model, suppose
that
Z_{t}\in\real^{d}
and that
\theta_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})=\theta^{T}Z_{t}
for
\theta\in\real^{d}
. Then

\gamma_{t}^{w}=\theta^{T}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}[Z_{s}w_{s}^{\textsc{ac}}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{s})]\mathrm{d}s=\theta^{T}\omega_{t}^{\textsc{ac}}=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\theta_{k}\omega_{k,t}^{\textsc{ac}}

is a linear combination of the
\theta_{k}
-parameters with

\omega_{k,t}^{\textsc{ac}}=\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{k,s}w_{s}^{\textsc{ac}}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{s})\right]\mathrm{d}s
.

Proposition
5.2.1
allows us to interpret the ACM as a cumulative direct effect in the best
L^{2}
model-approximation by a partially additive hazards model.
This underlines that the ACM is well-defined and retains some level of interpretability for (small) deviations from the partially additive model. In contrast, the model-based target parameter in (
5.2.11
) is
a priori
undefined when the partially additive model is misspecified.

Beyond the partially additive model, as treated in Examples
2
and
3
,
we cannot expect the ACM to have a simple closed-form expression. Even for
the simple baseline model (
5.1.2
), the innocent looking representation

\gamma_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\left[\theta_{s}(Z)w_{s}^{\textsc{ac}}(Z)\right]\mathrm{d}s,

that follows from Example
4
, involves
the weights and in turn the predictable projections
\pi_{t}(Z)
. We elaborate on the computation of

\pi_{t}(Z)
for a multiplicative (baseline) hazards model in supplementary Section
5
, and we discuss the
representation of the ACM further for the Cox proportional hazards model in
Example
5
.

5.2.4
Censoring

Let
N_{t}^{*}\coloneq\mathbbl{1}(T^{*}\leq t)
denote the uncensored counting process, and let
N_{t}^{\mathrm{c}}=\mathbbl{1}(C\leq t)
denote the counting process for censoring. Recall the definitions of the filtrations
\mathcal{F}_{t}
and
\mathcal{G}_{t}
in Remark
1
,
and define similarly the filtrations
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{*}\coloneq\sigma(Z_{s},N_{s}^{*};s\leq t)
and

\mathcal{G}_{t}^{*}\coloneq\sigma(X_{s},Z_{s},N_{s}^{*};s\leq t)
.

We assume two conditions on the censoring mechanism, both are forms of
independent right-censoring
in the sense of
Andersen et al. (1993)
. We refer to
Martinussen and Scheike (2006)
;
Andersen et al. (1993)
for interpretations and intuition behind this type of censoring.
Formulated in terms of local independence as in
(
Røysland et al., 2022
)
, we specifically assume that
N_{t}^{*}
is locally independent of
N_{t}^{\mathrm{c}}
given both
\mathcal{F}_{t}
and
\mathcal{G}_{t}
.
Alternatively, the censoring condition may be formulated as in Assumption
5.2.1
below.

An equivalent definition of the conditional hazards,
h_{s}
and
\bm{h}_{s}
, is that

N_{t}^{*}-\int_{0}^{t}\mathbbl{1}(T^{*}\geq s)h_{s}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{s})\mathrm{d}s\qquad\text{and}\qquad N_{t}^{*}-\int_{0}^{t}\mathbbl{1}(T^{*}\geq s)\bm{h}_{s}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{s})\mathrm{d}s

are martingales with respect to the filtrations
\mathcal{F}_{t}^{*}
and
\mathcal{G}_{t}^{*}
, respectively. Independent right-censoring means that the conditional hazards also yield valid compensators of the observed counting process in the following sense.

Assumption 5.2.1

(Independent right-censoring)
.

It holds that

\displaystyle N_{t}-\int_{0}^{t}\mathbbl{1}(T\geq s)h_{s}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{s})\mathrm{d}s\qquad\text{and}\qquad N_{t}-\int_{0}^{t}\mathbbl{1}(T\geq s)\bm{h}_{s}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{s})\mathrm{d}s

are martingales with respect to the filtrations
\mathcal{F}_{t}
and
\mathcal{G}_{t}
, respectively.

All of our results regarding estimation and asymptotic theory will rely on Assumption
5.2.1
, and no other assumptions on censoring.

It is natural to ask if the ACM depends on the censoring distribution.
For the partially additive hazards model (
5.2.9
), the ACM is determined by the conditional hazard
\bm{h}

and is thus unaffected by the censoring distribution in this special case. However, the more general interpretations of the ACM in (
5.2.12
) and in Proposition
5.2.1
depend
a priori
on the censoring distribution via the at-risk indicator
Y_{t}
.
In view of (
5.2.12
), the ACM is unaffected by censoring if for all
t\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}[u(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})\,|\,C\geq t,T^{*}\geq t]}{\mathbb{E}[v(X_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})\,|\,C\geq t,T^{*}\geq t]}=\frac{\mathbb{E}[u(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})\,|\,T^{*}\geq t]}{\mathbb{E}[v(X_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})\,|\,T^{*}\geq t]},

where

\displaystyle u(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})

\displaystyle=(X_{t}-\pi_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}))(\bm{h}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})-h_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})),

\displaystyle v(X_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})

\displaystyle=(X_{t}-\pi_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}))^{2}.

This holds, for example, when
C\,\perp\!\!\!\perp\,X,Z,T^{*}
.

5.3
Estimation

We let
[n]=\{1,\ldots,n\}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
and assume that a sample of
n
observations,
(\mathbf{W}^{(i)})_{i\in[n]}=(T^{(i)},\Delta^{(i)},X^{(i)},Z^{(i)})_{i\in[n]}
, is available as independent copies of the template observation
\mathbf{W}\coloneq(T,\Delta,X,Z)
.
For each index
i\in[n]
, we use a superscript notation,
(i)
, for the corresponding quantities introduced in Section
5.2
. For example,
M_{t}^{(i)}=N_{t}^{(i)}-H_{t\wedge T^{(i)}}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t\wedge T^{(i)}}^{(i)})
is the
Z^{(i)}
-compensated counting process, where
N_{t}^{(i)}=\mathbbl{1}(T^{(i)}\leq t,\Delta^{(i)}=1)
.

We will use
cross-fitting
to construct an estimator of the ACM, but for simplicity we first describe our estimator based on single sample split
[n]=\mathcal{I}_{1}\cup\mathcal{I}_{2}
, where
\mathcal{I}_{1}
and
\mathcal{I}_{2}
are disjoint sets. For convenience we let
n_{1}\coloneq|\mathcal{I}_{1}|
and
n_{2}\coloneq|\mathcal{I}_{2}|
, and we assume that
\min\{n_{1},n_{2}\}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
.
The data indexed by
\mathcal{I}_{1}
, henceforth called the
training data
, is used to compute estimates
\widehat{\pi}_{t}

and
\widehat{h}_{t}
of the functions
\pi_{t}
and
h_{t}
.

The remaining data
(\mathbf{W}_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}
is used to compute the estimate of ACM by the following
three-step procedure:

1.

Compute an estimate of
\rho(\cdot)
, as defined by (
5.2.6
). Such an estimate could be

\tilde{\rho}(t)=\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}Y_{t}^{(i)}\big(X_{t}^{(i)}-\widehat{\pi}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}^{(i)})\big)^{2}.

(5.3.16)

Since we need to divide by an estimate of
\rho(\cdot)
, it will be convenient to consider a clipped estimate of the form
\widehat{\rho}(\cdot)=\max\{\tilde{\rho}(\cdot),c\}
for some
c>0
. We return to this discussion in both the general theory and for practical considerations.

2.

Compute estimates of the residual process and
Z^{(i)}
-compensated counting process by

\displaystyle\widehat{G}_{t}^{(i)}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\rho}(t)^{-1}Y_{t}^{(i)}(X_{t}^{(i)}-\widehat{\pi}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}^{(i)})),

(5.3.17)

\displaystyle\lx@scalerel@obj{\hat{\lx@scalerel@obj{M}}}_{t}^{(i)}

\displaystyle=N_{t}^{(i)}-\int_{0}^{t}Y_{s}\,\widehat{h}_{s}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{s}^{(i)})\mathrm{d}s,

for each index
i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}
.

3.

Compute the ACM estimator, denoted
\widehat{\gamma}=(\widehat{\gamma}_{t})
, by

\widehat{\gamma}_{t}=\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\widehat{G}_{s}^{(i)}\mathrm{d}\lx@scalerel@obj{\hat{\lx@scalerel@obj{M}}}_{s}^{(i)},\qquad t\in[0,1].

(5.3.18)

While the formula (
5.3.18
) is the same for the LCM estimator proposed in
Christgau et al. (2023b)
, there is an important technical distinction:
the residual estimate
\widehat{G}_{t}^{(i)}
cannot be determined from the training data and
i
-th observation alone, since the estimate
\widehat{\rho}
is based on all of the data indexed by
\mathcal{I}_{2}
.
Thus the residual estimates are not conditionally independent given the training data, which makes the theoretical analysis of the estimator a bit more involved. See the discussion in Section
5.6
for an elaboration regarding this difference.

A general procedure for computing the ACM estimator is described in Algorithm
1
. There are additional numerical challenges when computing the ACM estimator in practice. The estimator (
5.3.18
) is, for instance, defined as an integral, and its practical evaluation may require numerical integration. We return to this practical discussion in
Section
5.5.1
.

Algorithm 1

Single split estimate of ACM

input
: sample
(T^{(i)},\Delta^{(i)},X^{(i)},Z^{(i)})_{i\in[n]}
and partition
\mathcal{I}_{1}\cup\mathcal{I}_{2}=[n]
;

1

options
: regression methods for estimation of
h_{t}(\cdot)
and
\pi_{t}(\cdot)
;

2

begin

3

fit conditional hazard
\widehat{h}_{t}(\cdot)
by regressing
(T^{(i)},\Delta^{(i)})_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{1}}
onto
(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu^{(i)}_{t})_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{1}}
;

4

fit projection
\widehat{\pi}_{t}(\cdot)
by regressing
(X^{(i)}_{t})_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{1}}
onto
(T^{(i)},\Delta^{(i)},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu^{(i)}_{t})_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{1}}
;

5

compute
\widehat{\rho}(\cdot)
using
\widehat{\pi}_{t}(\cdot)
and
(T^{(i)},\Delta^{(i)},X^{(i)},Z^{(i)})_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}
;

6

compute
(\widehat{G}^{(i)},\lx@scalerel@obj{\hat{\lx@scalerel@obj{M}}}^{(i)})_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}
according to (
5.3.17
);

7

output
: the ACM estimate
\widehat{\gamma}_{t}=\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\widehat{G}_{s}^{(i)}\mathrm{d}\lx@scalerel@obj{\hat{\lx@scalerel@obj{M}}}_{s}^{(i)}
;

5.3.1
Cross-fitted ACM estimator

The ACM estimator defined in (
5.3.18
), and described in Algorithm
1
, is based on a single data split
[n]=\mathcal{I}_{1}\cup\mathcal{I}_{2}
and only uses the data indexed by
\mathcal{I}_{2}
for estimation of the target. The more sophisticated technique of 2-fold cross-fitting produces an additional estimate by swapping the roles of
\mathcal{I}_{1}
and
\mathcal{I}_{2}
and aggregates the two estimates by averaging. More generally,
K
-fold cross-fitting partitions the data into
K
disjoint folds, and cyclically uses each fold for estimation of the ACM. We assume that the folds have sizes
at least
\lfloor{n/K}\rfloor
and at most
\lceil{n/K}\rfloor
.
The procedure is described in detail in Algorithm
2
, and the resulting cross-fitted estimator is referred to as the
x-acm
and is denoted by
\widecheck{\gamma}
. We refer to
Chernozhukov et al. (2018)
and references therein for a more general description of cross-fitting.

Algorithm 2

K
-fold cross-fitted
x-acm

input
: sample
(T^{(i)},\Delta^{(i)},X^{(i)},Z^{(i)})_{i\in[n]}
and partition
J_{1}\cup\cdots\cup J_{K}=[n]
;

1

options
: options for Algorithm
1
;

2

begin

3

for

k=1,\ldots,K

do

4

compute
\widehat{\gamma}^{k}
using Algorithm
1
with
\mathcal{I}_{1}=\bigcup_{\ell\neq k}J_{\ell}
and
\mathcal{I}_{2}=J_{k}
;

5

output
: the
x-acm
estimate
\widecheck{\gamma}=\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\widehat{\gamma}^{k}

5.4
Asymptotic theory

We present in this section asymptotic representations of the estimation errors,

\widehat{\gamma}-\gamma
and
\widecheck{\gamma}-\gamma
, for the sample split and cross-fit
estimators. We leverage these representations to derive asymptotic Gaussian process limits,
which can be used for statistical inference.
The asymptotic properties of the general LCM estimator were analyzed in
Christgau et al. (2023b)
for the specific purpose of understanding type I and type II errors of the
Local Covariance Test
– a test of local independence based on the LCM estimator. In
Christgau et al. (2023b)
it was established, under suitable regularity conditions, that:

•

The asymptotic limit of the LCM estimator is a Gaussian martingale under the null hypothesis
\gamma=0
.

•

The LCM estimator is
\sqrt{n}
-consistent using the
additive residual process

X_{t}-\Pi_{t}
.

These results cannot directly be applied to our proposed ACM estimator, as the residual estimates in (
5.3.17
) are constructed with a mutual dependence via
\widehat{\rho}
. However, by tailoring the theoretical analysis to the residual process given by (
5.2.7
), and its corresponding estimates in (
5.3.17
), we establish the asymptotic Gaussian process limit of
\sqrt{n_{2}}(\widehat{\gamma}-\gamma)
, also under the alternative
\gamma\neq 0
.

It will be instructive and useful for the general analysis to first understand the asymptotics of the ACM estimator in the simplified scenario where
\rho
is assumed known. In this case we can largely use the results from
Christgau et al. (2023b)
, and we can then in Section
5.4.2
combine these results with a decomposition
of the full estimation error to arrive at the general results when
\rho
is estimated.

5.4.1
Asymptotics with a
\rho
-oracle

In this section we analyze the asymptotics of the
\rho
-oracle estimator

\displaystyle\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}\coloneq\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{Y_{t}^{(i)}(X_{t}^{(i)}-\widehat{\pi}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}^{(i)}))}{\rho(s)}\mathrm{d}\lx@scalerel@obj{\hat{\lx@scalerel@obj{M}}}_{s}^{(i)},\qquad t\in[0,1].

(5.4.19)

That is, we assume that
\rho
is known and not estimated.
Since the terms in the sum are conditionally i.i.d. given
(\widehat{\pi}_{t},\widehat{h}_{t})
– and these nuisance estimates are obtained from the training data only – the estimator
\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}
fits into the framework of
Christgau et al. (2023b)
.
While
Christgau et al. (2023b)
did not establish an asymptotic limit in the alternative
\gamma\neq 0
, we can follow their initial asymptotic analysis.
To this end, it will be convenient to first define the processes

\displaystyle E_{t}

\displaystyle\coloneq Y_{t}(X_{t}-\Pi_{t})=\rho(t)G_{t},

(5.4.20)

\displaystyle\widehat{E}_{t}

\displaystyle\coloneq Y_{t}(X_{t}-\widehat{\pi}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}))=\widehat{\rho}(t)\widehat{G}_{t}.

(5.4.21)

The following assumption is essentially a reformulation of Assumption 4.1 in
(
Christgau et al., 2023b
)
.

Assumption 5.4.1

(Boundedness)
.

There exist constants
C_{\bm{h}},C_{E},c_{\rho}>0
such that for each
t\in[0,1]
:

(i)

Y_{t}\max\big\{\bm{h}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}),\ \widehat{h}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})\big\}\leq C_{\bm{h}}
.

(ii)

\max\{|E_{t}|,|\widehat{E}_{t}|\}\leq C_{E}
.

(iii)

\rho(t)\geq c_{\rho}
.

A few remarks regarding Assumption
5.4.1
are in order.

Remark 2

(Remarks on boundedness assumptions)
.

1.

From the innovation theorem it follows that

Y_{t}\bm{h}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})\leq C_{\bm{h}}\implies Y_{t}h_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})\leq C_{\bm{h}}.

Since hazards are non-negative, the assumption
(i)
also entails the bounds
Y_{t}|\bm{h}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})-h_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})|\leq C_{\bm{h}}
and
Y_{t}|h_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})-\widehat{h}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})|\leq C_{\bm{h}}
.x

2.

If the exposure process takes values in an interval, that is,
X_{t}\in[a,b]
, then also
\Pi_{t}\in[a,b]
and it is reasonable to impose
\widehat{\pi}_{t}\in[a,b]
.
In this case,
(ii)
is satisfied with
C_{E}=b-a
.

3.

Note that

\rho(t)=\mathbb{P}(T\geq t)\mathbb{E}[(X_{t}-\Pi_{t})^{2}\,|\,T\geq t].

Thus Assumption
5.4.1
(iii) is equivalent to the two conditions:

\mathbb{P}(T\geq 1)>0
and
\inf_{t\in[0,1]}\mathbb{E}[(X_{t}-\Pi_{t})^{2}\,|\,T\geq t]>0
. The first is natural in order to perform statistical inference up to time
t=1
.
The second condition should also not be surprising: even for the partially additive model we would need

\mathbb{E}[(X_{t}-\Pi_{t})^{2}\,|\,T\geq t]>0
for all
t\in[0,1]
to identify the coefficient
\theta_{t}
.

In case that
(iii)
appears to be violated for the observed data, it is always possible to restrict the observation window and target the ACM over a shorter timespan.

To establish convergence it is, of course, also necessary to control the estimation error of the nuisance parameters.

Assumption 5.4.2

(Analogous to Assumption 4.2 in
Christgau et al. (2023b)
)
.

With

\displaystyle a_{n}\coloneq\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}\Big[Y_{t}(\widehat{\pi}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})-\pi_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}))^{2}\Big]\mathrm{d}t,\quad\text{and}\quad b_{n}\coloneq\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}\Big[Y_{t}(\widehat{h}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})-h_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}))^{2}\Big]\mathrm{d}t,

it holds that

\displaystyle\max\{a_{n},b_{n},n_{2}\cdot a_{n}b_{n}\}\longrightarrow 0

as
n\to\infty
.

Under these assumptions, it follows that
\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}
is asymptotically equivalent to the corresponding oracle term, where the nuisance estimates
(\widehat{\pi}_{t},\widehat{h}_{t})
are replaced with
(\pi_{t},h_{t})
.

Proposition 5.4.1
.

Under Assumptions
5.4.1
and
5.4.2
, it holds that

\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|\sqrt{n_{2}}\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}-U_{t}|\xrightarrow{P}0

as
n\to\infty
, where
(U_{t})
is the oracle process given by

U_{t}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}G_{s}^{(i)}\mathrm{d}M_{s}^{(i)},\qquad t\in[0,1].

The proof of Proposition
5.4.1
is largely based on the results of
Christgau et al. (2023b)
, with a few minor modifications.
Under Assumptions
5.4.1
and
5.4.2
, it is possible to establish that the process
U-\sqrt{n_{2}}\gamma
converges in distribution to a continuous Gaussian process by appealing to a central limit theorem in Skorokhod space. However, as we are primarily interested in the actual ACM estimator,
\widehat{\gamma}
, we return to the asymptotics of the process
(U_{t})
in the proof of Theorem
5.4.4
, see Section
5.A.6
in the supplement, where it is analyzed in combination with another non-vanishing term.

Remark 3
.

It should be possible to weaken the rate requirements to the empirical errors. To wit, if we define

\displaystyle\tilde{a}_{n}

\displaystyle\coloneq\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}\int_{0}^{1}Y_{t}^{(i)}(\widehat{\pi}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}^{(i)})-\pi_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu^{(i)}))^{2}\mathrm{d}t,

\displaystyle\tilde{b}_{n}

\displaystyle\coloneq\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}\int_{0}^{1}Y_{t}^{(i)}(\widehat{h}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu^{(i)})-h_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu^{(i)}))^{2}\mathrm{d}t,

then Assumption
5.4.2
can likely be replaced by the weaker condition that

\max\{\tilde{a}_{n},\tilde{b}_{n},n_{2}\cdot\tilde{a}_{n}\tilde{b}_{n}\}\xrightarrow{P}0,\qquad n\to\infty.

In fact,
(\tilde{a}_{n},\tilde{b}_{n})\xrightarrow{P}0
is equivalent with
(a_{n},b_{n})=(\mathbb{E}[\tilde{a}_{n}],\mathbb{E}[\tilde{b}_{n}])\to 0
under Assumption
5.4.1
due to uniform integrability.
The scaled product error,
n_{2}\cdot\tilde{a}_{n}\tilde{b}_{n}
, is not
a priori
uniformly integrable, so its convergence does not imply that
n_{2}\cdot a_{n}b_{n}\to 0
.

5.4.2
Asymptotics when
\rho
is estimated

To analyze the ACM estimator in the general case, where
\rho
is estimated, we consider the decomposition

\displaystyle\sqrt{n_{2}}\cdot\widehat{\gamma}=\sqrt{n_{2}}\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}+B,

(5.4.22)

where
\widetilde{\gamma}
is the
\rho
-oracle estimator defined as in (
5.4.19
), and where
B
is the process given by

\displaystyle B_{t}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\left(\frac{1}{\widehat{\rho}(s)}-\frac{1}{\rho(s)}\right)\widehat{E}_{s}^{(i)}\mathrm{d}\lx@scalerel@obj{\hat{\lx@scalerel@obj{M}}}_{s}^{(i)}.

In Proposition
5.4.1
we found an asymptotically equivalent oracle process for
\sqrt{n_{2}}\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}
.
We proceed to find an asymptotically equivalent oracle process for
B
, and then we identify the limit distribution of the sum of the oracle processes.
The decompositions of
\widetilde{\gamma}
and
B
into oracle terms and remainder terms can be found in the proofs of Proposition
5.4.1
and Theorem
5.4.3
.

In view of Assumption
5.4.1

(iii)
, we will for our general theory, and in the residual estimates
\widehat{G}^{(i)}
, consider the clipped estimator
\widehat{\rho}
given by

\widehat{\rho}(t)=\max\{c_{\widehat{\rho}},\tilde{\rho}(t)\},

(5.4.23)

for a
c_{\widehat{\rho}}>0
and where
\tilde{\rho}
is the empirical estimate from (
5.3.16
). We assume that
c_{\widehat{\rho}}
is chosen such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}c_{\widehat{\rho}}=0
and
c_{\widehat{\rho}}^{-1}=o(\sqrt{n_{2}})
, or in other words,
c_{\widehat{\rho}}
tends to zero, but slower than
1/\sqrt{n_{2}}
. In particular, this implies that
c_{\widehat{\rho}}<c_{\rho}

eventually, and thus the event
(\widehat{\rho}\neq\tilde{\rho})
has vanishing probability.

To control the error of
\widehat{\rho}
with a sufficiently fast rate, we require not only that
\widehat{\pi}_{t}
is estimated consistently, but that it can be estimated with a
n^{1/4}
-rate.

Assumption 5.4.3
.

With
a_{n}^{\prime}\coloneq\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{t}(\widehat{\pi}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})-\pi_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}))^{4}\big]\mathrm{d}t,

it holds that
n_{2}a_{n}^{\prime}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.

This assumption is analogous to standard assumptions in the partially linear model, cf. Assumption 4.1(e)(ii) in
Chernozhukov et al. (2018)
.
However, we have assumed the rate on the slightly more restrictive 4-norm.
We believe that this rate requirement could be relaxed to
\sqrt{n_{2}}a_{n}\to 0
under (potentially) additional mild assumptions. The reason that
a_{n}^{\prime}
simplifies the asymptotic analysis is that it controls the estimation error of
\widehat{\rho}
in terms of the 2-norm.

Proposition 5.4.2
.

Under Assumptions
5.4.1
and
5.4.3
,
it holds that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}n_{2}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}\big[(\widehat{\rho}(t)-\rho(t))^{2}\big]\mathrm{d}t\leq\frac{C_{E}^{4}}{4}.

(5.4.24)

The following result describes the asymptotics of
B
.

Theorem 5.4.3
.

Under Assumptions
5.4.1
,
5.4.2
, and
5.4.3
, it holds that

\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|B_{t}-V_{t}|\xrightarrow{P}0,

where
V=(V_{t})
is the process given by

\displaystyle V_{t}=\sqrt{n_{2}}\gamma_{t}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{(E_{s}^{(i)})^{2}}{\rho(s)}\mathbb{E}[G_{s}(\bm{h}_{s}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{s},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{s})-h_{s}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{s}))]\mathrm{d}s.

The proof of Theorem
5.4.3
does not readily follow from any of the results in
Christgau et al. (2023b)
, and can be dissected into two novel steps: first control the approximation

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{\widehat{\rho}(s)}-\frac{1}{\rho(s)}\right)\!\left(X_{s}^{(i)}-\widehat{\pi}_{s}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{s}^{(i)})\right)\mathrm{d}\lx@scalerel@obj{\hat{\lx@scalerel@obj{M}}}_{s}^{(i)}\approx\frac{\rho(s)-\widehat{\rho}(s)}{\rho(s)^{2}}(X_{s}^{(i)}-\Pi_{s}^{(i)})\mathrm{d}M_{s}^{(i)},

asymptotically (Proposition
5.A.2
), and then use a functional law of large numbers to reduce all stochasticity to the stochastics from
\rho-\widehat{\rho}
(Proposition
5.A.3
).

To summarize, we have considered the decomposition

\sqrt{n_{2}}\cdot\widehat{\gamma}=\sqrt{n_{2}}\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}+B
, and established under Assumptions
5.4.1
,
5.4.2
, and
5.4.3
that

\displaystyle\sqrt{n_{2}}\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}=U+o_{P}(n^{-1/2})\quad\text{and}\quad B=V+o_{P}(n^{-1/2}).

In view of (
5.2.13
) we can regard
\gamma
as a signed measure on
[0,1]
with Radon-Nikodym derivative

\frac{\mathrm{d}\gamma_{s}}{\mathrm{d}s}\coloneq\mathbb{E}[G_{s}(\bm{h}_{s}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{s},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{s})-h_{s}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{s}))],

which gives that

\displaystyle U_{t}+V_{t}-\sqrt{n_{2}}\gamma_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}\left(\int_{0}^{t}G_{s}^{(i)}\mathrm{d}M_{s}^{(i)}-\int_{0}^{t}E_{s}^{(i)}G_{s}^{(i)}\mathrm{d}\gamma_{s}\right).

(5.4.25)

The integrand ‘
G_{s}^{(i)}\mathrm{d}M_{s}^{(i)}-E_{s}^{(i)}G_{s}^{(i)}\mathrm{d}\gamma_{s}
’ corresponds to the oracle term of a partially linear model on an ‘infinitesimal scale’. Theorem
5.A.6
in the supplement establishes asymptotic normality of (
5.4.25
) based on a central limit theorem in Skorokhod space.

Combining Proposition
5.4.1
, Theorem
5.4.3
, and Theorem
5.A.6
, we obtain asymptotic Gaussianity of the ACM estimator and the
x-acm
,
\widecheck{\gamma}
, described in Section
5.3.1
.

Theorem 5.4.4
.

Let
\Gamma=(\Gamma_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
be a continuous Gaussian process with mean zero and the same covariance function as the process

\big(\int_{0}^{t}G_{s}\mathrm{d}M_{s}-\int_{0}^{t}E_{s}G_{s}\mathrm{d}\gamma_{s}\big)_{t\in[0,1]}
.

Under Assumptions
5.4.1
,
5.4.2
, and
5.4.3
, it holds that

\displaystyle\sqrt{n_{2}}(\;\!\widehat{\gamma}-\gamma)\xrightarrow{d}\Gamma\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sqrt{n}(\;\!\widecheck{\gamma}-\gamma)\xrightarrow{d}\Gamma

with respect to the uniform topology as
n\to\infty
.

Note that the cross-fitted estimate is scaled with
\sqrt{n}
rather than
\sqrt{n_{2}}
, and is thus more efficient than the single-split estimator
\widehat{\gamma}
.

Remark 4
.

Having established asymptotic Gaussianity of the ACM estimator, it is natural to ask if the (co)variance function of
\Gamma
can be estimated in order to perform statistical inference for
\gamma
.
Let
\mathcal{V}
be the variance function of the Gaussian process
\Gamma
. Under the null hypothesis that
\gamma=0
,
\Gamma
is a martingale and

\mathcal{V}(t)=\var\left(\int_{0}^{t}G_{s}\mathrm{d}M_{s}\right)=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{t}G_{s}^{2}\mathrm{d}N_{s}\right],

which can be estimated by

\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}\ \left(\int_{0}^{t}\widehat{G}_{s}^{(i)}\mathrm{d}\lx@scalerel@obj{\hat{\lx@scalerel@obj{M}}}_{s}^{(i)}\right)^{2}\quad\text{or}\quad\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}(\widehat{G}_{s}^{(i)})^{2}\mathrm{d}N_{s}^{(i)},

cf. Proposition 4.7 in
Christgau et al. (2023b)
. However, if the main purpose is to test
the null hypothesis, it suffices to use the LCM with the additive residual process
X_{t}-\Pi_{t}
that omits the scaling by
\rho
and thus avoids estimation of
\rho
. Then, a test of this null can be carried out using the X-LCT from
Christgau et al. (2023b)
. However, if a pointwise confidence band for the ACM estimator is desired, we surmise that

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{V}}(t)=\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\widehat{G}_{s}^{(i)}\mathrm{d}\lx@scalerel@obj{\hat{\lx@scalerel@obj{M}}}_{s}^{(i)}-\int_{0}^{t}\widehat{E}_{s}^{(i)}\widehat{G}_{s}^{(i)}\mathrm{d}\widehat{\gamma}_{s}\right)^{2}

is a consistent estimator of
\mathcal{V}(t)
, though a proof is outstanding.

5.5
Simulation study

Simulations were conducted in the special case with time-independent
X
and
Z
, and with administrative censoring
C=1
.
Two different settings were considered for the data generating process:

(
\mathcal{S}_{\text{lin}}
)

An Aalen additive model with

\displaystyle Z=(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{d})

\displaystyle\sim\mathrm{Unif}(0,1)^{\otimes d},

\displaystyle X\,|\,Z

\displaystyle\sim\mathrm{Unif}(Z_{1},Z_{1}+1),

\displaystyle\bm{h}_{t}^{\mathrm{A}}(X,Z)

\displaystyle=2t(1+X+\beta^{\top}Z),

where the coefficient
\beta=(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{d})
is sampled once and independently for each dataset, with
(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{4})\sim\mathrm{Dirichlet}(\mathbf{1}_{4})

and
\beta_{i}=0
for
5\leq i\leq d
.

(
\mathcal{S}_{\text{par}}
)

A partially additive model with

\displaystyle Z=(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{d})

\displaystyle\sim\mathrm{N}(0,1)^{\otimes d},

\displaystyle X\,|\,Z

\displaystyle\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\varphi(Z_{1}),\varphi(Z_{1})+1),

\displaystyle\bm{h}_{t}^{\mathrm{pA}}(X,Z)

\displaystyle=2t(1+X+\varphi(Z_{1})),

where
\varphi(t)=\exp(-2t^{2})
is a scaled Gaussian density.

Note that both settings adhere to the partially additive model (
5.2.9
), and in this case the ACM coincides with the cumulative direct effect
\Theta_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}2s\mathrm{d}s=t
.
The uncensored survival time
T^{*}
can in this case be simulated by

T^{*}\coloneq\frac{\sqrt{E}}{\sqrt{1+X+f(Z)}},

where
E\,\perp\!\!\!\perp\,X,Z
is standard exponentially distributed, and where
f(z)

equals
\beta^{\top}z
for the setting
\mathcal{S}_{\text{lin}}
, and equals
\varphi(z_{1})
for the setting
\mathcal{S}_{\text{par}}
.

The simulations were performed with covariate dimensions
d\in\{4,16\}
and datasets of sample sizes
n\in\{200,600,1800\}
.
For each setting
N=500
datasets were simulated.

5.5.1
Estimators and implementation

The simulation study was implemented in Python.
As a benchmark, an estimator of the exposure coefficient in the Aalen additive model was considered, which was implemented using
AalenAdditiveFitter
in the
lifelines
package
(
Davidson-Pilon, 2019
)
.
This estimator is referred to as ‘Aalen estimator’ and denoted by
\widehat{A}=(\widehat{A}_{t})
.

Estimation of the ACM requires methods for estimating the predictable projection and the conditional hazard. For estimation of
\pi_{t}
we implemented the following procedure.

1.

Instantiate a grid
\mathcal{T}=(\frac{k}{n_{\mathcal{T}}-1}\colon k=0,1,\ldots,n_{\mathcal{T}}-1)
. Simulations were conducted with
n_{\mathcal{T}}=20
.

2.

Let
[n]=\mathcal{I}_{1}\cup\mathcal{I}_{2}
be a given sample-split, and
let
x_{it}\coloneq(t,Y_{t}^{(i)},Z_{1t}^{(i)},\ldots,Z_{dt}^{(i)})
, where

Z_{jt}^{(i)}
denote the
j
-th covariate for the
i
-th individual (independent of
t
in this setting). Then format:

•

covariate matrices
\mathbb{X}_{\text{train}}\coloneq(x_{it})_{(i,t)\in\mathcal{I}_{1}\times\mathcal{T}}
and

\mathbb{X}_{\text{test}}\coloneq(x_{it})_{(i,t)\in\mathcal{I}_{2}\times\mathcal{T}}
of dimensions
(|\mathcal{I}_{1}|\cdot n_{\mathcal{T}})\times(d+2)
and
(|\mathcal{I}_{2}|\cdot n_{\mathcal{T}})\times(d+2)
, respectively.

•

a response vector
\mathbb{Y}_{\text{train}}\coloneq(X_{t}^{(i)})_{(t,i)\in\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{I}_{1}}
of length
n_{\mathcal{T}}\cdot|\mathcal{I}_{1}|
.

3.

Regress
\mathbb{Y}_{\text{train}}
onto
\mathbb{X}_{\text{train}}
using any regression method. For this we considered OLS and
gradient boosting

(
Friedman, 2001
;
Hastie et al., 2009
)
, which are denoted by a subscript
lin
and
gb
, respectively. These were implemented using classes
LinearRegression
and
GradientBoostingRegressor
from the Scikit-learn package
(
Pedregosa et al., 2011
)
.

4.

Use the fitted methods to predict response values for
\mathbb{X}_{\text{test}}
to produce estimates of the predictable projection over the grid
\mathcal{T}
for each test observation.

5.

Interpolate to general time points
t\in[0,1]
using the estimated value at time
t_{\leq}=\max\{s\in\mathcal{T}\,|\,s\leq t\}
.

It is also possible to subset matrices in step
(2)
to pairs
(i,t)
such that
(T^{(i)}>t)
. This corresponds to directly targeting the quantity
\pi_{t}(Z)=\mathbb{E}[X\,|\,Y_{t}=1,Z]
, rather than targeting
\mathbb{E}[X\,|\,Y_{t},Z_{t}]
as done in the steps
(3)
and
(4)
. Both methods were tried initially, with no noteworthy differences in the analysis result observed.
For the interpolation in step
(5)
, it is important that the method preserves predictability, which would not be ensured by, e.g.,
linear interpolation
.

For conditional hazard estimation, we considered two off-the-shelf implementations:

GradientBoostingSurvivalAnalysis
from the scikit-survival module
(
Pölsterl, 2020
)
and the
AalenAdditiveFitter
from the
lifelines
package
(
Davidson-Pilon, 2019
)
. The two methods are again indicated by a subscript
gb
and
lin
, respectively.

The estimate
\widehat{\rho}
was computed with the clipping value
c_{\widehat{\rho}}=0.005
. However, the estimate was set to ‘
nan
’ rather than
c_{\widehat{\rho}}
whenever
\tilde{\rho}(t)<c_{\widehat{\rho}}
. This is similar to how the
AalenAdditiveFitter
returns
nan
for times
t
such that
\sum_{i}Y_{t}^{(i)}<3d
.

The
x-acm
was implemented with 4-fold cross-fitting according to Algorithm
2
. In addition, a version of the ACM estimator without any sample splitting was implemented, and this estimator is referred to as the
n-acm
.
Thus, 4 different estimators of the ACM were implemented:

\textsc{n-acm}_{\texttt{lin}}
,

\textsc{x-acm}_{\texttt{lin}}
,

\textsc{n-acm}_{\texttt{gb}}
, and

\textsc{x-acm}_{\texttt{gb}}
.

5.5.2
Results

Figure 5.5.1
:
Scaled
RMSE
for various estimators with respect to the cumulative direct effect.

Figure
5.5.1
shows the scaled root mean square error
RMSE
for various estimators with respect to the cumulative direct effect at time
t=67/127\approx 0.622
. This timepoint was the largest, for which all estimators yielded well-defined estimates for all the simulated datasets (the Aalen estimator yielded
nan
at time
t=68/127
for a dataset in the additive setting with
n=200
and
d=16
).
We observed initially that the ACM estimators based on linear regression methods, namely
\textsc{n-acm}_{\texttt{lin}}
and
\textsc{x-acm}_{\texttt{lin}}
, behaved similarly to the Aalen estimate
\widehat{A}_{t}
. In view of this fact, and since our focus is on estimation of the ACM using flexible learning methods, the results for linear methods are omitted for more clear visualization.

In the left column of Figure
5.5.1
, corresponding to the setting
\mathcal{S}_{\text{lin}}
, we observe that the Aalen estimator and
\textsc{x-acm}_{\texttt{gb}}
have similar performance and appear to be
\sqrt{n}
-consistent.
It is expected that both these estimators are
\sqrt{n}
-consistent, but it is perhaps surprising that the
\textsc{x-acm}_{\texttt{gb}}
is as efficient as the Aalen estimator in the linear setting. The small discrepancy might be due to numerical approximations when fitting the estimators to the same common grid, or difference in bias-variance tradeoff because of (implicit) penalization.

The estimator
\textsc{n-acm}_{\texttt{gb}}
without sample splitting performs significantly worse, although the discrepancy seems to diminish for larger sample sizes.
In theory, sample-splitting is used to control the empirical process terms
13
13

13

In our analysis, these are the terms denoted by
R^{(1)},R^{(2)},R^{(4)},r_{1},\tilde{R}^{(2)}
, and
\tilde{R}^{(4)}
found in the supplementary Section
5
.
, but these terms can also be handled by Donsker conditions or algorithmic stability, see for example the discussions in
Chernozhukov et al. (2018)
;
Chen et al. (2022)
. This indicates, that such Donsker conditions are violated in our setup, and that the gradient boosting methods are not sufficiently stable.
In practice, overfitting can lead to biases that would otherwise not appear with sample-splitting
(
Chernozhukov et al., 2018
)
.
Since the gradient boosted methods are fitted with the same hyper parameters, and these were tuned towards the larger sample sizes, it is plausible that they overfit in the smaller sample sizes, resulting in a systematic bias.

We observe that each of the ACM estimators perform similarly across the linear setting
\mathcal{S}_{\text{lin}}
and in the partial setting
\mathcal{S}_{\text{par}}
. This is different for the Aalen estimator
\widehat{A}_{t}
, which fails severely in estimating the effect
\Theta_{t}
.
This is to be expected, as the Aalen additive model is misspecified, and as a consequence,
\widehat{A}_{t}
cannot reasonable account for the nonlinear effect
\varphi(Z_{1})
.

In summary, we conclude that
\textsc{x-acm}_{\texttt{gb}}
performs well across all settings, indicating that it provides an efficient and robust estimator of the cumulative direct effect.

5.6
Discussion

The ACM was proposed as a model-free estimand to quantify the conditional relationship between a time-to-event and an exposure, controlling for given covariates.
While it is a specific instance of the more general LCM introduced by
Christgau et al. (2023b)
, we have highlighted several unique properties and results that apply to the ACM – and not necessarily to the LCM – which were not considered in
Christgau et al. (2023b)
.

The ACM may be understood as a weighted hazard difference estimand, and we showed in Proposition
5.2.1
that it coincides with the cumulative exposure coefficient in the partially additive hazards model (
5.2.9
) that best approximates the true hazard in
L^{2}
. As a result, the ACM measures the cumulative direct effect within partially additive hazards model, and in particular within the Aalen additive model.

The asymptotic analysis for our proposed estimator, the
x-acm
, was to some extent based on results from
Christgau et al. (2023b)
. However, a novel examination of the term
B
in (
5.4.22
) was necessary to account for the estimation error in the function
\rho
appearing in the denominator. Notably, the remainder term
D^{(2)}
from
Christgau et al. (2023b)
, analogous to
R^{(4)}
in (
5.A.3
), does not vanish for the ACM when
\rho
is estimated. Moreover, the decomposition in (
3.4.17
) differs from typical decompositions of estimators related to the
partially linear model
, considered in, e.g.,

(
Robinson, 1988
;
Lundborg and Pfister, 2023
;
Hines et al., 2023
)
,
because these decompositions are not feasible when integrating over time.

Estimating
\rho
using the training data
.
Suppose that the function
\rho
is estimated using the training data, which corresponds to replacing
\mathcal{I}_{2}
with
\mathcal{I}_{1}
in (
5.3.16
).
Although the residual estimates would then fit within the framework of
Christgau et al. (2023b)
, the analysis does not simplify as the term
D^{(2)}
in
Christgau et al. (2023b)
still remains non-vanishing. Nevertheless, the analysis in Section
5.4.2
can be adapted to derive an oracle decomposition similar to (
5.4.25
), but with the integrals
\int_{0}^{t}E_{s}^{(i)}G_{s}^{(i)}\mathrm{d}\gamma_{s}
summed over
i\in\mathcal{I}_{1}
. This makes it unfeasible to aggregate the cross-fitted estimates, resulting in a smaller effective sample when using this approach. Additionally, controlling the estimation error of
\rho
in this case requires Donsker class conditions or algorithmic stability, cf. the term
r_{1}
in the proof of Proposition
5.4.2
. Our simulations in Section
5.5
suggest that such conditions may not hold in practice, at least when gradient boosting is used.

The cross-fitting scheme
.
The
x-acm
, described in Section
5.3.1
, employs cross-fitting similarly to the
dml
1 estimator described in
Chernozhukov et al. (2018)
. While it is conceivable that a cross-fitting scheme corresponding to
dml
2 – where numerators and denominators are aggregated separately across sample splits – could be implemented, the integration over time complicates this approach. If possible, however, such an approach might lead to increased numerical stability in small samples, cf. Remark 3.1 in
Chernozhukov et al. (2018)
.

Relaxations of assumptions
.
We already discussed, in Remark
3
, the possibility of relaxing the rate requirements to hold for the empirical errors instead of the expected errors.
The assumptions on boundedness, Assumptions
5.4.1

(i)
and
(ii)
,
can be found in similar works, cf. Theorem 2 in
Vansteelandt et al. (2022)
or Assumption 1 in
Hou et al. (2023)
. It is, however, likely that they can be replaced by suitable tail bounds or bounds on conditional variances, but at the cost of more technical proofs.
Finally, Assumption
5.4.3
states that
\Pi
can be estimated at an order
o(n^{-1/4})
in terms of the
4
-norm. While the order
o(n^{-1/4})
is to be expected, see
(
Chernozhukov et al., 2018
)
, it is conceivable that the condition can be relaxed to hold for the
2
-norm rather than the slightly more restrictive
4
-norm.

Alternative estimands.

A limitation of the ACM is the inverse weighting by the function
\rho
, potentially making it difficult to estimate accurately. An interesting direction for future work is to consider alternative ways of weighting the hazard difference.
This may lead to estimands that are easier to estimate, but still interpretable.
Such a compromise was considered by
Vansteelandt et al. (2022)
, who propose a hazard ratio estimand that deliberately avoids inverse weighting by the conditional density of the exposure. However, their estimand still requires inverse weighting by the cumulative hazard, which imposes a limitation similar to the inverse weighting with
\rho
.

The related works
Dukes et al. (2019)
;
Hou et al. (2023)
, cf. Table
5.1.1
, consider estimation of
\Theta_{t}
in (
5.2.9
), with time-independent exposure and covariates, based on (efficient) orthogonal score methodology. It would be interesting to investigate how the
x-acm
performs relatively to their methodology, both for a well-specified and misspecified model. Furthermore, supposing that their methodologies can be shown to target general model-free estimands, it would be interesting to compare these estimands with the ACM.

Supplement to ‘Assumption-lean Aalen regression’

The supplementary material consists of Section
5
, which contains proofs of the main results and
related auxiliary lemmas, and Section
5
, which includes a discussion of the ACM in the context of multiplicative hazards models.

5.A. Auxillary results and proofs

5.A.1
Additional notation for proofs

While the main manuscript is formulated using histories and the conditional hazards
h_{t}
and
\bm{h}_{t}
, it will be convenient to work with filtrations and intensities in the proofs, as also discussed in Remark
1
. Recall that
we define the filtrations
\mathcal{F}_{t}\coloneq\sigma(Z_{s},N_{s};s\leq t)
and
\mathcal{G}_{t}\coloneq\sigma(X_{s},Z_{s},N_{s};s\leq t)
.
The stochastic processes
\lambda=(\lambda_{t})
and
\bm{\lambda}=(\bm{\lambda}_{t})
, defined by

\displaystyle\lambda_{t}\coloneq Y_{t}h_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bm{\lambda}_{t}\coloneq Y_{t}\bm{h}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}),

are then, under independent right-censoring as described in Section
5.2.4
,
the
\mathcal{F}_{t}
- and
\mathcal{G}_{t}
-intensity, respectively, of
N_{t}
.
We remark that, by definition, this means that
M_{t}=N_{t}-\int_{0}^{t}\lambda_{s}\mathrm{d}s
is an
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-martingale and that

\displaystyle\bm{M}_{t}\coloneq N_{t}-\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\lambda}_{s}\mathrm{d}s

is a
\mathcal{G}_{t}
-martingale.
By the innovation theorem, it also holds that
\mathbb{E}[\bm{\lambda}_{t}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{t-}]=\lambda_{t}
.
This notation is consistent with
Christgau et al. (2023b)
.

For the proofs in the asymptotic analysis we will additionally use the following notation:

\displaystyle\widehat{\lambda}_{t}^{(i)}\coloneq Y_{t}^{(i)}\widehat{h}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu^{(i)})

\displaystyle\widehat{\Pi}_{t}^{(i)}\coloneq\widehat{\pi}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu^{(i)})

for
i\in[n]
and
t\in[0,1]
.
Finally, we let
\|\cdot\|_{p}
denote the norm on
L^{p}([0,1]\times\Omega,\mathrm{d}t\otimes\mathbb{P})
, so that, for example,

\displaystyle b_{n}=\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}\big[(\widehat{\lambda}_{t}-\lambda_{t})^{2}\big]\mathrm{d}t=\|\widehat{\lambda}-\lambda\|_{2}^{2}.

5.A.2
Proof of Proposition
5.2.1

We first consider the minimization objective for a fixed timepoint
t\in[0,1]
.

Lemma 5.A.1
.

For fixed
t\in[0,1]
, it holds that

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\vartheta_{t}^{\star}=\cov(G_{t},\bm{\lambda}_{t}-\lambda_{t})\\
g_{t}^{\star}=h_{t}-\cov(G_{t},\bm{\lambda}_{t}-\lambda_{t})\pi_{t}\end{cases}

(5.A.1)

are solutions to

\displaystyle\minimize_{\vartheta_{t}\in\real,g_{t}\in L^{0}(P_{\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}})}

\displaystyle\quad\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{t}\left(\bm{h}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})-(\vartheta_{t}X_{t}+g_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}))\right)^{2}\right]

where
P_{\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}}
denotes the distribution of
\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}
.
Moreover, the solution
\vartheta_{t}^{\star}
is unique.

Proof.

Recall that on the event
(Y_{t}=1)=(T\geq t)
, the predictable projection
\Pi_{t}
takes the value
\pi_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})=\mathbb{E}[X_{t}\,|\,T\geq t,\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}]
.
Since
\pi_{t},h_{t}\in L^{0}(P_{\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}})
, the minimization problem is equivalent to minimizing

\displaystyle u(\vartheta_{t},\ell_{t})

\displaystyle\coloneq\mathbb{E}\Big[Y_{t}\big(\bm{h}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})-h_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})-\vartheta_{t}(X_{t}-\pi_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}))-\ell_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})\big)^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\big(\bm{\lambda}_{t}-\lambda_{t}-\vartheta_{t}Y_{t}(X_{t}-\Pi_{t})-Y_{t}\ell_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})\big)^{2}\Big],

which corresponds to the substitution

g_{t}=\ell_{t}+h_{t}-\vartheta_{t}\pi_{t}
. Note that

\mathbb{E}[\bm{\lambda}_{t}-\lambda_{t}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{t-}]=0=\mathbb{E}[X_{t}-\Pi_{t}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{t-}].

Now, expanding the square in
u(\vartheta_{t},\ell_{t})
and using that
\mathbb{E}[\ell_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})^{2}]\geq 0
, we obtain that

\displaystyle u(\vartheta_{t},\ell_{t})

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}[(\bm{\lambda}_{t}-\lambda_{t})^{2}]+\vartheta_{t}^{2}\mathbb{E}[Y_{t}(X_{t}-\Pi_{t})^{2}]-2\vartheta_{t}\mathbb{E}[(\bm{\lambda}_{t}-\lambda_{t})(X_{t}-\Pi_{t})].

Equality is attained when
\ell_{t}=0
, which is equivalent to

g_{t}=h_{t}-\vartheta_{t}\pi_{t}\in L^{0}(P_{\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}})
. The right-hand side is a quadratic in
\vartheta_{t}
and its unique minimizer is given by

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}[(\bm{\lambda}_{t}-\lambda_{t})(X_{t}-\Pi_{t})]}{\mathbb{E}[Y_{t}(X_{t}-\Pi_{t})^{2}]}=\cov(G_{t},\bm{\lambda}_{t}-\lambda_{t}).

This shows that
(\vartheta_{t}^{\star},g_{t}^{\star})
in (
5.A.1
) are indeed minimizers and that
\vartheta^{\star}
is unique.
∎

Returning to the proof of Proposition
5.2.1
, we let
(\vartheta_{t}^{\star},g_{t}^{\star})
be the pointwise minimizers from (
5.A.1
) for each
t\in[0,1]
.
For any
(\vartheta,g)
as in Proposition
5.2.1
, it follows directly from Tonelli’s theorem and Lemma
5.A.1
that

minimization objective

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{t}\big(\bm{h}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})-\vartheta_{t}X_{t}-g_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})\big)^{2}\right]\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{t}\big(\bm{h}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})-\vartheta_{t}^{\star}X_{t}-g_{t}^{\star}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})\big)^{2}\right]\mathrm{d}t.

(5.A.2)

We conclude that
(\vartheta^{\star},g^{\star})
is indeed a minimizer to (
5.2.14
), since
\vartheta_{t}
is measurable and
g_{t}^{\star}\in L^{0}(P_{\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t}})
for each
t\in[0,1]
.
Note that equality occurs in (
5.A.2
)
if and only if

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{t}\big(\bm{h}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})-\vartheta_{t}X_{t}-g_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})\big)^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{t}\big(\bm{h}_{t}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muX\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t},\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})-\vartheta_{t}^{\star}X_{t}-g_{t}^{\star}(\mkern 2.0mu\overline{\mkern-2.0muZ\mkern 0.0mu}\mkern 0.0mu_{t})\big)^{2}\right]

for almost all
t\in[0,1]
. By the uniqueness of
\vartheta^{\star}
in Lemma
5.A.1
, this is equivalent to
\vartheta_{t}=\vartheta_{t}^{\star}
for almost all
t\in[0,1]
. The last part of the proposition now follows since
\gamma_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\vartheta_{s}^{\star}\mathrm{d}s
, according to (
5.2.12
).

\Box

5.A.3
Proof of Proposition
5.4.1

We consider a decomposition similar to that of
Christgau et al. (2023b)
, but with their non-vanishing processes combined. To this end, define the
\rho
-oracle residual estimates

\widetilde{G}_{t}\coloneq\frac{\widehat{E}_{t}}{\rho(t)}=\frac{Y_{t}(X_{t}-\widehat{\Pi}_{t})}{\rho(t)},\qquad t\in[0,1].

Then note that

\displaystyle\sqrt{n_{2}}\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}=U+R^{(1)}+R^{(2)}+R^{(3)}+R^{(4)},

(5.A.3)

where the processes
U
,
R^{(1)}
,
R^{(2)}
,
R^{(3)}
, and
R^{(4)}
are given by

\displaystyle U_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}G_{s}^{(i)}\mathrm{d}M_{s}^{(i)},

\displaystyle R^{(1)}_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}G_{s}^{(i)}\big(\lambda_{s}^{(i)}-\widehat{\lambda}_{s}^{(i)}\big)\mathrm{d}s,

\displaystyle R^{(2)}_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\big(\widetilde{G}_{s}^{(i)}-G_{s}^{(i)}\big)\mathrm{d}\bm{M}_{s}^{(i)},

\displaystyle R^{(3)}_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\big(\widetilde{G}_{s}^{(i)}-G_{s}^{(i)}\big)\big(\lambda_{s}^{(i)}-\widehat{\lambda}_{s}^{(i)}\big)\mathrm{d}s,

\displaystyle R_{t}^{(4)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\big(\widetilde{G}_{s}^{(i)}-G_{s}^{(i)}\big)\big(\bm{\lambda}_{s}^{(i)}-\lambda_{s}^{(i)}\big)\mathrm{d}s.

In view of this decomposition, it suffices to show that

\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|R_{t}^{(\ell)}|\xrightarrow{P}0

as
n\to\infty
for
\ell=1,\ldots,4
.
To this end, note that Assumption
5.4.1
ensures that Assumption 4.1 in
Christgau et al. (2023b)
holds with:

\displaystyle\max\{|G_{t}|,|\widetilde{G}_{t}|\}\leq c_{\rho}^{-1}C_{E},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\max\{\bm{\lambda}_{t},\widehat{\lambda}_{t}\}\leq C_{\bm{h}}.

Assumption
5.4.2
can be translated directly into Assumption 4.2 in
Christgau et al. (2023b)
.
We therefore conclude that
R^{(1)}
,
R^{(2)}
, and
R^{(3)}
converge (uniformly) to zero in probability by Proposition 4.4 in
Christgau et al. (2023b)
.
For the last remainder process we have that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|R_{t}^{(4)}|\leq c_{\rho}^{-1}\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\Big\lvert\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\big(\widehat{E}_{s}^{(i)}-E_{s}^{(i)}\big)\big(\bm{\lambda}_{s}^{(i)}-\lambda_{s}^{(i)}\big)\mathrm{d}s\Big\rvert\xrightarrow{P}0,

where the convergence in probability is established in Lemma A.10 in
Christgau et al. (2023b)
.

\Box

5.A.4
Proof of Proposition
5.4.2

For
n
sufficiently large it holds that
c_{\widehat{\rho}}<c_{\rho}
, in which case Assumption
5.4.1
(iii)
implies that
|\widehat{\rho}(t)-\rho(t)|\leq|\tilde{\rho}(t)-\rho(t)|
for all
t\in[0,1]
.
Hence it suffices to establish the bound for the empirical estimator
\tilde{\rho}
from (
5.3.16
). Note also that

\tilde{\rho}(t)=\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}\big(\widehat{E}_{t}^{(i)}\big)^{2},

and consider the following decomposition

\displaystyle\sqrt{n_{2}}\cdot(\tilde{\rho}(t)-\rho(t))=\sqrt{n_{2}}\cdot(\overline{\rho}-\rho)+2r_{1}(t)+r_{2}(t)

where

\displaystyle\overline{\rho}(t)

\displaystyle\coloneq\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}(E_{t}^{(i)})^{2},

\displaystyle r_{1}(t)

\displaystyle\coloneq\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}E_{t}^{(i)}(\widehat{E}_{t}^{(i)}-E_{t}^{(i)}),

\displaystyle r_{2}(t)

\displaystyle\coloneq\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}(\widehat{E}_{t}^{(i)}-E_{t}^{(i)})^{2}.

We first note that the terms in
r_{1}
are conditionally i.i.d. given
\mathcal{D}_{1}\coloneq\sigma(\mathbf{W}^{(i)}\colon i\in\mathcal{I}_{1})
with conditional mean zero.
It follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[r_{1}(t)^{2}\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\big[\,\var(r_{1}(t)\,|\,\mathcal{D}_{1})\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}\var\big(E_{t}^{(i)}(\widehat{E}_{t}^{(i)}-E_{t}^{(i)})\,|\,\mathcal{D}_{1}\big)\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\big[(E_{t}^{(i)})^{2}(\widehat{E}_{t}^{(i)}-E_{t}^{(i)})^{2}\big]\leq C_{E}^{2}\mathbb{E}[(\Pi_{t}-\widehat{\Pi}_{t})^{2}],

where we have used that
\widehat{E}_{t}^{(i)}-E_{t}^{(i)}=Y_{t}(X_{t}^{(i)}-\widehat{\Pi}_{t}^{(i)}-X_{t}^{(i)}+\Pi_{t}^{(i)})=Y_{t}(\Pi_{t}^{(i)}-\widehat{\Pi}_{t}^{(i)})
.
By integrating over time we obtain

\|r_{1}\|_{2}\leq C_{E}\|\widehat{\Pi}-\Pi\|_{2}\to 0.

(5.A.4)

For the other remainder, Cauchy-Schwarz yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[r_{2}(t)^{2}]=\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i,j\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}\mathbb{E}[(\widehat{E}_{t}^{(i)}-E_{t}^{(i)})^{2}(\widehat{E}_{t}^{(j)}-E_{t}^{(j)})^{2}]\leq n_{2}\mathbb{E}\big[(\widehat{E}_{t}-E_{t})^{4}\big].

By Assumption
5.4.3
we conclude that

\|r_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\leq n_{2}\|\widehat{E}-E\|_{4}^{4}=n_{2}\|\widehat{\Pi}-\Pi\|_{4}^{4}\longrightarrow 0,\qquad n\to\infty.

(5.A.5)

For the oracle sum,
\overline{\rho}
, we note that
\mathbb{E}[\overline{\rho}(t)]=\rho(t)
, and that
\overline{\rho}(t)
is a sum of i.i.d. random variables with the same distribution as
E_{t}^{2}\in[0,C_{E}^{2}]
.
Invoking Popoviciu’s inequality on variances
(
Popoviciu, 1935
)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}\big[(\overline{\rho}(t)-\rho(t))^{2}\big]\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{1}\var\big(\overline{\rho}(t)\big)\mathrm{d}t=\frac{1}{n_{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\var\big(E_{t}^{2}\big)\mathrm{d}t\leq\frac{C_{E}^{4}}{4n_{2}}.

(5.A.6)

Combining (
5.A.4
), (
5.A.5
), and (
5.A.6
) via Minkowski’s inequality we obtain (
5.4.24
).

∎

5.A.5
Proof of Theorem
5.4.3

We first note that

\frac{1}{\widehat{\rho}(s)}-\frac{1}{\rho(s)}=\frac{\rho(s)-\widehat{\rho}(s)}{\rho(s)\widehat{\rho}(s)}=\underbrace{\frac{\rho(s)-\widehat{\rho}(s)}{\rho(s)^{2}}}_{\eqcolon\zeta_{s}}+\underbrace{\frac{(\rho(s)-\widehat{\rho}(s))^{2}}{\widehat{\rho}(s)\rho(s)^{2}}}_{\eqcolon\xi_{s}}.

Now consider a decomposition of
B
similar to that of
\sqrt{n_{2}}\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}
,

\displaystyle B=\tilde{B}_{t}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{5}\tilde{R}^{(\ell)}

where

\displaystyle\tilde{B}_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\zeta_{s}E_{s}^{(i)}(\bm{\lambda}_{s}^{(i)}-\lambda_{s}^{(i)})\mathrm{d}s,

\displaystyle\tilde{R}_{t}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\zeta_{s}(\widehat{E}_{s}^{(i)}-E_{s}^{(i)})(\bm{\lambda}_{s}^{(i)}-\lambda_{s}^{(i)})\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\tilde{R}^{(2)}_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\zeta_{s}\widehat{E}_{s}^{(i)}\mathrm{d}\bm{M}_{s}^{(i)},

\displaystyle\tilde{R}^{(3)}_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\zeta_{s}\widehat{E}_{s}^{(i)}\left(\lambda_{s}^{(i)}-\widehat{\lambda}_{s}^{(i)}\right)\mathrm{d}s,

\displaystyle\tilde{R}_{t}^{(4)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\xi_{s}\widehat{E}_{s}^{(i)}\mathrm{d}\bm{M}_{s}^{(i)}

\displaystyle\tilde{R}_{t}^{(5)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\xi_{s}\widehat{E}_{s}^{(i)}(\bm{\lambda}_{s}^{(i)}-\widehat{\lambda}_{s}^{(i)})\mathrm{d}s

The decomposition may seem peculiar at first glance: some of the remainder processes could be decomposed further into terms that would be more manageable. However, crude bounds on these error terms will suffice since
\zeta
and
\xi
also decay at an order of
n_{2}^{-1/2}
.
Theorem
5.4.3
follows from combining Proposition
5.A.2
and Proposition
5.A.3
, stated below.
\Box

Proposition 5.A.2
.

Under Assumptions
5.4.1
,
5.4.2
, and
5.4.3
, it holds that

\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|\tilde{R}_{t}^{(\ell)}|\xrightarrow{P}0

(5.A.7)

for
\ell\in\{1,2,3,4,5\}
.

Proof.

Let
\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle_{2}
denote the inner product on
L^{2}([0,1]\times\Omega,\mathrm{d}t\otimes\mathbb{P})
.
The triangle inequality and Cauchy-Schwarz can be used to bound the expectation of the first remainder term as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|\tilde{R}_{t}^{(1)}|\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}\big\langle\,|\zeta|,\,|\widehat{E}^{(i)}-E^{(i)}|\cdot|\bm{\lambda}^{(i)}-\lambda^{(i)}|\big\rangle_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{n_{2}}\cdot\|\zeta\|_{2}\cdot\big\|(\widehat{E}-E)\cdot(\bm{\lambda}-\lambda)\big\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq c_{\rho}^{-2}C_{\bm{h}}\sqrt{n_{2}}\cdot\big\|\widehat{\rho}-\rho\big\|_{2}\cdot\big\|\widehat{E}-E\big\|_{2},

(5.A.8)

where we have used Assumption
5.4.1
for the last inequality.
By a similar argument we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|\tilde{R}_{t}^{(3)}|\Big]\leq c_{\rho}^{-2}C_{E}\sqrt{n_{2}}\cdot\big\|\widehat{\rho}-\rho\big\|_{2}\cdot\big\|\widehat{\lambda}-\lambda\big\|_{2}.

(5.A.9)

Now, combining Assumption
5.4.2
with Proposition
5.4.2
, we see that the right-hand sides of (
5.A.8
) and (
5.A.9
) vanish as
n\to\infty
. Hence we conclude that the desired convergence, (
5.A.7
), holds for
\ell=1
and
\ell=3
.

For the second and fourth remainder, let
\mathcal{D}_{1}\coloneq\sigma(\mathbf{W}_{i}\colon i\in\mathcal{I}_{1})
denote the information contained in the training data. Let further
\mathsf{G}_{t}=\mathcal{D}_{1}\vee\sigma(\mathcal{G}_{t}^{(i)}\colon i\in\mathcal{I}_{2})
denote the filtration containing the histories of the processes
(N^{(i)},X^{(i)},Z^{(i)})_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}

together with the information
\mathcal{D}_{1}
.
Then note that:

•
