since the observations are independent, the compensated
\mathcal{G}_{t}^{(i)}
-martingale
\bm{M}_{t}^{(i)}
is in fact also a
\mathsf{G}_{t}
-martingale
for each
i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}
.

•

each term in the empirical estimator
\tilde{\rho}
, defined in (
5.3.16
), is
\mathsf{G}_{t}
-predictable, and hence
\widehat{\rho}(t)=\max\{c_{\widehat{\rho}},\tilde{\rho}(t)\}
is
\mathsf{G}_{t}
-predictable.
As a consequence, both
\zeta_{t}
and
\xi_{t}
are also
\mathsf{G}_{t}
-predictable.

•

Assumption
5.4.1
implies that

|\zeta_{t}|\leq c_{\rho}^{-2}C_{E}^{2}
and that
|\xi_{t}|\leq c_{\widehat{\rho}}^{-1}c_{\rho}^{-2}C_{E}^{4}
.
Hence both
\int_{0}^{1}\zeta_{t}^{2}\bm{\lambda}_{t}\mathrm{d}t
and
\int_{0}^{1}\xi_{t}^{2}\bm{\lambda}_{t}\mathrm{d}t
have finite expectations.

We conclude that the terms in
\tilde{R}^{(2)}
are mean-zero
\mathsf{G}_{t}
-martingales, cf. Lemma A.1 in
Christgau et al. (2023b)
, and hence
\tilde{R}^{(2)}
is also a
\mathsf{G}_{t}
-martingale.
By the same argument,
\tilde{R}^{(4)}
is a
\mathsf{G}_{t}
-martingale.
Therefore we can apply Doob’s submartingale inequality, which yields that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|\tilde{R}_{t}^{(\ell)}|>\varepsilon\Big)\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}\big[(\tilde{R}_{1}^{(\ell)})^{2}\,\big]

(5.A.10)

for
\ell\in\{2,4\}
.
Now using the calculus of the quadratic characteristic for compensated counting processes
(see, for example, Proposition II.4.1 in
(
Andersen et al., 1993
)
) we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[(\tilde{R}_{1}^{(2)})^{2}\Big]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\langle\tilde{R}_{1}^{(2)}\rangle\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{1}\zeta_{t}^{2}\widehat{E}_{t}^{2}\bm{\lambda}_{t}\mathrm{d}t\Big]

\displaystyle=C_{\bm{h}}C_{E}^{2}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{(\rho(t)-\widehat{\rho}(t))^{2}}{\rho(t)^{4}}\mathrm{d}t\leq c_{\rho}^{-4}C_{\bm{h}}C_{E}^{2}\|\rho-\widehat{\rho}\|_{2}^{2}\to 0.

Similarly, observe that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[(\tilde{R}_{1}^{(4)})^{2}\big]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\langle\tilde{R}_{1}^{(4)}\rangle\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{1}\xi_{t}^{2}\widehat{E}_{t}^{2}\bm{\lambda}_{t}\mathrm{d}t\Big]

\displaystyle\leq C_{\bm{h}}C_{E}^{2}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{(\rho(t)-\widehat{\rho}(t))^{4}}{\widehat{\rho}(t)^{2}\rho(t)^{4}}\mathrm{d}t\leq c_{\widehat{\rho}}^{-2}c_{\rho}^{-4}C_{\bm{h}}C_{E}^{6}\|\rho-\widehat{\rho}\|_{2}^{2}\to 0,

where the convergence follows from Proposition
5.4.2
and the fact that
c_{\widehat{\rho}}^{-2}=o(n)
.
Combined with (
5.A.10
), we conclude that (
5.A.7
) holds for
\ell=2
and
\ell=4
.

For the final term, a direct bound yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|\tilde{R}_{t}^{(5)}|\Big]\leq C_{E}C_{\bm{h}}\sqrt{n_{2}}\|\xi\|_{1}\leq c_{\rho}^{-2}c_{\widehat{\rho}}^{-1}C_{E}C_{\bm{h}}\sqrt{n_{2}}\|\rho-\widehat{\rho}\|_{2}^{2}\to 0.

Thus we have established (
5.A.7
) for
\ell\in\{1,2,3,4,5\}
.
∎

Proposition 5.A.3
.

Under Assumptions
5.4.1
and
5.4.3
, it holds that

\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|\tilde{B}_{t}-V_{t}|\xrightarrow{P}0,

where
V=(V_{t})
is the process given by

\displaystyle V_{t}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{(\rho(s)-(E_{s}^{(i)})^{2})}{\rho(s)^{2}}\mathbb{E}[E_{s}(\bm{\lambda}_{s}-\lambda_{s})]\mathrm{d}s,\qquad t\in[0,1].

Proof.

The results follows from combining Lemmas
5.A.4
and
5.A.5
.
∎

Lemma 5.A.4
.

Under Assumptions
5.4.1
and
5.4.3
, it holds that
\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|\tilde{B}_{t}-\widehat{V}_{t}|\xrightarrow{P}0
, where
\widehat{V}=(\widehat{V}_{t})
is the process given by

\displaystyle\widehat{V}_{t}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{n_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\zeta_{s}\mathbb{E}[E_{s}(\bm{\lambda}_{s}-\lambda_{s})]\mathrm{d}s,\qquad t\in[0,1].

Proof.

Letting
\|\cdot\|_{[0,1]}
denote the norm on
L^{2}([0,1],\mathrm{d}t)
, an application of Cauchy-Schwarz yields that

\displaystyle\sup_{t}|\tilde{B}_{t}-\widehat{V}_{t}|

\displaystyle=\sqrt{n_{2}}\int_{0}^{1}|\zeta_{s}|\Big\lvert\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}E_{s}^{(i)}(\bm{\lambda}_{s}^{(i)}-\lambda_{s}^{(i)})-\mathbb{E}[E_{s}(\bm{\lambda}_{s}-\lambda_{s})]\Big\rvert\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\Big\|\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}E^{(i)}(\bm{\lambda}^{(i)}-\lambda^{(i)})-\mathbb{E}[E(\bm{\lambda}-\lambda)]\Big\|_{[0,1]}\cdot\sqrt{n_{2}}\|\zeta\|_{[0,1]}

(5.A.11)

Since

\mathbb{E}\|E(\bm{\lambda}-\lambda)\|_{[0,1]}\leq C_{E}C_{\bm{h}},

the law of large numbers – in the space
L^{2}([0,1])
–
gives that the first factor of (
5.A.11
) converges to zero almost surely, and in particular also in probability. See for example
Ledoux and Talagrand (1991, Cor. 7.10)
for a general formulation of the law of large numbers on separable Banach spaces.

For the second factor, we note that

\sqrt{n_{2}}\|\zeta\|_{[0,1]}\leq c_{\rho}^{-2}\sqrt{n_{2}}\|\widehat{\rho}-\rho\|_{[0,1]}.

The right-hand side is bounded in expectation by (
5.4.24
), and in particular it is bounded in probability.
Combined we conclude that the right-hand side of (
5.A.11
) converges to zero in probability.
∎

Lemma 5.A.5
.

Under Assumptions
5.4.1
and
5.4.3
, it holds that

\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|\widehat{V}_{t}-V_{t}|\Big]\xrightarrow{P}0

Proof.

Let
\tilde{V}=(\tilde{V}_{t})
be the process given by

\displaystyle\tilde{V}_{t}\coloneq\sqrt{n_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\varsigma_{s}\mathbb{E}[E_{s}(\bm{\lambda}_{s}-\lambda_{s})]\mathrm{d}s,\qquad\text{where}\quad\varsigma_{s}\coloneq\frac{\rho(s)-\tilde{\rho}(s)}{\rho(s)^{2}}.

In other words,
\varsigma_{s}
corresponds to
\zeta_{s}
, but where the clipped estimate,
\widehat{\rho}
, is replaced by the empirical estimate
\tilde{\rho}
from (
5.3.16
).
As such, we have equality of the events
(\varsigma_{s}\neq\zeta_{s})=(\tilde{\rho}(s)<c_{\widehat{\rho}})
.
Let
n
be large enough such that
c_{\widehat{\rho}}<c_{\rho}<\rho(s)
for all
s\in[0,1]
,
and then we observe that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|\widehat{V}_{t}-\tilde{V}_{t}|

\displaystyle\leq C_{E}C_{\bm{h}}\sqrt{n_{2}}\int_{0}^{1}|\zeta_{s}-\varsigma_{s}|\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq c_{\widehat{\rho}}c_{\rho}^{-2}C_{E}C_{\bm{h}}\sqrt{n_{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbbl{1}\left(\tilde{\rho}(s)<c_{\widehat{\rho}}\right)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq c_{\widehat{\rho}}c_{\rho}^{-2}C_{E}C_{\bm{h}}\sqrt{n_{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbbl{1}\left((\tilde{\rho}(s)-\rho(s))^{2}<(\rho(s)-c_{\widehat{\rho}})^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}s.

Applying Tonelli’s theorem and using Markov’s inequality to the above we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|\widehat{V}_{t}-\tilde{V}_{t}|\Big]

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{n_{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{P}\left((\tilde{\rho}(s)-\rho(s))^{2}<(\rho(s)-c_{\widehat{\rho}})^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{n_{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathbb{E}\big[(\tilde{\rho}(s)-\rho(s))^{2}\big]}{(\rho(s)-c_{\widehat{\rho}})^{2}}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq(c_{\rho}-c_{\widehat{\rho}})^{-2}\sqrt{n_{2}}\|\tilde{\rho}-\rho\|_{2}^{2}\to 0,

where ‘
\lesssim
’ is an inequality up to the constant
c_{\widehat{\rho}}c_{\rho}^{-2}C_{E}C_{\bm{h}}
, and where the last convergence follows from Proposition
5.4.2
.

It now suffices to show that
\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|\tilde{V}_{t}-V_{t}|\Big]\xrightarrow{P}0
. Using the decomposition
\tilde{\rho}=\overline{\rho}+r_{1}+r_{2}
from the proof of Proposition
5.4.2
, we first see that

\displaystyle V_{t}=\sqrt{n_{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\rho(s)-\overline{\rho}(s)}{\rho(s)^{2}}\mathbb{E}[E_{s}(\bm{\lambda}_{s}-\lambda_{s})]\mathrm{d}s,

so that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|\tilde{V}_{t}-V_{t}|\leq\frac{C_{E}C_{\bm{h}}}{c_{\rho}^{2}}\sqrt{n_{2}}\int_{0}^{1}|r_{1}(s)|+|r_{2}(s)|\mathrm{d}s.

Using the intermediate results (
5.A.4
) and (
5.A.5
) from the proof of Proposition
5.4.2
, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|\tilde{V}_{t}-V_{t}|\Big]=\frac{C_{E}C_{\bm{h}}}{c_{\rho}^{2}}\sqrt{n_{2}}(\|r_{1}\|_{1}+\|r_{2}\|_{1})\to 0.

∎

5.A.6
Proof of Theorem
5.4.4

We first establish asymptotic Gaussianity of the oracle process.

Theorem 5.A.6
.

Let
S=(S_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
be the process given by

S_{t}=U_{t}+V_{t}-\sqrt{n_{2}}\gamma_{t}
,
and let
\Gamma=(\Gamma_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
be the process from Theorem
5.4.4
.

Under Assumption
5.4.1
, it holds that

S\xrightarrow{d}\Gamma

with respect to the uniform topology as
n\to\infty
.

Proof.

For any
0\leq x<y\leq 1
, define the integrals

\displaystyle I_{xy}\coloneq\int_{x}^{y}G_{z}\mathrm{d}M_{z},\qquad\text{and}\qquad J_{xy}\coloneq\int_{x}^{y}E_{z}G_{z}\mathrm{d}\gamma_{z}.

The process
S_{t}
is a sum of i.i.d. processes with same distribution as
I_{0t}-J_{0t}
, and note that this process has mean zero: it holds that
\mathbb{E}[I_{0t}]=\gamma_{t}
by the definition of the LCM, and that
\mathbb{E}[J_{0t}]=\gamma_{t}
since
\mathbb{E}[E_{s}G_{s}]=1
.
Thus we can apply the central limit theorem in Skorokhod space derived in

van der Vaart and Wellner (2023, Example 1.14.24)
.
In view of this CLT, it suffices to show that
there exist continuous, strictly increasing functions
F_{1},F_{2}\colon[0,1]\to[0,\infty)
and constants
\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}>0
, such that for every
0\leq s<t<u\leq 1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(I_{st}+J_{st})^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq|F_{1}(t)-F_{1}(s)|^{1/2+\epsilon_{1}},

(5.A.12)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(I_{st}+J_{st})^{2}(I_{tu}+J_{tu})^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq|F_{2}(u)-F_{2}(s)|^{1+\epsilon_{2}}.

(5.A.13)

To this end, let
0\leq s<t<u\leq 1
be given.
In the following use
x\lesssim y
to denote that the inequality
x\leq K\cdot y
holds for a constant
K>0
independent of
(s,t,u)
. In fact, in each case the constant will be a polynomial over the constants
C_{\bm{h}},C_{E}
, and
c_{\rho}^{-1}
from Assumption
5.4.1
.

We first note that

J_{st}\lesssim t-s,\quad\text{and}\quad J_{tu}\lesssim u-t,

(5.A.14)

under Assumption
5.4.1
. Using the quadratic characteristic for compensated counting processes, we also have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[I_{st}^{2}]

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\int_{s}^{t}G_{r}\mathrm{d}\bm{M}_{r}\Big)^{2}\Big]+\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\int_{s}^{t}G_{r}(\bm{\lambda}_{r}-\lambda_{r})\mathrm{d}r\Big)^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{s}^{t}G_{r}^{2}\bm{\lambda}\mathrm{d}r\Big]+\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\int_{s}^{t}G_{r}(\bm{\lambda}_{r}-\lambda_{r})\mathrm{d}r\Big)^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\lesssim(t-s)+(t-s)^{2}\leq 2(t-s)

Thus we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(I_{st}+J_{st})^{2}]\lesssim\mathbb{E}[I_{st}^{2}]+\mathbb{E}[J_{st}^{2}]\lesssim t-s.

This shows that the condition (
5.A.12
) holds with
\epsilon_{1}=\frac{1}{2}
and
F_{1}(r)=C_{1}r
for a suitably large constant
C_{1}>0
.

For the second condition we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(I_{st}+J_{st})^{2}(I_{tu}+J_{tu})^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq 4(\mathbb{E}[I_{st}^{2}I_{tu}^{2}]+\mathbb{E}[I_{st}^{2}J_{tu}^{2}]+\mathbb{E}[J_{st}^{2}I_{tu}^{2}]+\mathbb{E}[J_{st}^{2}J_{tu}^{2}]).

By same argument as
\mathbb{E}[I_{st}^{2}]\lesssim t-s
, we also have that
\mathbb{E}[I_{tu}^{2}]\lesssim t-u
.
Combining this with the inequality (
5.A.14
) we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[I_{st}^{2}J_{tu}^{2}]+\mathbb{E}[J_{st}^{2}I_{tu}^{2}]+\mathbb{E}[J_{st}^{2}J_{tu}^{2}]\lesssim(t-s)(u-t)

For the term
\mathbb{E}[I_{st}^{2}I_{tu}^{2}]
, we note that if
T\in(s,t]
then
M
is constant on the interval
(t,u]
, in which case
I_{tu}=0
.
Therefore

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[I_{st}^{2}I_{tu}^{2}]=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{s}^{t}G_{r}\lambda_{r}\mathrm{d}r\right)^{2}I_{tu}^{2}\right]\lesssim(t-s)^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[I_{tu}^{2}\right]\lesssim(t-s)^{2}(u-t)

Combined we conclude that

\mathbb{E}[(I_{st}+J_{st})^{2}(I_{tu}+J_{tu})^{2}]\lesssim(t-s)(u-t)\leq(u-s)^{2}.

This shows that the second condition (
5.A.13
) is satisfied with
\epsilon=1
and
F_{2}(r)=C_{2}r
for a suitably large constant
C_{2}>0
.
∎

Returning to the proof of Theorem
5.4.4
, the limit distribution of the ACM estimator is now a simple consequence of the continuous mapping theorem.
14
14

14

Applied to the map
\mathbb{D}^{3}\ni(x,y,z)\mapsto x+y+z\in\mathbb{D}
, where
\mathbb{D}
is the space of càglàd functions on
[0,1]
endowed with the uniform norm.

Using the limits establised in Proposition
5.4.1
, Theorem
5.4.3
, and Theorem
5.A.6
, we conclude that

\sqrt{n_{2}}(\;\!\widehat{\gamma}-\gamma)=\underbrace{S}_{\xrightarrow{d}\Gamma}+\underbrace{(\sqrt{n_{2}}\cdot\widetilde{\gamma}-U)}_{\xrightarrow{p}0}+\underbrace{(B-V)}_{\xrightarrow{p}0}\>\xrightarrow{\>d\>}\>\Gamma

(5.A.15)

in the uniform topology as
n\to\infty
.

Turning to the cross-fitted ACM estimator, let
\widehat{\gamma}^{k}
denote ACM estimator fitted with
\mathcal{I}_{1}=[n]\setminus J_{k}
and
\mathcal{I}_{2}=J_{k}
for each
k\in[K]
. Let also

S_{t}^{k}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|J_{k}|}}\sum_{i\in J_{k}}s_{t}^{(i)},\qquad\text{where}\quad s_{t}^{(i)}\coloneq\int_{0}^{t}G_{s}^{(i)}\mathrm{d}M_{s}^{(i)}+\int_{0}^{t}E_{s}^{(i)}G_{s}^{(i)}\mathrm{d}\gamma_{s},

denote the corresponding oracle processes.

In the following computation, we use
x\sim y
as a shorthand for
\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|x_{t}-y_{t}|\xrightarrow{p}0
.
Using that folds are assumed to have sizes
|J_{k}|\in\{\lfloor{n/K}\rfloor,\lceil{n/K}\rfloor\}
,
Proposition
5.4.1
and Theorem
5.4.3
applied to each fold yields that

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}(\widecheck{\gamma}-\gamma)\sim\frac{\sqrt{n}}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{S^{k}}{\sqrt{|J_{k}|}}=\frac{\sqrt{n}}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{|J_{k}|}\sum_{i\in J_{k}}s^{(i)}\sim\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\in[n]}s^{(i)}

where last equivalence follows from Lemma
5.A.7
.
By the continuous mapping theorem, applied as in (
5.A.15
), it suffices to establish that
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i\in[n]}s^{(i)}\xrightarrow{d}\Gamma
.
However, this is simply the CLT established in Theorem
5.A.6
with
\mathcal{I}_{2}=[n]
.
\Box

The following lemma is a Banach space generalization of one of the standard steps for proving validity of cross-fitting, cf. Lemma S1 in
Lundborg and Pfister (2023)
.

Lemma 5.A.7
.

Let
W
be a random element in a Banach space
(\mathbb{D},\|\cdot\|)
, satisfying that
\mathbb{E}[\|W\|]<\infty
, and let
(W_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be i.i.d. copies of
W
.
For a fixed
K\in\mathbb{N}
, let
[n]=J_{1}\cup\cdots\cup J_{K}
be a disjoint partition of
[n]
with

|J_{k}|\in\{\lfloor{n/K}\rfloor,\lceil{n/K}\rfloor\}
for each
k\in[K]
.
Finally, let
S=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in[n]}W_{i}
and
S_{k}=\frac{1}{|J_{k}|}\sum_{i\in J_{k}}W_{i}
for each
k\in[K]
.

Then, for any
r<1
,

\displaystyle n^{r}\cdot\Big(\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}S_{k}-S\Big)\xrightarrow{L^{1}}0

as
n\to\infty
.

Proof.

Note that

\frac{1}{n+K}<\frac{1}{K|J_{k}|}<\frac{1}{n-K}

and hence

|\frac{1}{K|J_{k}|}-\frac{1}{n}|\leq\frac{1}{n-K}-\frac{1}{n}=\frac{K}{(n-K)n}
.
Since

\displaystyle\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}S_{k}-S=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i\in J_{k}}\left(\frac{1}{K|J_{k}|}-\frac{1}{n}\right)W_{i},

we conclude that

\displaystyle n^{r}\cdot\mathbb{E}\Big\|\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}S_{k}-S\Big\|\leq\frac{Kn^{r}}{n-K}\mathbb{E}\|W\|\longrightarrow 0

as
n\to\infty
.
∎

5.B. Multiplicative hazards models

The predictable projection,
\pi_{t}(\overline{Z}_{t})=\mathbb{E}[X_{t}\,|\,T\geq t,\overline{Z}_{t}]
, is generally
difficult to compute – even with baseline covariates
Z
and a binary baseline exposure
X
,
where
\pi_{t}(Z)=\mathbb{P}(X=1\,|\,T\geq t,Z)
. However, for a multiplicative hazards
model, and under a strengthened censoring assumption, we give a fairly explicit
representation of
\pi_{t}(Z)
in Proposition
5.B.1
below.
Example
5
elaborates further on the ACM in the Cox proportional hazards model.

Proposition 5.B.1

(Predictable projection for binary exposure)
.

Suppose that
X\in\{0,1\}
is a binary baseline exposure, that
Z
is a baseline covariate and that the
model is a nonparametric time-varying proportional hazards model:

\displaystyle\bm{h}_{t}(X,Z)=(X\theta_{t}+1)\varphi_{t}(Z).

If
C\,\perp\!\!\!\perp\,(T^{*},X)\,|\,Z
, then the predictable projection is given by

\displaystyle\pi_{t}(Z)=\frac{\pi_{0}(Z)}{\pi_{0}(Z)+(1-\pi_{0}(Z))e^{\mathsf{I}_{t}(Z)}},

where
\pi_{0}(Z)=\mathbb{P}(X=1\,|\,Z)
is the (baseline) propensity score and

\displaystyle\mathsf{I}_{t}(Z)\coloneq\int_{0}^{t}\theta_{s}\varphi_{s}(Z)\mathrm{d}s.

The predictable projection can be expressed as the logistic model:

\mathrm{logit}(\pi_{t}(Z))=\mathrm{logit}(\pi_{0}(Z))-\mathsf{I}_{t}(Z).

Proof.

Recall that
Y_{t}=\mathbbl{1}(T\geq t)=\mathbbl{1}(C\geq t)\mathbbl{1}(T^{*}\geq t)
. Using the
definition of
\pi_{t}(Z)
and the conditional independence assumption regarding censoring,
we find that

\displaystyle\pi_{t}(Z)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[X\,|\,T\geq t,Z]=\frac{\mathbb{E}[XY_{t}\,|\,Z]}{\mathbb{P}(T\geq t\,|\,Z)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}[X\mathbbl{1}(T^{*}\geq t)\,|\,Z]\mathbb{P}(C\geq t\mid Z)}{\mathbb{P}(T^{*}\geq t\,|\,Z)\mathbb{P}(C\geq t\,|\,Z)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}[X\mathbbl{1}(T^{*}\geq t)\,|\,Z]}{\mathbb{P}(T^{*}\geq t\,|\,Z)}.

Using the correspondence between survival time and cumulative hazard we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[X\mathbbl{1}(T^{*}\geq t)\,|\,Z]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[X\cdot\mathbb{P}(T^{*}\geq t\,|\,X,Z)\,|\,Z]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[X\exp\Big(-X\int_{0}^{t}\theta_{s}\varphi_{s}(Z)\mathrm{d}s-\int_{0}^{t}\varphi_{s}(Z)\mathrm{d}s\Big)\,|\,Z\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(X=1\,|\,Z)\exp\Big(-\int_{0}^{t}\theta_{s}\varphi_{s}(Z)\mathrm{d}s-\int_{0}^{t}\varphi_{s}(Z)\mathrm{d}s\Big)

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}(Z)e^{-\mathsf{I}_{t}(Z)-\mathsf{II}_{t}(Z)},

where
\mathsf{I}_{t}(z)\coloneq\int_{0}^{t}\theta_{s}\varphi_{s}(z)\mathrm{d}s

and
\mathsf{II}_{t}(z)\coloneq\int_{0}^{t}\varphi_{s}(z)\mathrm{d}s
.
Similarly the denominator is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(T^{*}\geq t\,|\,Z)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(-X\int_{0}^{t}\theta_{s}\varphi_{s}(Z)\mathrm{d}s-\int_{0}^{t}b_{s}\varphi_{s}(Z)\mathrm{d}s\right)\,|\,Z\right]

\displaystyle=((1-\pi_{0}(Z))+e^{-\mathsf{I}_{t}(Z)}\pi_{0}(Z))e^{-\mathsf{II}_{t}(Z)}.

From this we obtain that

\displaystyle\pi_{t}(Z)

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi_{0}(Z)e^{-\mathsf{I}_{t}(Z)}}{(1-\pi_{0}(Z))+e^{-\mathsf{I}_{t}(Z)}\pi_{0}(Z)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi_{0}(Z)}{\pi_{0}(Z)+(1-\pi_{0}(Z))e^{\mathsf{I}_{t}(Z)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+e^{-\mathrm{logit}(\pi_{0}(Z))}e^{\mathsf{I}_{t}(Z)}}=\mathrm{expit}(\mathrm{logit}(\pi_{0}(Z))-\mathsf{I}_{t}(Z)).

∎

Example 5

(ACM in a Cox proportional hazards model)
.

With baseline covariates
Z\in\real^{d}
, in addition to a binary exposure
X\in\{0,1\}
, the Cox
proportional hazards model asserts that:

\displaystyle\bm{h}_{t}(X,Z)=h^{0}(t)\exp(\beta_{X}X+\beta_{Z}^{\top}Z)=(X(e^{\beta_{X}}-1)+1)h^{0}(t)\exp(\beta_{Z}^{\top}Z).

(5.B.1)

Letting
\phi(Z)=(e^{\beta_{X}}-1)e^{\beta_{Z}^{\top}Z}
we see that this model is a special case of Example
1
with

\theta_{t}(Z)=h^{0}(t)\phi(Z)
. It follows by (
5.1.4
)
that the ACM can be expressed as

\displaystyle\gamma_{t}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\left[h^{0}(s)\phi(Z)w_{s}^{\textsc{ac}}(Z)\right]\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\phi(Z)\underbrace{\int_{0}^{t}h^{0}(s)w_{s}^{\textsc{ac}}(Z)\mathrm{d}s}_{\eqcolon W_{t}^{\textsc{ac}}(Z)}\Big]=\mathbb{E}\left[\phi(Z)W_{t}^{\textsc{ac}}(Z)\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\beta_{Z}^{\top}Z}W_{t}^{\textsc{ac}}(Z)\right](e^{\beta_{X}}-1).

Thus
\gamma_{t}=c(t)(e^{\beta_{X}}-1)
is proportional to
e^{\beta_{X}}-1
with a
t
-dependent proportionality constant
c(t)=\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\beta_{Z}^{\top}Z}W_{t}^{\textsc{ac}}(Z)\right]\geq 0
.

Using the innovation theorem we see that

\displaystyle h_{t}(Z)

\displaystyle=\big(\pi_{t}(Z)(e^{\beta_{X}}-1)+1\big)h^{0}(t)\exp(\beta_{Z}^{\top}Z),

and we could also arrive at the representation of the ACM directly from the identity (
5.2.13
).
Indeed,
\bm{h}_{s}(X,Z)-h_{s}(Z)=(e^{\beta_{X}}-1)(X-\pi_{s}(Z))h^{0}(s)\exp(\beta_{Z}^{\top}Z)
, and
(
5.2.13
) gives

\displaystyle\gamma_{t}

\displaystyle=(e^{\beta_{X}}-1)\int_{0}^{t}h^{0}(s)\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\beta_{Z}^{\top}Z}\frac{Y_{s}(X-\pi_{s}(Z))^{2}}{\rho(s)}\right]\mathrm{d}s

The proportionality constant must, of course, be
c(t)
, but this can also be verified directly using
the same argument as in Example (
4
). We may note that

h_{t}(\cdot)
is not generally of the form of a Cox proportional hazards model, which illustrates
the fact that the Cox model is not closed under marginalization.

To compute the proportionality constant we need the weights

w_{t}^{\textsc{ac}}(Z)=\frac{Y_{t}\pi_{t}(Z)(1-\pi_{t}(Z))}{\mathbb{E}[Y_{t}\pi_{t}(Z)(1-\pi_{t}(Z))]},

and to this end we need to compute the predictable projection
\pi_{t}(Z)
.
Under the censoring assumption
C\,\perp\!\!\!\perp\,(T^{*},X)\,|\,Z
, it follows
from Proposition
5.B.1
that

\displaystyle\mathrm{logit}(\pi_{t}(Z))=\mathrm{logit}(\pi_{0}(Z))-\phi(Z)H^{0}(t),

(5.B.2)

where
H^{0}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}h^{0}(s)\mathrm{d}s
.
This shows that
\pi_{t}(Z)
evolves in time with a logistic growth rate precomposed with
H^{0}
.
Formula (
5.B.2
) makes it straightforward to compute
\pi_{t}(z)(1-\pi_{t}(z))

for any given
t
and
z
(provided that we can compute
\pi_{0}(z)
), but it does not
appear to yield a simple analytic expression of neither the weights
w_{t}^{\textsc{ac}}(Z)

nor the proportionality constant
c(t)
. It is, however, useful for Monte Carlo computation
of the ACM.

34
32
31
33
15
